Level X

33 books · 10 pages each

1. A Brave Call for School

1Can you picture a country that will not let some children go to school? A child there may hold one hope above the rest. That hope is an education. The hope is denied. Malala was a brave girl. This world is where she grew up. A picture shows her at her studies. She is in Pakistan. She is not famous yet. Six parts make up this book. An introduction opens it. Malala's world comes next. Malala's work comes after that. The attack comes next. A global voice comes after the attack. A glossary closes the book. Her name belongs to a story from long ago. A woman in Afghanistan carried that name first. She was a courageous poet. She was a warrior. British soldiers were invading. She led the fighters of Afghanistan. The battle was in 1880. The fighters won a victory. A picture shows Malala beside her family. Her parents stand with her. Her brothers stand with her. Khushal is on the left. Atal is there too. Her name is Malala Yousafzai. Her last name sounds like YOO-suf-zy. July 12 is her birthday. The year was 1997. Her birth city was Mingora. Mingora sits in the Swat Valley. The valley is in Pakistan. Two younger brothers are in her family. Ziauddin is her father. His name sounds like zee-OW-deen. He is a champion for human rights. Human rights are basic rights. All people should have them. He wanted every child in Pakistan to enter school. That work began in 1994. He helped many schools open. They stand in the Swat District of Pakistan.

2Over one thousand students fill them. Malala attended one for many years. Her father founded that one. He liked her questions from her youngest years. He wanted her to think for herself. Speaking up was a skill he passed on. Human rights was one cause for it. Equality was another cause. Education was another cause. Tor Pekai is her mother. She works as a homemaker. Muslim traditions guide her. She keeps a private life. She stays away from public view. Malala admires her mother. The child will walk a different path. Northern Pakistan contains the Swat District. The mountains are high. The valleys are lush. The valleys are also green. Many people call the region beautiful. Tourists used to arrive. Few arrive now. The Taliban is the cause. The Taliban is a political group. One base is Pakistan. Another base is Afghanistan. They follow an extreme form of Islam. That form is fundamentalist. Most Muslims follow a form far from this one. Women face hard limits on their rights. Strict religious rules bind most people on land they hold. Music is not allowed. Movies are not allowed. Television is not allowed. Outside work is not allowed for women. School is not allowed for girls. Makeup is not allowed. Bright clothing is not allowed. Loud laughter is not allowed. After a certain age, a girl must take a male relative outdoors. Violence makes people follow the laws. Terrorism is part of that violence. Faith runs deep in Malala's family. Their way does not fit the Taliban way.

3People ask about her religion. She answers at once. Islam does not stand against school for girls, she says. Each child has a right to learn, in her view. Each child has a duty to learn. Each child has that responsibility. The year 2007 opened a fight for control. The Swat Valley was the prize. The Taliban drove the fight. Pakistan's government could not end it. The army could not end it. Police officers were targeted. Blasts took religious buildings. Blasts took government buildings. Girls' schools in the area came down. Losses were counted near the close of 2008. Swat saw roughly 400 schools destroyed. Blasts were the cause. The full loss was over 1,500 schools. A ban came next from the Taliban. The first day was January 15, 2009. Girls lost the chance to attend school. Taliban land was the only place of the ban. A picture catches local people near rubble. A girls' high school had stood on that spot. The spot was in the Swat Valley. Destruction came from the Taliban. It came in the year 2009. Her father kept every school of his open then. Danger did not change him. Other parents acted from fear of the Taliban. They withdrew classmates. The share that left was over half. She put away her school uniform. It could have marked her out. There is more to education than reading. There is more to it than writing. Tools come from it. The tools let people care for themselves. The tools let people care for their families. The tools let people share in community life.

4Hold these questions. How can education change the job a person may hold? How can a family gain food through education? How can a family gain medical care through it? How can children receive their own learning chances? How can other goods come within reach? How can other services come within reach? How can education join the work against child labor? How can education leave a community in better health? How can study of certain issues affect a vote? How can education help peace grow in a community? How can education help peace grow in a country? How can science lessons change views of the environment? What more can education change in daily life? Her father pressed students to speak. A school right was fading. He wanted words raised against that loss. In 2008 Malala gave an interview. Several friends joined her. A Pakistan television show ran it. She next volunteered for a talk show. Worry was in her. She still let her views out. She knew she must. On the show she faced the Taliban with a question. She questioned their daring. They were taking a basic right. Education was that right. Her courage won respect. Four pieces form her picture of change. A child is one piece. A teacher is one piece. A pen is one piece. A book is one piece. Set together, they can change the world. She adds one more line. Learning is the one fix, she says. She began a blog in 2009. It lived on the Internet.

5She was eleven. The British Broadcasting Corporation carried it. The short name is BBC. Some posts shared dreams for her life. Some posts shared daily life under Taliban rule. A pseudonym hid her. A pseudonym is a fake name. The name blocked readers from knowing her. It lowered the chance the writing made her a target. The blog later appeared in English. Far-off readers opened it. Shock followed. Swat's hardships were part of the shock. Girls kept from school were part of it. The Taliban stood behind that act. She had wanted her name hidden. December 2009 made her known. She went on giving talks. To her, education is a right of every person. Her story traveled. Reporters interviewed her often. Television showed some interviews. Print showed others. A film of her life came out in 2010. Its maker was the New York Times. Desmond Tutu nominated her in 2011. He is a widely known activist. He is a religious leader from South Africa. The nomination named the International Children's Peace Prize. Another prize came before 2011 ended. Pakistan's prime minister awarded it. The name was Pakistan's National Youth Peace Prize. Inside her country her voice stood for hope. Outside it her voice stood for hope. It stood for empowerment as well. Wide fame shows in a picture. Her cause was girls' rights. Courage shaped her stand. The world noticed. More talks meant more danger. She got in the way of Taliban goals. Her father got in the way too. Both were seen as threats. The warning came at age fourteen.

6The Taliban wanted her dead. She feared for her father's safety. His activist work opposed the Taliban. She did not feel in danger herself at first. Her family felt the same. Youth, they thought, would protect her. A picture shows Malala with terrible wounds. The year of the harm was 2012. The Taliban did the harm. Healing followed in a hospital. England was the country of that hospital. She watched over safety. She watched for her family. She watched for herself. Doors stayed locked because of her. She would not take the path home on foot. School was where that path began. The bus was her ride. October 9, 2012, brought gunfire during the trip home. She was riding a bus. Several friends shared the ride. Classes had ended. A man came aboard. He wanted Malala pointed out. Words did not answer him. Eyes of her friends settled on her. Those eyes named her. He showed a gun. Three shots left the gun. Malala caught one shot. She was injured in a very bad way. Two girls beside her caught shots too. Their injuries stayed small. Her life was nearly lost. A plane carried her to care. Care began at a military hospital. The cities were a few hours apart. The care kept her alive. A second hospital received her later. Birmingham was the city. The city is in England. Many hands worked for her care. They sought the best care. She was fortunate. Most wounds closed up. A few challenges remain hers. She will meet them in all the years left.

7She teaches with a story about pens. Someone can take every pen away. The empty desk shows why education counts. The Taliban announced ownership of the attack. A warning went to other girls. A girl may be hurt for speaking. They wanted that price understood. Cards came while she lay healing. Gifts came with them. Flowers came with them. The senders numbered in the thousands. World leaders were included. Movie stars were included. People gathered with candles. The gatherings circled the world. Support was their purpose. She was well along when classes started again. March 2013 was that month. Her new school was in Birmingham. Her family did not depart. The United Kingdom became their home. High school ended for her in 2017. She graduated. Joy stayed away. She missed the nearness of the Swat Valley. She missed the nearness of her friends. Pakistan saw her in 2018. That was her first return. The long gap began with the attack. A Karachi picture shows support. Pakistan is the country. Students came. Parents came. Teachers came. They came to support Malala. One month had passed since the attack. A time line lists the years. She was born in 1997. Mingora was the city. Pakistan was the country. Her first public appearance was in 2008. A talk show was the place. Peshawar was the city. Her first blog post was in 2009. The BBC published that post. The same year, schools for girls were ordered shut. The order covered all of them. The Taliban gave the order.

8A nomination came in 2011. The prize was the International Children's Peace Prize. A win came in 2011 too. She received Pakistan's National Youth Peace Prize. The prize was renamed later. The new name was hers. She was shot in 2012. The Taliban fired the shot. She was runner-up in 2012 too. TIME Person of the Year was the title. The Nobel Peace Prize came in 2014. She was the youngest winner. Another picture shows her introduction. The place is United Nations headquarters. The city is New York City. The day is July 12, 2013. Someone introduces her. Then she gives a speech. It is her first speech since the attack. A side photo shows her book. Her United Nations speech was in July 2013. The attack was still close. Less than a year had passed. She asked that school be open to children. She meant children everywhere. An award came later that year. It was the top human rights award in Europe. Dedication was the cause. Freedom of thought was one part. Human rights was the other part. She has met President Obama. She has met Queen Elizabeth II. She has met other world figures. A request went out in 2012 for girls' education. Ban Ki-moon made it. He was Secretary-General of the United Nations. He asked governments to help. He asked organizations to help. He asked businesses to help. He asked religious leaders to help. He asked other people to help. He asked groups around the globe to help. A fund started in 2012 as well.

9Pakistan helped build it. The United Nations helped build it. The fund carries Malala's name. The fund serves girls' education. President Zardari gave the first money. He is Pakistan's president. The sum was $10 million. October 2014 brought word of a winner. The Nobel Peace Prize went to Malala. Her age was seventeen. No younger person had won it before. The prize changed her feelings. She felt more powerful. She felt courageous too. She gave the prize to children who have no voice. Voiceless was the name she used. Her message went to children everywhere. She told them to stand up. She told them to guard their rights. The Taliban wanted silence from her. They got the reverse. Her voice reached farther. Her voice grew louder. She still supports education. She supports it for every child. She will not let fear stop her. She has a goal for the years ahead. She wants to be prime minister. She wants that role in Pakistan. Her father received his own post. The title was Special Advisor on Global Education. He holds it at the United Nations. The two of them still work together. A vision guides both. One part is a peaceful world. One part is education for every child. A picture shows a meeting at the White House. The date is October 11, 2013. Malala is in a meeting. The U.S. president is with her. His name is Barack Obama. The First Lady is with her. Her name is Michelle Obama. Their daughter is with her. Her name is Malia Obama.

10Meanings for hard words follow. An activist works for change in society. An activist may work for change in politics. Muslim means of the people who follow Islam. It can also mean linked to their faith. Nominated means a person was named for a job. It can mean named for an award. It can mean named for a position. Blogging means writing placed on a website. The writing may be thoughts. It may be opinions. It may be experiences. Other people are expected to read it. A prime minister leads a government. Most countries with a parliament use this title. Dedication means commitment to a cause. It can mean commitment to an activity. Hard work often belongs to it. Loyalty often belongs to it. The Taliban is a strict Islamic group. Fundamentalist describes the group. Afghanistan is one base for it. Pakistan is one base for it. Empowerment means helping someone gain power. The power is over that person's situation. Terrorism means violence used to seek change. A threat of violence is terrorism too. The change may be of a government. The change may be of a system. A fundamentalist keeps to beliefs in a strict way. The beliefs form a set. Religion is often behind the set. The United Nations is an international organization. Many countries are in it. It works in support of peace. Human rights are rights all people have. Most societies say people have them from the start. Justice is among the rights. Freedom is among the rights. Equality is among the rights.

2. A Water Path Between Two Oceans

1Ships moving through the Gatun locks appear in a photo of the canal's eastern side. The canal was a modern marvel. Most people thought it would never be built. Yet thousands cheered on August 15, 1914. That day the cargo ship Ancon steamed from the Atlantic Ocean to the Pacific. No ship had taken that route until this newly finished canal opened. For many people, the voyage of the Ancon was a day of triumph. Ten years of labor went into cutting earth out of the isthmus. They had beaten back countless obstacles. American technology had made it possible to build the canal. The canal is fifty miles long. It was a triumph of human ingenuity. It was a colossal feat of engineering. The world had never seen a feat like it. The canal spurred commerce around the globe. It helped make the United States a world power. Almost a century on, thousands of ships still make the crossing. The ships range from yachts to aircraft carriers. They still pass from one mighty ocean to the other. A picture shows the Ancon making the first trip through the new canal in 1914. The canal is a remarkable shortcut. A map shows San Francisco, New York, and the United States. It shows the Atlantic Ocean, the Caribbean Sea, and the Panama Canal. It shows the equator, South America, and the Pacific Ocean.

2A trip through the Panama Canal covers 5,300 miles. That is 8,370 kilometers. A trip around South America covers 13,000 miles. That is 20,900 kilometers. The idea of linking the Atlantic and the Pacific with a canal was not new. Early Spanish explorers were the first to think of a waterway between the two oceans. In the 1700s, Spain lacked the engineering skill to take on such a venture. Later, in the early 1800s, the British government developed plans for a canal. The French government developed plans too. Not until 1848 did the idea of a canal across the isthmus take a firm hold in the public mind. A ranch hand that year found gold lying in a California creek bed. The find set off a major gold rush. California drew thousands of hurrying people from many countries. They hoped to strike it rich. The East Coast sent a large share of them. They usually made the hazardous journey by sea. At the time, no trains could carry people across the country. No stagecoaches could do it either. In 1848, only five states of the United States lay west of the Mississippi River. People who did go overland found the trek dangerous. They crossed mountains, plains, and deserts. The trip was long. By comparison, a boat trip from New York to San Francisco was much safer. It still took months.

3People traveling by ship had two choices. One choice was a sail to Panama, a walk across the isthmus, and a second ship onward to California. The other choice was a voyage around Cape Horn, at South America's southern tip. Heavy winds made that route hazardous. Rough seas made it hazardous too. Around the middle of the 1800s, some Americans believed a Panama railroad would ease the trip a bit. Construction began in 1850. The line followed an old Spanish trail once used by mules. The work was difficult. It rained most of the year. The soil was always wet. The deadly heat often made workers faint. Insects swarmed everywhere. They spread deadly diseases. The railroad still reached completion in 1855. The success of the railroad made many governments look again at a canal. Businessmen looked again too. A canal, they thought, would earn still more than the railroad had. A map shows the Caribbean Sea, Panama City, and the Panama Canal. It shows Panama, the Gulf of Panama, and Costa Rica. It shows the Pacific Ocean and Colombia. Panama is a curved ribbon of land. It connects Central America and South America. No narrower land stands between the Atlantic Ocean and the Pacific Ocean than Panama. The land is hot. It is humid. It is often rainy. Panama is a diverse land. It has thick jungles. It has many types of plants and animals.

4French businessmen set up a canal-digging company in 1879. Panama was not yet its own country. It was still part of Colombia. Work could not start until France and Colombia signed a treaty. Ferdinand de Lesseps headed the project. He had led the building of another great canal, the Suez Canal in Egypt. Philippe Bunau-Varilla, also French, helped him shift huge crews of workers onto the isthmus. They moved heavy machinery there too. De Lesseps was a great manager of projects. He ignored warnings from people who said the jungle would doom the work. Digging started slowly. Workers carved out tiny slices of the jungle. Then the rains came. Day after day, it rained. Heat in the jungle was stifling, which made the work even harder. Insects took a toll on the workforce. Snakes, swamps, accidents, and disease took a toll too. Twenty thousand people died. After seven years, the project crumbled. It crumbled like the mudslides that killed so many. De Lesseps left Panama as a failure. The investment was more than 287 million dollars. That would be over 7 billion dollars today. Very little progress had been made. A timeline of the canal marks several dates. A treaty dated November 18, 1903, let the United States start canal work with the new Republic of Panama. On June 28, 1904, the first American workers arrived in Panama.

5On November 12, 1904, the first steam shovel arrived. On November 21, 1904, workers recorded the first case of yellow fever. On July 26, 1905, John Stevens arrived to take charge of the project. On August 15, 1914, the canal officially opened. On January 1, 2000, Panama took control of the canal. During the ten years after France failed, canal building in Panama sat untouched. To many observers, the region was only a grim swamp full of death and disease. The United States saw it differently. The United States was growing into a world power. A Central American canal offered the United States large gains. Theodore Roosevelt entered office in 1901 believing America's future ran through a Central American canal. Through that channel, he pictured American businesses reaching new buyers in Asia. American warships, he thought, could use the canal like a highway. Nicaragua, farther north, was the first site the Americans preferred. Bunau-Varilla had worked with de Lesseps. He convinced officials to dig in Panama instead. There was only one problem. Panama was still part of Colombia. A treaty with Colombia was signed so the digging could proceed. The congress of Colombia rejected the treaty. They believed it was unfair to their country. Roosevelt would not accept a refusal. He helped revolutionaries in Panama overthrow the government. Rebels started taking control on November 3, 1903.

6By sundown the revolt had ended. Three days later, the United States formally recognized the independence of Panama. A photo shows U.S. President Theodore Roosevelt testing a steam shovel during canal construction in 1906. The new country quickly filled with thousands of arriving workers. They brought steam shovels as big as buildings. They began digging a canal 50 miles long, which is 80.5 kilometers. The line ran from Colon to Panama City, on the Pacific coast. Malaria was a constant problem in Panama. Yellow fever was a constant problem too. Mosquito-borne disease had already sent more than 85 percent of the canal workers for treatment by 1906. A case of yellow fever often sent the workers into panic. Roosevelt called on William Gorgas, a doctor in the army, to battle the mosquitoes. Mosquitoes lay their eggs in water. Gorgas answered by sending Panamanian teams out to walk the streets. They looked for barrels full of standing water. They dumped the water out. Fumigators by the hundreds sprayed insecticide through homes. Gorgas drained swamps. Night nets over beds were required, so bites would be less likely. A photo shows Dr. Gorgas inspecting a construction area as part of his fight against deadly diseases spread by mosquitoes. His ideas worked. Yellow fever cases were down by nearly half in August 1906, leaving twenty-seven. November brought the case count down to a single person.

7That person was the last yellow fever victim Gorgas would see. A 1913 picture shows steam machines carving through Panama's central mountains. The world watched as construction began in 1904. John Wallace, an engineer, led the work. Roosevelt told Wallace that the dirt must fly. That order was hard to carry out. The dirt dug by the steam shovels was not dry. It was sticky mud. The mud slid back down into trenches that had just been dug. Most of the American workforce had quit Panama by June 1905. The task had overwhelmed them. The project was in peril. Wallace quit. John Stevens, an engineer who pushed hard, took his place. Stevens came to Panama while the morale of the workers sagged. About seventeen thousand men stood idle, waiting for orders. Stevens took his time before he pushed the digging forward. He knew the mightiest steam shovels were useless if the dirt they dug was not hauled away at once. He solved the problem by building a railroad. He built roads too. The railroad and the roads hauled the mud away. Stevens even built churches. He built hospitals, mess halls, and schools. He did this to improve the morale of the workers. He also changed the construction plans. Early plans had the diggers cut the canal down at the level of the sea. The mountains would have to be cut through.

8That digging would add years to the project. Another barrier was a river running high beside the mountains. To solve these problems, Stevens proposed a series of locks over the mountains. Each lock would be more than three football fields long. His plan also dammed that river so a huge lake would form. Ships, he said, could climb by lock to the mountain top and then reach the new lake. A second set of locks would bring ships down the range to the ocean waiting below. Crews were told to dig inward from each end until the two cuts met. These huge locks took four years to build. Each lock was 70 feet deep, which is 21.3 meters. Each lock was 110 feet wide, which is 33.5 meters. Here is how the Panama Canal works. A diagram shows lock gates, sea level, and the height of Gatun Lake. It shows the Caribbean Sea and the Pacific Ocean. It shows Miraflores Lake and Gatun Lake. It shows the Gatun locks, the Miraflores locks, and the Pedro Miguel locks. The side view of the lock system is seen from the north, and it is not drawn to scale. A lock is a watertight chamber. Huge steel doors stand at each end. Inside a lock, engineers can raise the water level. They can lower it too. When a ship wants to pass, the front door opens.

9The ship sails into the chamber. Once the ship is inside, the chamber door closes. Engineers then open valves. Water enters the chamber from the lock that sits higher than the ship. As a result, the water level rises. The ship rises to the level of the next lock. Three locks lift the ship, and then the inland lake is in front of it. The far end runs the same steps backward. Engineers let water out of a lock rather than pouring more in. The ship then drops until it matches the next lock. In the first step, a ship moves into an open lock. Next, the gates shut, water fills the lock, and the ship climbs to match the lock ahead. Further locks keep lifting the ship up to the height of Gatun Lake. After that, the ship crosses Gatun Lake and meets locks on the far shore, which run the steps in reverse. The 1914 finish brought locks that performed beyond what anyone had hoped. American control lasted through the close of 1999. Panama then received the canal under a treaty. Early in the twenty-first century, the canal looked old and worn. It badly needed a makeover. It needed to handle larger ships. It needed to resolve traffic jams. Panama's voters said yes in 2006 to two fresh sets of locks.

10They also voted to build two new navigation lanes. Those lanes would connect the new locks to the existing channels. In 2016, forty thousand workers finished the construction project. The project cost 5.4 billion dollars. It took ten years. The new locks let much larger ships pass through the canal. In effect, they double the amount of cargo the canal can handle each day. That enlargement stands as one more engineering wonder in the canal's long story. A canal is a dug water path that carries boats across land. Locks are gated sections in a river or a canal where the water level is raised and lowered so ships and boats can pass. A channel is a broad water path joining two bigger waters. Malaria is a dangerous tropical disease spread by mosquitoes. Commerce is business or trade. Morale is the confidence or enthusiasm of a person or a group working toward a goal. Engineering is the work of designing and building things with science and math. Ingenuity is the clever skill of solving a hard problem. A treaty is a formal peace or friendship deal between two nations or groups. An investment is money spent in the hope of profit. A trek is a long and challenging journey, especially one made on foot. An isthmus is a narrow strip of land that connects two larger landmasses.

3. Autism Looks Different Every Time

1This book opens by meeting Noah. It then asks what autism is. It describes characteristics of autism. It shows ways to support friends who are autistic. It looks at how wider understanding can open more opportunities. A glossary ends the book. Noah Mundt enjoys taking pictures. A tablet is what he uses for those pictures. He enjoys watching videos. Swimming is a favorite activity. Hiking is another. Video games fill some of his time. Music does too. Korean pop is among his favorite tunes. Disco is on that list. Rock and roll is on it. Polka is on it too. A show about a big red dog has been a favorite since Noah was small. Daina Mundt is his mom. She thinks Noah feels a bond with that big red dog. Mrs. Mundt teaches language arts at a middle school. The school is in Wisconsin. Noah is eighteen. He is a big guy. His height is 6 feet and 6 inches, equal to 2 m. The scale reads 275 pounds for him, equal to 124.7 kg. His mom says laughter comes easily to him. A hug is something he likes. Being touched is something he likes. Being tickled is something he likes. In many ways Noah resembles other teens. Important differences set him apart. Looking people in the eye is uncomfortable for him. Words spoken to him make sense. He rarely begins a conversation. If he does speak, he may request help with the Internet. He may also repeat lines from movies he loves. His hands sometimes flap.

2His head rocks forward and back. The motion can look a little like dancing. Nervous feelings may lead him to cover his ears. A new place may do the same. At those times he may talk quietly to himself. Noah is autistic. Autism is a difference in the brain. Communication changes because of it. Actions change because of it. Learning changes because of it. Specialists like to say that one meeting is not a pattern. You have learned only about that one autistic person. Someone else with autism will not match them. Each life with autism is its own experience. Speech comes easily to some autistic people. Writing comes easily to some. Others are nonverbal. School support can be light for some autistic students. Other students need much more help. The characteristics cover a wide span. The behaviors do too. Autism shows many faces. Scientists still cannot name one cause. Studies point to genetics as a possible major piece. Parents pass genes to their children. Those genes act like plans for building a person. The body follows the plans as it grows. The brain follows them too. Further studies look past genes. They ask whether environmental factors also play a part. Childhood illness is one possible contributor that some researchers question. Chemicals around a child are another. The causes are still unsettled. What is clear is that an autistic brain takes shape in its own way. A neurotypical brain takes a different shape. Neurotypical describes someone with no developmental condition. The word neuro points to the nervous system.

3Signals travel on that system between the brain and the rest of the body. Some experts picture the difference as wiring. Cells inside an autistic brain send messages in unusual ways. Surroundings therefore feel different to an autistic person. A neurotypical person does not get the same impression from those surroundings. Classroom noise may leave a student without autism untroubled. That same noise may feel unbearably loud to an autistic student. A girl steps into an airplane cockpit during a special visit. The program behind the visit helps autistic children relax about travel. Wide use of the term autism began in the 1940s. Earlier doctors sometimes wrote down a brain injury. Sometimes they wrote down a developmental disorder. The real picture was larger than those labels. The early use of autism pointed to social struggle. It also pointed to interests that were very intense. Later the meaning stretched. Other mental differences were included. Emotional differences were included too. Extra help at school is necessary for some students with autism. Fear shaped the public view for a long time. Families were often separated from autistic children. The children were sent to special institutions. Knowledge has grown among parents. It has grown among doctors. It has grown among educators. Home is now where more autistic children stay. Regular schools are where more of them go. A vaccine is a medicine that blocks serious disease. Some parents worry that vaccines lead to autism. Medical studies in many countries have tested that worry.

4No tie was found between a vaccination and autism. A long study counted children at age eight who had an autism identification. The span of years is 2004 through 2016. America's CDC ran the count. The letters stand for Centers for Disease Control. Prevention belongs to that agency's job too. Each row gives an average out of 1,000 children. Each row also gives about how many children match one case. For 2004 the average was 8.0 out of 1,000. The share was about 1 in 125. For 2006 the average was 9.0 out of 1,000. The share was about 1 in 110. For 2008 the average was 11.3 out of 1,000. The share was about 1 in 88. For 2010 the average was 14.7 out of 1,000. The share was about 1 in 68. For 2012 the average was 14.6 out of 1,000. The share was about 1 in 68. For 2014 the average was 16.9 out of 1,000. The share was about 1 in 59. For 2016 the average was 18.5 out of 1,000. The share was about 1 in 54. About 1.5 million people in America are autistic today. Recent years have brought many more diagnoses. The jump is large. Researchers do not know which explanation fits. One idea is that more autistic babies are being born. Another idea is that doctors now recognize autism more accurately. Boys are identified four times as often as girls. The reason is still a mystery. Strong debate often follows autism and the way it is treated.

5Some advocacy groups put their energy into prevention. They also seek a cure, as though autism were a disease. Other groups refuse that frame. They say this is not a sickness to be treated that way. Meeting daily needs should come first. They would rather not try to fix autistic people. A fuller life is the goal they name. A happier life is part of that goal. Feelings on each side run high. Opinions are firm. Daily life can turn complicated for an autistic person. A step that looks easy to someone else can feel confusing. It can feel challenging too. Aubrey plays in the backyard with her sister. The sister's name is Sophie. In the photo, Aubrey is on the right. She is nine years old. Her list of loves and likes is long. Making art is something she loves. Coloring books are something she likes. Apples are a food she likes. Pears are too. Pizza is too. Cheese is too. Apple juice is too. Video games are among the things she likes. She enjoys more than one kind. Board games appeal to her. Toys appeal to her. Time with Sophie appeals to her. Time with Aubrey Dog appeals to her. Aubrey Dog is a stuffed toy. It travels everywhere she goes. Adding numbers is a strength, she says. Making friends is a joy. She is only starting to sort out what autism means in her life. Loud sound really bothers her. A fire drill is one sound she names.

6A blender in the kitchen is another. Stopping a favorite activity upsets her deeply. Math leaves her feeling sure of herself. Reading is where she struggles. Inventing stories is fun for her. Telling those stories out loud is fun too. Listeners sometimes cannot follow what she means. She says autism makes a few tasks a little hard. Reading is one of them. Paying attention is one of them. Listening is one of them. She puts real effort into those tasks. Kind treatment matters to her. Unkind treatment hurts her feelings. She tries to be nice in return. She wants people to remember that she is a regular kid. Drawing is another love. Aubrey Dog stays beside her while she draws. People feel more at ease when they know common autistic traits. Understanding grows from that knowledge. No two autistic people are the same. Certain characteristics still show up often. Senses can work in a different way. Loud noise can upset a person. Bright light can upset a person. Learning often follows a path of its own. Some school subjects come quickly. Other subjects are a battle. A single topic can hold intense fascination. Almost any subject can become that interest. Movement can be a way to stimulate the body. People call the habit stimming. Rocking back and forth is one form. Flapping the hands is another. Other motions get repeated as well. Nerves settle when the motion is a comfort. Boredom eases when the motion is entertainment. Order matters to many autistic people. A routine can matter just as much.

7Lining objects up can feel satisfying. Organizing a space can feel satisfying. A day that follows a familiar plan is what many of them prefer. Getting a message across can be hard. Catching another person's meaning can be hard too. Saying what one wants may not be possible. Saying what one needs may not be possible. Spoken words are not how some people communicate. Writing is the choice for some. A device is the choice for others. A social setting can feel uncomfortable. Many autistic people would rather avoid that strain. Stories from autistic people are reaching the public more than before. Writing carries some of those stories. Speaking carries others. Art carries others. The message is a picture of daily life. Unique needs are part of the message. A need for support is part of it. Respect is what they ask for at the same time. Speech can come through a communication device. An tablet can serve that role. One student uses a device while ordering lunch. The line is in the school cafeteria. Temple Grandin offers a clear example. An autism diagnosis came when she was two. Speech therapy built her language. It built social skills as well. Other abilities grew there too. She later became a professor. Animal science is her field. Colorado State University is where she teaches. Autism, she says, lets her grasp how animals think. It lets her grasp how they behave. She travels widely to speak about rights. Autistic people are the ones those rights protect.

8A wish that could erase autism would not tempt her. She would leave that wish unused. Autism, in her words, belongs to her identity. Noah Mundt's seventh-grade teacher built a small crew. Three boys joined it. None of the three was autistic. They were neurotypical. Friendship with Noah grew from that crew. Mentoring grew from it too. Time with the crew taught Noah new habits. He picked up more about how to greet someone. He picked up more about getting along. He picked up ways to steady himself when he felt upset. The boys gained a friend they had not had. They also saw his challenges more clearly than before. Acceptance reached Noah in a way that was new to him. His mom talks about what the boys gave. They offered time. They offered energy. The offer was genuine. Each person in the group came out ahead. Their attention helped Noah open himself to more people. Teasing is a burden some autistic children carry. Bullying is another. The people doing it may not see the struggle underneath. Social cues are easy to miss. Meanness can leave an autistic person confused. Being ignored can do the same. Agitation can follow. A full day of difficulty stays hidden from many teasers. The next day brings those difficulties again. Noah's mom believes a real friendship can shift how we look at other people. The world may not read the same way to an autistic friend. Emotions are still present. Those emotions may even run stronger than they do for the rest of us.

9A friend or a classmate with autism gives you a chance to be an ally. These habits help. Study autism, then pass what you learn to your family. Pass it to your friends as well. Take autistic children as they are. Odd-looking behavior is not a joke. Flapping hands is one behavior people should not mock. Comfort is not the same for every person. A bright light can be too much. A loud noise can be too much. Hugs are welcome to some autistic people. Hugs are unwelcome to others. Tell a teacher if autistic schoolmates are picked on. Defending them in some other manner can also help. Spend time getting to know an autistic neighbor. Do the same with an autistic classmate. Let that person lead when talking about likes. Join after they open the door. If no spoken words are available, you can narrate the thing you are doing together. Say hi. Ask a question. Offer a thought. Shape a party so an autistic guest can feel at ease. Shape any other gathering the same way. Keep autistic friends inside ordinary plans. A game counts. An art project counts. Have support ready when they need it. Camps and other programs operate around the world. Autistic people use them to build skills. Concentration is one of those skills. Language is another. Understanding of autism inside science is still young. The condition is full of complexity. Research in this area is exciting. Families are searching for the best kind of support.

10Communities are searching too. Personal stories now include needs. They include wants. They include hopes. More autistic people are the ones telling those stories. The many faces of autism are on display more than they used to be. More people are able to see them. These words appear in the book. Advocacy is the act of standing up for a cause. It is also the process of doing so. Environmental refers to the world around a person, the part that can shape that person. Autism is a developmental condition. Repeated behavior patterns can be part of it. Trouble with communication can be part of it. Trouble with social interaction can be part of it. Genetics is the science of traits that parents hand down. Children born later carry those traits. Neurotypical means the brain is working in a typical way. A characteristic is a feature that helps someone recognize a thing. It can also mark a group of things. Nonverbal means words are not used. Speech is not used. A controversy is a disagreement about an idea. Sensory describes whatever relates to the physical senses. Developmental describes growth. A person can be the one growing. A thing can be the one growing. A trait is a feature or a quality. Animals can have traits. Plants can have them. Things can have them. Groups can have them. Diagnosed means an illness was identified. An injury can be identified that way. A condition can be identified that way.

4. Baseball's Biggest Series

1A chart counts World Series wins by league from 1903 to 2021. The American League has 66. The National League has 51. This book calls the Series baseball's grandest stage. It tells of the first Series. It follows the road that teams travel to get there. It ends with famous moments and a word list. Picture your favorite team ahead in the World Series. The lead is three games to two. A championship comes with one more win. Now the team is behind by a score of 6 to 5. It is the bottom of the ninth inning. One batter is out. Two men are on base. Joe Carter comes up. He drove in 121 runs in the regular season. That total was third best in the league. The slugger knocked in a run earlier in the Series. He has not had a good Series. The stadium holds 52,195 voices. Those voices swing between hushed silence and throaty roars. At the same time, 40 million people watch the game on television. The other team's ace reliever is Mitch Williams. He brings the count to two balls and two strikes. What will he throw next? Will Carter be the hero? Here is the pitch. Here is the swing. Crack! The ball soars high. It goes higher. Will it get out? It does. The hometown crowd goes wild. Carter leaps around the bases. The hit is a walk-off home run. It scores three runs. It wins the game. It wins the Series. Never before in World Series history has a player ended the Fall Classic this way.

2He did it with a home run that came from behind. Winning the World Series earns a team the Commissioner's Trophy. A picture shows Toronto Blue Jay Joe Carter. He made World Series history in 1993. His home run came in the bottom of the ninth inning. This thrilling and dramatic ending sounds like a Hollywood movie. It is not a movie. It is the true story of the 1993 World Series. Unbelievable moments run through more than a century of the Series. Clutch hitting is part of that history. Clever pitching is part of it. Great fielding is part of it. October is when stars showcase their amazing talents. It is also when unknown players shine. They stand in the spotlight of baseball's grandest stage. A photograph shows a team from 1882. The team is the National League's Chicago White Stockings. Those players later became the White Sox. Baseball captured the country's imagination in the mid-nineteenth century. Amateurs played the game then. They played for fun. They played for exercise. Interest in the game led to a new league in 1876. It was a National League of professional teams. In the decades that followed, other professional leagues started and then disappeared. Sometimes the best teams in each league played against each other. These season-ending games were not very popular. By 1901, fans wanted play of a high caliber. That demand led to a new league. The American League was competitive. At first, team owners in the National League ignored the American League.

3Players in the National League ignored it too. They called it a minor league. Meanwhile, the American League had lured players away from the National League. Many of its teams were in the same cities as the National League. Fans of the public's national pastime went to see American League games. That hurt National League team owners in the place they felt it most. It hurt their pocketbooks. The owners quickly realized they could make extra money. They could stage a contest between the top teams from both leagues. Gate fees would be shared with the players. So, in 1903, a matchup was set. The American League Boston Americans took the field. Their nickname was the Pilgrims. They took on the National League's best team. That team was the Pittsburgh Pirates. A picture of the first World Series, in 1903, shows a packed park. Crowds clustered around the outfield. Crowds clustered around the infield too. This first series between the two leagues was not a formal competition. Yet it offered heart-stopping highs. It offered heartbreaking lows. Moments like these have stayed part of every World Series since then. The Boston team was the underdog. The team was down three games to one. Boston came back with four straight victories. The American League pitching great Cy Young won two of those games. Meanwhile, Honus Wagner choked under the pressure. He was the National League batting champ. He hit a measly .222. He made six errors. A side note tells how the game changed. In 1887, Major League Baseball became an officially segregated sport.

4The owners decided not to allow Black players on their teams. They decided not to allow other nonwhite players either. After World War II, there was a push to desegregate baseball. In 1947, desegregation began. Jackie Robinson joined the Dodgers. He went on to play in six World Series. He became one of the most famous players in baseball. A picture shows Jackie Robinson sliding into home base. Christy Mathewson set a record in the 1905 World Series. He threw three shutouts. That mark has never been equaled. People called him Matty. He was modern baseball's first superstar. In 1904, the New York Giants led the National League. They refused to accept the post-season challenge from the American League. In October 1905, the Giants took the field again. They faced an American League powerhouse. The other team was the Philadelphia Athletics. The series made history. An old baseball saying was on display. The saying is that good pitching beats good hitting. The Giants' pitcher was Christy Mathewson. He was just 25. He had won 94 games in the last three seasons. He threw three shutouts across six days. That is 27 innings. Not a single run scored in those innings. He did it with style too. He struck out 18 batters. He walked only one batter. From that year on, the World Series became an official event. The format was best of seven. Today, the World Series is the high point of the season. The season belongs to Major League Baseball. People call the league MLB.

5The winner of the American League playoffs faces another winner. The other winner comes from the National League playoffs. They play a best-of-seven series. The team that wins the World Series gets the Commissioner's Trophy. The team also gets bragging rights. For a year, that club is called the best in the world. Reaching the World Series is a long trip. It is a hard trip. First, each of the thirty MLB teams plays 162 games. Each league has 15 teams. Play in the regular season runs from April through September. Then there are playoffs in each league. The playoffs include a wild card playoff. They include two division series. They include a league championship. Here is how it works. Each league has fifteen teams. The teams sit in three geographic divisions. The divisions are East, Central, and West. Each division has five teams. The leader of each division earns a playoff spot automatically. That leader is the team with the best season record. Those leaders make six teams. Two more teams from each league are the wild card teams. They hold the next-best records. They play a one-game playoff. The winner takes the last spot in the Division Series. The playoffs therefore include ten teams in total. The American League team that wins its league's playoffs moves on. The National League team that wins its playoffs moves on too. Those two clubs advance to the World Series. A chart lays out the 2021 playoffs. It shows the American League and the National League. It labels a championship series and division series on the way to a World Series champion.

6The chart names Houston, Milwaukee, Atlanta, Chicago, Tampa Bay, and San Francisco. It names Boston and Los Angeles. Atlanta won 4 of 6 games. Boston beat the New York Yankees for a wild card slot. Los Angeles beat St. Louis for a wild card slot. The trip to the first World Series was not as complicated. That series was in 1903. Back then, there were only sixteen teams. Eight teams played in each league. There were no playoffs either. The team from each league with the best season record went straight to the World Series. The format of this playoff is best of seven. It pits the two teams against each other. They play as many games as it takes for one team to win four games. One team hosts the first two games at its home stadium. The next three games are played at the home stadium of the other team. Those three games happen if they are necessary. The final two games are played back at the first team's home stadium. Those two games happen if they are necessary. Someone must win the series by game seven. Famous moments fill the World Series. Christy Mathewson's performance in the 1905 World Series made him a superstar. The World Series grew as well. It became the climax of professional American sports. In those years, football was mostly played by colleges. Basketball had been invented only a few years earlier. It was decades away from becoming a professional sport.

7Hockey was popular in other countries. Soccer was popular in other countries too. Neither sport was well known in the United States. Baseball was the sporting event people talked about most. It was the event reporters covered most. It was the event fans awaited most eagerly. The World Series stood at the center of that attention. Players drew inspiration from all that attention on the World Series. Some of baseball's most amazing feats have happened during the World Series. Some of its most memorable feats happened there too. One famous moment is Babe Ruth's called shot. The teams were the New York Yankees and the Chicago Cubs. It was Game 3 in 1932. The Cubs are down two games to none. Their fans boo loudly. The boos come when Yankees home-run king Babe Ruth hits one out. The home run is in the first inning. By the fifth inning, the Cubs have tied the game. The score is 4 to 4. Ruth comes up again. The chorus of boos is even louder. A picture shows Babe Ruth. The pitcher throws a strike. Ruth holds up a finger. He seems to point toward center field. Another called strike comes from the pitcher. Ruth holds up the finger again. The gesture looks like a message. It says to throw another pitch like that, and he will hit it to center field. The pitcher takes the challenge. Ruth swings. He sends the ball over the wall. The landing spot is deep center field. The crowd goes silent. Ruth laughs while rounding the bases.

8Did Ruth really call the place where he would hit the ball? Or were the raised fingers just his way of marking the count? Was the gesture only a try at quieting the crowd? There is no instant replay to show what really happened. The moment was a World Series game. It does not really matter which story is true. Ruth's colorful performance has become a baseball legend. He did it in front of a World Series crowd. Another famous moment is Willie Mays's catch. The teams were the New York Giants and the Cleveland Indians. It was Game 1 in 1954. The score is tied 2 to 2. It is the eighth inning. The Indians have put two runners on base. The Indians are now named the Guardians. Vic Wertz hits a ball to deep center field. Willie Mays of the Giants turns when the bat cracks. He sprints straight to the outfield wall. The wall is some 450 feet from home plate. That distance is 137 meters. He extends his hands at just the right moment. He makes an over-the-shoulder catch. A picture note says the catch has never been equaled for grace on such a big stage. It has never been equaled for speed. It has never been equaled for exactness. Broadcasters cry out that the play is unbelievable. They cry out that it is miraculous. Then Mays has the presence of mind to spin. He uncorks an amazing throw back to the infield. The runners, startled, go back to their bases. This long out stopped the Indians' momentum.

9It set the tone for the series. The Giants won four games in a row. A third famous player is Reggie Jackson. People call him Mr. October. The teams were the New York Yankees and the Los Angeles Dodgers. It was Game 6 in 1977. The Yankees are ahead, three games to two. They lead 7 to 3. It is the eighth inning. Reggie Jackson steps up. What a game he has had. He hit home runs in the fourth inning. He hit a home run in the fifth inning. Both times the hit came on the first pitch. The pitches came from different pitchers. A picture shows Reggie Jackson. Here is the first pitch from a third pitcher. The pitcher is Charlie Hough. The bat makes a smack. The ball is going, going, and gone. Reggie Jackson is the first player to hit three home runs in one World Series game since Babe Ruth. Ruth did it in 1928. Even more remarkable, Jackson did it on the first pitch each time. Jackson set another World Series record in 1978. He hit a total of five home runs. He earned the nickname Mr. October. The grand stage of the World Series has hosted rare feats. Two of them are among the rarest in baseball. In 1920, the feat was a one-man triple play. In one play, second baseman Bill Wambsganss made all three defensive outs. He made them by himself. Don Larsen pitched a perfect game in 1956. No batter on the other team reached base.

10What will this year's World Series bring? When October comes, watch and see what happens. A side story tells of an underdog upset. The New York Mets franchise had a rough start. Seven years were dreadful. They were frequently embarrassing. The lowly Mets finished ninth in 1968. They beat odds of 100 to 1. They made the World Series in 1969. They went on to overcome the Baltimore Orioles. The Orioles were highly favored. The Mets won the series 4 games to 1. The win earned them the nickname Miracle Mets. These words help a reader follow the Series. Caliber is the degree of skill, ability, or importance. A season is a certain time of year when something usually happens. A sport may be played then. A type of weather may take place then. A division is a separate group inside a larger organization or group. Shutouts are games in which one side never scores. Feats are amazing actions or accomplishments. A triple play is a baseball play that gets three players out. A league is a group of sports teams that play each other. An underdog is the expected loser in a struggle or contest. Playoffs are a series of games after the regular season. They determine a champion. A wild card is a player or team chosen after the other competitors have qualified. Professional means earning money for an activity. The person is not doing it purely for pleasure. The World Series is the yearly championship of Major League Baseball in the United States.

5. Coach Pat Summitt's Winning Life

1The year 1976 kept Pat Summitt very busy. Her age that year was twenty-four. College basketball had already made her a star player. The team she once played for now had her as coach. The team held little money. The school offered little support. Even so, she led them through a season of wins. Graduate school came to an end during those same months. A silver medal was part of the year too. The medal came from the Olympic Games. The host city was Montreal, in Canada. Very few women before her had so many chances to rise in sports. Later, millions of women found those chances. Millions of girls found them too. Her career ran a long time, and it inspired people. That career is one reason later athletes could succeed. She piled up more wins than any other coach in college basketball for women. Attention, she always said, should stay on the athletes. She did not want it fixed on the coach. The players were the point of the work. A busy year of study, medals, and team wins was only the start. Pat's birth year was 1952. Clarksville, Tennessee, was her birthplace. Hard work became a lesson while she was still young. The classroom for that lesson was her family's farm. Morning started at 5:00 am. The cows had to be milked.

2Play was just as fierce. She shot hoops with her older brothers. The barn was their court. They played only after the chores were finished. Competitive sports were rarely open to young girls then. Grade school basketball meant travel for Pat. The games sat several towns away. High school graduation left her with few paths. Athletic scholarships had carried her older brothers to college. Girls could not get those scholarships in 1970. Her parents paid the college bill. The campus she entered was the one at Martin. It belonged to the University of Tennessee. Basketball filled her time there. Teammates chose her as the most valuable player. The short name for that honor is MVP. A photo catches her skill in those college years. She is playing at Martin for the University of Tennessee. During senior year, Summitt hurt her knee badly. Back in the 1970s, damage like that usually ended a playing career. Surgery repaired the knee. She returned to the court anyway. Pain did not keep her off it. College ended that same year at UT Martin. No player in the program had scored more across a career. Her total was 1,045 points. Four seasons produced that sum. Determination marked her as a player. Leadership on the court marked her too. A chart tracks how many students play high school sports.

3A 2017 report from the NCAA supplies the chart. The report looks back across 45 years of Title IX. Its subject is women in college sports. Eight figures appear on the chart. They are 4,544,574 and 4,494,406. They are 3,709,512 and 3,666,917. They are 3,324,326 and 3,173,549. They are 1,854,400 and 294,015. Columns separate boys from girls. Girls in school sports have increased in a dramatic way since 1972. More than 19,500 schools contributed the counts. A national federation is where those schools hold membership. The federation ties together state associations for high school sports. Summitt started college near the beginning of the 1970s. Across the United States, female athletes met steep barriers. Women who wanted a sport were guided toward games with little contact. Tennis was one approved choice. Croquet was another. Archery was another. Men held most of the team sports. College teams for women were usually intramural. Intramural play stays inside one school. Other schools are not the opponent. Money for the women's teams was only a fraction of the men's money. Support was only a fraction too. A legal change arrived in 1972. Congress passed Title IX. Public schools could no longer discriminate on the basis of gender. Universities fell under the same rule. Women had to be offered the same opportunities men received.

4Observers linked Title IX to an older law. The older law was the Civil Rights Act of 1964. Race discrimination became illegal under that act. Sports felt a tremendous positive impact. Young women were encouraged to be athletic. More resources were placed in their hands so success was possible. President Richard Nixon signed Title IX. His photo sits at the top of the page. A second photo shows a women's soccer team from the United States. The players are celebrating a championship. The year was 2015, and the tournament was the Women's World Cup. Because of Title IX, American women now succeed in many sports at a world-class standard. Summitt coaches during a game in a photo from 1978. The opponent is Kentucky. Graduation came in 1974. Summitt then headed to Knoxville and the University of Tennessee. A master's degree was her aim. A job opened when the women's basketball coach left. The departure came on short notice. Her leadership as a player had already impressed the school. She was twenty-two. The head coach role was offered to her. The team name was Lady Volunteers. Fans say Lady Vols. Four players on the roster were close to her in age. Each of those four was younger by only a year. Title IX was already in effect. True equality for women's sports was still slow to arrive.

5The budget for women's basketball at Tennessee was tiny. Doughnut sales covered the cost of uniforms. Between games, Summitt washed those uniforms herself. Her first season as coach ended 16-8. Year two produced sixteen wins once more. She completed the master's degree during that year. The largest success of the year came while she was still a player. She made the first Olympic basketball team the United States ever fielded for women. The year was 1976. Her age was twenty-four, which made her the oldest player in the group. Leadership of the group fell to her at once. A silver medal followed, and her play helped earn it. Coaching wins piled up over the next two years. Each of those seasons brought the Lady Vols past twenty wins. Two regional championships came in that span as well. Later seasons climbed higher. One season brought thirty wins. The next brought thirty-three. A historic coaching career was just getting started. College basketball has few records that stand as tall. Another photo shows her handling the ball. The opponent is Czechoslovakia. The setting is the Olympic Games of 1976. Players crowd around Pat Summitt in a celebration photo. They have just taken the NCAA Women's National Championship. The year is 1997. A national title event for women finally appeared in 1982.

6Fans know the event as March Madness. The National College Athletics Association created it. The short name is NCAA. The Lady Vols reached the Final Four. Louisiana Tech beat them. An Olympic coaching post followed two years after that loss. She led the women's basketball squad for the United States. The squad took gold. No American before her held Olympic medals from both jobs. Playing had produced one medal. Coaching had produced the other. Thirty-eight seasons passed without a losing record for the Lady Vols she led. Nine NCAA tournaments ended in a title. The 1997-98 team went undefeated. Basketball almost never sees a season with no losses. Retirement came with 1,098 wins on her coaching record. No college coach, in the men's game or the women's game, had more. The mark lasted until 2016. Doughnut sales had once kept the team going. Her pay later reached a million dollars a year. Respect from the school finally arrived for her players. Support arrived with it. Schools across the country began to offer the same respect. They began to offer the same support. Players from the 1984 Olympic women's basketball team of the United States lift their coach. They carry Pat Summitt off the court. Gold medals are the reason for the celebration. She taught twelve rules and called the list her Definite Dozen.

7Every player heard them. Show respect for yourself, and show it for other people. Accept full responsibility for what you do. Build loyalty, then let people see it. Become someone who can share ideas clearly. Set your own discipline so nobody else must supply it. Care deeply about hard work. Pair that hard work with smart work. Place the team ahead of your own wishes. Treat a winning mindset as the standard. Compete with real drive. Welcome change, because change has to come. Face a success with the same steadiness you use after a failure. Hard work was a belief she passed on. Personal responsibility was another. Players felt the result during games. They felt it away from the court too. College graduation happened for every player she coached. A professional league for women basketball players was founded in 1996. Its full name is Women's National Basketball Association. People say WNBA. Players in that league had grown up with women's sports treated as serious. Fans had grown up in that same world. Title IX had made the opportunities more equal. Coaches and players showed something else as well. Pat Summitt was one of them. They proved women's basketball could be competitive. They proved it could be exciting. The WNBA never employed Summitt directly.

8Her influence still reached the league. The New York Liberty played the league's earliest game. That team also played in the earliest championship. Nancy Darsch was the Liberty coach. She had served as an assistant under Summitt. College players under Summitt numbered 161. Almost a quarter of them later played in the league. Sixteen players she had coached were chosen in a WNBA first round. A photo catches her in talk with Candace Parker. Parker was already a star. The setting is the NCAA Tournament of 2008. Parker went on to rank among the biggest stars the WNBA has had. A later photo catches Summitt reacting to a play. The game is a semifinal. The event is the NCAA Championship of 2005. The court had rarely found a way to beat her. A harder opponent showed up in 2011. Doctors discovered Alzheimer's disease. Memory fades as this disease advances. Elderly people are the usual patients. She was not yet sixty when the news came. One last season of coaching followed. A longtime assistant coach helped her through it. Retirement came next. Continuing the work was about to be too hard on her health. Death came five years after that retirement. The legacy she left is powerful. Women's basketball changed because of her career. Sports opened further for women. People near her felt inspired.

9She pushed them to grow as people. She pushed them to grow as competitors. A foundation carries her name today. Its fundraising looks for an Alzheimer's cure. Strength from her life still inspires others. Perseverance from her life does the same. Tyler, her son, celebrates beside Summitt. The win they share is the NCAA Championship. The year is 1996. A timeline marks Pat Summitt through the years. The year 1952 is her birth on a farm. Clarksville, Tennessee, is the place. The year 1970 is when she starts college at Martin, part of the University of Tennessee. The year 1972 is when Congress passes Title IX. The year 1974 is her college graduation and her naming as coach of the Lady Vols. The year 1976 is a silver medal with the first Olympic women's basketball team from the United States, a team she helps lead. The year 1982 is when the NCAA adds March Madness for women, and her Lady Vols get as far as the Final Four. The year 1984 is a return to the Olympics, where the women's team she coaches for the United States wins gold. The year 1998 is an undefeated season for the Lady Vols under her lead. The year 2011 is the Alzheimer's disease diagnosis. The year 2012 is her retirement, holding more wins than any coach in college basketball history before her.

10The year 2016 is her death at age sixty-four. These words appear in the book. Alzheimer's disease makes sections of the brain quit working correctly, a little at a time. Gender can mean being male or being female. It can mean males taken as a group. It can mean females taken as a group. Athletic describes speed, strength, or skill in sports. An injury is harm or damage done to the body. A championship is a contest that decides a winner. A league is a set of sports teams that play one another. A legacy is what later generations receive from someone's actions. Beliefs can produce it. Achievements can produce it. Competitive describes a rivalry among people. The rivalry can also be among teams or businesses. To discriminate is to treat a person unfairly, or to treat a group that way. Gender can be the reason. Race, age, or religion can be the reason. Some other difference can be the reason. Perseverance is the quality of staying determined. Commitment holds even through a challenge. It holds even through a delay. A feat is an action that amazes people. It can be a great accomplishment. A tournament is a run of games, or of other competitions, that names a final champion.

6. Dry Lands of Earth

1Saguaro cacti grow in only one desert. Say their name like this: suh-WAR-oh. That desert is the Sonoran Desert. Most saguaros stand in southern Arizona. Many also stand in northwestern Mexico. A saguaro can live longer than two hundred years. This book follows dry lands in order. It begins with open space and quiet. It explains the desert biome. It sorts deserts into types. It looks at desert plants. It looks at desert animals. It tells how people live in deserts. It ends by showing how much there is to see. A glossary lists important words. In spring, wildflowers cover the ground like a carpet. They fill the Mojave Desert. That desert lies in Southern California. People who search online for desert descriptions often meet the phrase empty wasteland. They also meet barren, desolate landscape. Deserts have a reputation as harsh places. Many people call them unpleasant. They picture life there hanging on by a thread. They think a sensible person would not dare to go. People who love deserts tell another story. For them, these lands are still and beautiful. You can hear your own thoughts. You can see for miles in each direction. Living things there developed fascinating ways to survive. Water is rare in a desert. Water there is precious too. Different kinds of environments cover the surface of Earth. A biome is a region of that kind. It includes the plants living there. It also includes the animals.

2Grasslands are biomes. Rainforests are biomes. Oceans are biomes. Deserts are biomes too. Many factors help build Earth's biomes. Latitude is one important factor. It tells how far a place sits from the equator. Elevation is another factor. It means height above sea level. Both help decide whether a place stays warm or cool. Climate helps build a biome. Land and water help too. Plants and animals are part of the build as well. People often picture a desert as hot, empty, and full of endless sand. That picture fits some deserts. It does not fit every desert. Heat is not the trait that defines a desert. Low rainfall is the trait. As a rule, yearly rain in a desert stays under 25 centimeters. That measure equals 10 inches. The Sahara lies in northern Africa. It is the world's largest hot desert. Wind keeps changing the shape of its land. A map of desert regions shows Asia, Europe, and North America. It shows the Atlantic Ocean. It shows the Pacific Ocean on two sides. It also shows Africa, the Indian Ocean, South America, Australia, and Antarctica. Deserts cover about one-fifth of the land on Earth. Every continent has at least one desert. Antarctica is included. Most of Antarctica is counted as a cold desert. Hot deserts often have clear skies day after day. Humidity means how much water vapor the air holds. In a hot desert, that amount is often too small for clouds to form.

3Clouds do form at times. Rain from them may turn to gas before it reaches the ground. Rain like that is called virga. The kind of precipitation depends on the kind of desert. Some deserts can go a full year, or even longer, with no rain. A long dry spell can raise the chance of wildfire. Wildfires happen more often now than they did long ago. Global warming brings droughts to many desert regions. It also brings higher temperatures there. A large haboob moves over the land in Mali. Mali is in West Africa. In a cold desert, snow makes up much of the precipitation. It does not fall mainly as rain. A warmer desert may have one rainy season. Some have two. A sudden, violent storm can send a flash flood through a desert. Thunderstorms often bring strong winds. In a hot desert, those winds pick up lots of sand. They pick up lots of dust too. At times the wind builds a haboob. A haboob is a huge wall of sand and dust. It may rise nearly 3 kilometers. That height is about 2 miles. The hottest temperature ever recorded on Earth was measured at Death Valley. Death Valley is in California. The year was 1913. The reading was 57 degrees Celsius, or 134 degrees Fahrenheit. Day and night temperatures in a desert can be very different.

4The reason is a lack of humidity. Where the air is wetter, humidity tends to keep heat in after sunset. In Chihuahua, Mexico, a daytime reading can reach 37 degrees Celsius. That is 100 degrees Fahrenheit. On that same day, night can fall below freezing. Freezing is 0 degrees Celsius, or 32 degrees Fahrenheit. Earth's deserts show amazing variety. They still fit into four main types. Hot, dry deserts are the hottest type. They are also the best known. The weather is generally warm through the whole year. A summer extreme can hit 49 degrees Celsius. That is 120 degrees Fahrenheit. After dark, the temperature can fall fast. This drop is strongest in winter. Rain may stay low all year. Or short bursts of rain may come. Long dry seasons separate those bursts. The Sahara, in Africa, is the largest hot, dry desert. Its size is close to the size of the continental United States. Other deserts of this type lie in southwestern North America. They also lie in Central America and South America. More stand in South Asia and in Australia. A semiarid desert has a long, dry summer. That summer is not as hot as summer in a hot, dry desert. Winters are cold. They bring little rain or snow. In North America, the Great Basin Desert in Nevada is semiarid. The Colorado Plateau is semiarid too. That large region takes in part of Utah. It also takes in part of Colorado, part of Arizona, and part of New Mexico.

5The Tabernas Desert in Spain is Europe's only true desert. It is also classed as semiarid. Mountains can keep moisture away from some deserts. That is why those deserts stay dry. Warm, wet air climbs one side of a range. As the air rises, it cools. Clouds form. Rain or snow falls. The air then moves down the far side. It warms again on the way down. The clouds disappear. The land on that far side sits in a rain shadow. A coastal desert usually lies along the western edge of a continent. Cold ocean currents run along that coast. They bring winter fog. They often block sunlight. The weather is very dry. It is not extremely hot. Summers are warm. Winters are cool. In a typical year, rain totals 8 to 13 centimeters. That span is 3 to 5 inches. The Atacama Desert is in South America. The Namib Desert is in Africa. Both are coastal deserts. A cold desert has a long, cold winter with high snowfall. Summer is short. Snow may remain frozen through many years. Cold desert covers most of Antarctica. The Gobi Desert in Asia is a cold desert. So is the Patagonian Desert in South America. Most of Greenland is cold desert too. A desert is so dry that living things find very little liquid water. Adaptations still let them live where precipitation is low.

6Desert plants use three main ways to survive. One group is the succulents. They store water in roots, stems, or leaves. A cactus is a succulent. Many other plants are succulents too. Thick, waxy skin covers most succulents. That skin holds water in. They often grow shallow roots. Those roots can soak up rain quickly when it falls. A second group drops its leaves in the dry season. The plants become dormant. When rain returns, they spring back to life fast. Some plants in this group grow a long taproot. A mesquite tree is one of them. The taproot goes far down under the ground, where it can find water. Baby toes is a succulent. It is not a cactus. It does not have spines. A third set of adaptations avoids drought instead of lasting through it. Plants in this group include many desert wildflowers. They grow up fast. A full life cycle ends for them before dry weather begins. They put their energy into seeds. Those seeds germinate when the rains come back. Plants make their own food by a chemical process called photosynthesis. Tiny holes called stomata let carbon dioxide gas enter. Stomata sit mainly on the underside of a leaf. Leaves hold a green pigment called chlorophyll. It helps a plant capture sunlight. With that light, the plant makes glucose. Carbon dioxide and water supply the materials. Glucose is a simple sugar.

7Photosynthesis needs sunlight, so most plants do it by day. Opening stomata makes a plant lose a lot of water. That loss would be a serious problem in a desert. Many desert plants make food without losing much water. They keep stomata shut during the day. They open those holes at night. Night air is cooler. It is also moister. They store carbon dioxide through the night. On the following day, they use that stored gas as they make food. A palo verde tree has another way to save water. In a very dry stretch, it drops its leaves so water will not escape. It can still make food. Chlorophyll in its green bark does that work. An African bullfrog hides under sand. A ground squirrel holds its tail up as a shield against sunlight. Both animals live in the Kalahari Desert in Africa. A shortage of water is hard for desert animals. High heat in many deserts is hard too. Few large mammals live in a hot desert, compared with most other biomes. They do not handle the heat well. They do not handle the dry conditions well either. During the hottest hours, many desert animals stay out of the sun. Squirrels do this. They hide in a burrow. They also hide under rocks or plants. Some animals are nocturnal, which means they are active at night. Owls are nocturnal. So are bilbies, small marsupials from Australia.

8Others are crepuscular. They are active at dawn and at dusk. A rattlesnake is usually crepuscular. Some animals leave during the hottest or driest part of the year. Bats are among them. A desert toad becomes dormant while it waits. It waits for rain or for cooler air to return. Desert animals have more ways to stay cool. A coyote pants. An owl flutters its throat. A jackrabbit lets body heat leave through its large ears. A fennec fox does the same. A bighorn sheep sheds its winter coat in stages. Hair on the belly goes first. Hair on other shaded parts goes first too. Losing that hair helps body heat escape. Getting enough water is another hard job. Many desert animals eat succulents. Others take nectar or sap.
