Some animals pass waste that holds very little water. Reptiles often do this. Birds often do it too. A few animals have unusual adaptations for getting water. A thorny devil lizard has grooves between its spikes. The grooves take in dew that collects there. They send that dew to the mouth. Pictures of these animals show a black-tailed jackrabbit and a thorny devil lizard. Desert living goes back many thousands of years. Today this biome is home to about one billion people. That is about one-seventh of the people on Earth. Like plants and other animals, people had to adapt. A home there required them to get enough water.

9In the past, many desert dwellers were nomadic. As each season changed, they moved. The move was a search for water. It was also a search for food. Protective clothes shielded them from sunlight. The same clothes shielded them from sandstorms. In deserts of Africa and Asia, animals often gave people a way to travel. Those animals were camels, sheep, goats, and horses. They also gave milk. The nomadic Tuareg people live in the Sahara Desert. They are sometimes called the blue people. Newer ways of desert life often bring water in from somewhere else. Dams and irrigation have let millions of people live in deserts. People in modern desert cities and towns treat air-conditioning as essential. Deserts draw many tourists. Visitors come to hike and to camp. They also come to enjoy the quiet and the natural beauty. Crepuscular can describe more than animals. It can describe other things linked to twilight. During a Sonoran Desert sunset, crepuscular rays stream outward from the clouds. Desert plants are unique and fascinating. Desert animals are too. So are the landscapes. There is still more to notice. Tall trees are scarcer here than in most other biomes. That makes the sky easier to see. The sky often puts on a show that is too dramatic to miss. Storm clouds can grow so huge that they seem to have a life of their own. At times the sky fills with unusual sunrays.

10They look so amazing that they hardly seem real. Meteor showers are a special treat at night. The Milky Way's huge arc is another special sight at night. Deserts are often seen as harsh places. In reality they are treasures. Wondrous gems in them are waiting to be found. Here are meanings for words in this book. An adaptation is a change in a living thing, or in a species, that helps it survive better where it lives. To conserve means to use as little as you can so nothing is wasted. Dormant means quiet for now. The living thing can become active later. A drought is a long dry spell with little rain, or with none. An environment is the full set of conditions that affect living things in a certain area. Those conditions include plants, animals, weather, and landforms. Evaporate means to change from a liquid into a gas, usually because of heat. Fascinating means very interesting. Flash flooding is what happens when a large amount of rainwater floods a place. Irrigation means bringing water to land or crops so they will grow. Precipitation is water that comes down from the sky. Hail is one form. Sleet, rain, and snow are forms too. A reputation is the general way others think of a person or a thing. A trait is one feature. It may belong to an animal, a plant, a thing, or a group.

7. Earth's Great Salt Waters

1Waves break on the Indian Ocean. The ocean is a huge place. It is full of mystery. Stand on a ship and give the water a quick look. You may feel you are nowhere in particular. The water can seem to have no end. The surface can look empty of life. Dark waves chop the surface. They stretch away on every side. The real ocean is not empty. Life fills it. So do movement and color. Marine life comes in a wide range of sizes. At the small end are green algae made of one cell. Giant beds of seagrass are at a large end. Viruses too small to see are here as well. Blue whales live here too. Earth has no record of any animal bigger than a blue whale. Mountain ranges rise under the water. Active volcanoes are there. Huge plateaus are there. Deep trenches are there too. People still have much of this fascinating world left to explore. This biome is one of the most important on Earth. Weather depends on it. Climate depends on it. Food supplies around the world depend on it too. This book moves through that hidden world in order. It starts with how vast the ocean is. It explains the ocean biome. It looks at water and air. It looks at shapes of the land under the sea. It explains zones and the organisms in them. It tells how people and oceans meet. It closes with treasures from a world that stays mostly hidden. A glossary gives word meanings. Earth's surface holds different kinds of environments. A biome is one of those regions.

2The plants of that region belong to the biome. The animals belong to it too. A desert is a biome. A rainforest is a biome. A river is a biome. An ocean is a biome too. Many factors help make Earth's biomes. On land, latitude is important. It measures the distance from the equator, either toward the north or toward the south. Elevation is important too. It is how high a place sits above the sea. Together they help set whether a place is warm or cool. In a water biome, another factor matters a great deal. The water may be salty. Or the water may be fresh. This book is about the ocean biome. Salt water fills it. About 70 percent of the surface of Earth lies under it. A map of Earth shows these oceans beside the continents. You can find North America, Asia, and Europe on that map. You can find Africa, South America, Australia, and Antarctica. The Arctic Ocean is marked. So are the Atlantic Ocean and the Pacific Ocean. The Indian Ocean is marked too. Long ago, children in school learned about only four oceans. Those four were the Pacific, the Atlantic, the Indian, and the Arctic. In that same period, scientists argued about the count. Some said four. Others said five. In the year 2000, one group set the official list at five. That group is the International Hydrographic Organization. It surveys the oceans and seas of Earth. It also draws charts of them. The ocean added to the list is the Southern Ocean. That ocean wraps around Antarctica.

3Its northern edge is the latitude line at 60 degrees south. Not every country treats the Southern Ocean as a separate ocean. In some schools, children are still taught a count of four. Oceans hold 97 percent of the water on this planet. They are the biggest bodies of water on Earth. People often talk as if there were many oceans. They do that because different sections have names. Those sections all connect. In fact there is just one ocean. By size, the Atlantic is 24 percent. The Pacific is 47 percent. The Indian is 19 percent. The Southern is 6 percent. The Arctic is 4 percent. The Pacific is so huge that it nearly equals the other four oceans added together. A sea is a smaller piece of ocean. A gulf is a smaller piece too. So is a bay. Land wraps partway around each of them. They hold salt water as well. Every ocean on Earth holds salt water. In many other ways the oceans are not alike. At a basic level, parts of the ocean fit Earth's three big climate zones. Those zones are tropical, temperate, and polar. The tropical zones circle the equator. They are the warmest zones. The polar zones sit near the poles. They are the coldest zones. Many other factors shape ocean features too. Water moves in currents. Some currents travel on the surface. Others travel far below. A current can bring warm water into a cold place. A current can bring cold water into a warm place. Blowing wind helps cause surface currents. So does the turning of Earth on its axis.

4Deep currents start for another reason. Water is not the same temperature everywhere. Salt levels differ too. Salinity is the salt level in the water. Those differences set deep currents going. Most ocean waves start because of wind. Tides can form waves as well. Each day, tides go up and then go down. Gravity from the Moon tugs on Earth. That tug makes the tides. The Moon is much nearer to us than the Sun is. For that reason, the Moon changes our tides more than the Sun does. From the surface down to the seafloor, temperature changes a great deal. Water near the top is warmer. One reason is heat from the Sun. Cold water sinks. Cold water is denser than warm water. Cold water also bears much more pressure. The ocean soaks up most of the rays from the Sun. That is one way it shapes weather and climate. It acts much like a giant solar panel. This is especially true in the tropical zones. It carries that heat around the world. The ocean is enormous, so a huge amount of water turns to vapor above it. That evaporation helps run the water cycle. The same process sometimes builds huge storms. The ocean takes carbon dioxide from the air above it. Taking in that gas helps soften what climate change does. At the same time, climate change hits the ocean hard. Temperatures are rising. Ice is melting. Weather patterns are changing. Ocean currents are changing. Shores are wearing away in new ways. Marine ecosystems are changing too. A tropical cyclone is a fierce storm that spins.

5A hurricane is one kind. These storms build over ocean water that is warm. They usually lose strength once they pass onto land. Serious harm is still possible. Land along the ocean edge is called a coast. People also call it a shoreline. The land may be sandy. It may be rocky. It may be muddy. Shallow water lies beside it. That water sits over ground that is a little lower. The lower ground is the continental shelf. Past that shelf, the continental slope begins. The slope drops steeply. It leads down into the deep ocean. The seafloor is always changing. Tectonic plates shift inside Earth's crust. The crust is the rocky outside layer. Those shifting plates reshape the seafloor. Underwater volcanoes stand on the seafloor. Seamounts stand there too. Plateaus and plains are there as well. Mid-ocean ridges are a major seafloor feature. They form a system of mountain chains. The system stretches nearly 65,000 kilometers. That distance is 40,390 miles. The chains formed as tectonic plates moved apart. They keep changing for that same reason. Magma is rock that has melted. It fills the space that opens between the plates. The highest seafloor rises above the waves. Volcanic islands formed there. Iceland is one of those islands. The Hawaiian Islands are others. Tectonic plates are giant slabs of Earth's crust. Hot rock under them is weak, and that rock moves. The moving rock is in the mantle. The mantle is the layer beneath the crust. When plates move, earthquakes can strike. Volcanoes can erupt. Many other changes happen on Earth's surface.

6Trenches are another feature of the seafloor. They are the ocean's deepest places. A trench starts when a pair of tectonic plates head toward one another. One plate is lighter. It forces the denser plate downward, under itself. Then a long valley forms in the seafloor. The valley is steep. Its shape is like the letter V. This process has set off some of the biggest earthquakes on record. It also builds volcanic islands. Japan's islands formed in this way. A group of islands near the coast of Alaska formed this way too. Most trenches are in the Pacific Ocean. The Mariana Trench is the deepest trench. Japan is to the north of it. The Mariana Islands lie close by. Inside a trench it is fully dark. The water is close to freezing. Pressure in that water is more than a thousand times the pressure up on the surface. A picture of the ocean floor can show the continental shelf and a volcanic island. It can show the continental slope, a seamount, and an underwater volcano. It can show a mid-ocean ridge, a trench, the seafloor, and magma. The ocean is split from top to bottom into zones. The split follows how much sunlight each zone gets. The surface receives the most light. Greater depth brings more darkness. The zones also differ in how warm they are. Pressure is not the same. Oxygen is not the same. Mineral nutrients are not the same. The sunlit zone is the surface zone. This zone is only a small share of the full depth, about 5 percent.

7A depth chart puts the sunlit zone at about 200 meters. That is about 660 feet. Enough sunlight arrives for photosynthesis. In photosynthesis, plants make food for themselves. Certain other organisms do this too. Sunlight, air, and water are what they use. Phytoplankton are algae so small that a microscope is needed to see them. Seagrass grows here. Giant kelp grows here too. Giant kelp is another kind of algae. These are the main living things in the ocean that carry out photosynthesis. All life on our planet depends on them. At least half of the oxygen on Earth comes from them. The same chart puts the twilight zone at about 1,000 meters. That is about 3,300 feet. It puts the midnight zone at about 4,000 meters. That is about 13,100 feet. It puts the ocean floor as far down as 6,000 meters. That is about 20,000 feet. The chart is not drawn to scale. Phytoplankton also sit at the base of many food chains in the ocean. They feed a huge mix of animals, and so do other organisms that make food from light. Some animals eat that food straight from the source. Others get it through a longer path. Sea jellies are part of this life. So are crustaceans, fish, and seabirds. So are sea turtles. So are marine mammals, including seals and whales. Photosynthesis is why about 90 percent of ocean life lives in this zone. The sunlit zone is also where coral reefs grow. A reef is a huge community beneath the sea. It is made from skeletons of hard corals, stacked up over time.

8A remarkable number of organisms live with a reef. Algae live together with the corals. The algae make food for the corals. Reef fish eat food that these algae make. Other animals eat it too. Most coral reefs grow in the sunlit zone. Most corals rely on algae. Those algae need the light of the Sun so photosynthesis can happen. Under the sunlit zone is the twilight zone. A little sunlight gets there. It is not enough for photosynthesis. Plants do not live in this zone. No other organism here makes food from sunlight. The light is also too weak to warm the water. The water is cold all the time. Squid live in the twilight zone. Octopuses live there. Sperm whales live there. Certain kinds of fish live there too. Animals in this zone, and farther down, have adaptations for harder conditions. Darkness is greater. Cold is greater. Pressure is greater. Some of these animals give off light. Others have no eyes at all. They depend on senses besides sight. If you measure the whole ocean, about 90 percent of it is midnight zone. Darkness there is total. The water stays near freezing the whole time. Sea cucumbers make a home here. Gulper eels do too. So do unusual fish. This zone is still mostly unknown to us. Exploring it is very hard. The midnight zone includes the trenches. Conditions in a trench are harsh. Even so, organisms do live there, though not many. Bacteria are the base of those food chains. Sunlight is not their energy source. They use hydrogen sulfide gas instead, and that gas lets them produce energy.

9Glow-in-the-dark animals live in both the twilight zone and the midnight zone. They adapted to darkness by glowing. Some are bioluminescent. Their own light comes from a chemical reaction in the body. Others are biofluorescent. They take blue light in. They change it. Then they send out light of other colors. A pyrosome shines with bright flashes in the water near Hawaii. That animal is bioluminescent. In Alaska, a fishing crew loads a catch of silver salmon. Scientists study the ocean so they can learn about Earth's landforms. They learn about weather, climate, and animals. They learn about much more as well. This biome is so fascinating that science alone might seem to be its main gift. It also feeds huge numbers of people in every part of the world. So much food is taken that catching more than the ocean can spare has become a serious problem. The ocean supplies ingredients for medicines. Those medicines are used to treat cancers. They are used to ease pain. They are used to help wounds heal. People have traveled by water since ancient times because the ocean made that travel possible. People also enjoy the ocean for recreation. They go scuba diving and snorkeling. They go surfing, boating, and fishing. They watch whales. They visit islands that lie far away. Walk along a beach and study what you find. Seashells may lie at your feet. Stones may too. So may small pieces of seaweed. Pause and be glad for the ocean. Be glad for the treasures that arrive from it.

10Think about the depth of the water. Think about the organisms it holds. Think about the landforms inside it. Each interesting object that washed ashore came from some place. What was that object's journey like? Most people experience just a little of what the ocean holds. The surface can sparkle in the light. That shine hides a world as fascinating as any biome on land. The hidden world is just as complex. The upper layer is sunny. The trenches are cold and dark. In both places, amazing new finds will always be waiting. A sperm whale near New Zealand slips out of sight in the Pacific Ocean. These words have special meanings in this book. An adaptation is a change in an organism, or in a whole species, that lets it live more successfully where it is. Fascinating means full of interest. Marine means linked to the sea. Plateaus are wide, high stretches of land with flat tops. Algae are organisms that live and grow in water. They produce their own food. They are not plants. Salinity means the quantity of salt held in water. Biomes are groups of plants and animals living in one kind of habitat. Seamounts are undersea mountains built by volcanoes. A current is a flow of air, or of water, moving in a set direction. Tides are the daily rise of the ocean and its fall. They happen because the Moon pulls with gravity. The Sun pulls with gravity too. Evaporation means water turning from liquid into gas when temperature goes up. Trenches are long, narrow cracks along the seafloor.

8. Fire on the Gulf Oil Rig

1A photograph was taken from a helicopter. The helicopter flew in a circle around the burning oil rig on the night after the accident. Night had fallen on April 20, 2010, when a blast lit the sky above the Gulf of Mexico. A fireball shook the Deepwater Horizon, a rig built to drill for oil. The rig was at work forty-two miles from the coast of Louisiana. Within seconds, fire swallowed the Deepwater Horizon. There were 126 workers on the rig. They were trapped in a fiery inferno. The ocean was the only way out. Eleven of the 126 workers died in the explosion. Ships nearby rescued the rest. The injured people were flown to a hospital in Alabama. For two days, ships tried to kill the flames. They sprayed huge amounts of water on the massive rig. A second blast on April 22, 2010, dropped the Deepwater Horizon onto the seabed. The deaths were a major disaster. The injuries were a major disaster. The loss of the rig was a major disaster.

2When the accident happened, no one knew how bad the disaster would become. In the days after it, they still did not know. Fireboats fought the blaze on the Deepwater Horizon. The accident was far worse than the first fears, because the safety valve on the well failed. A safety valve on an oil well is built to stop the oil when an emergency hits. For a reason no one could explain at once, the valve under the Deepwater Horizon did not close. The failed valve let roughly 60,000 barrels of oil escape into the sea each day. By May 3, the oil slick covered 2,500 square miles. That patch of sea was larger than the state of Delaware. On July 15, workers capped the well from the top. By then, about 5 million barrels of oil had entered the Gulf of Mexico. A map of the spill shows Alabama, Mississippi, and Florida. It shows the cities of Mobile and Biloxi, and it shows Mobile Bay.

3It shows Louisiana and the city of New Orleans. It shows the Deepwater Horizon oil spill as it looked on May 14, 2010. The map marks Benton Sound and Barataria Bay. It marks booms, oil on the beach, and wildlife reserves. A scale measured in miles is marked at 0, 40, 80, 120, and 160. The Gulf of Mexico and the Deepwater Horizon oil rig are labeled on the map. Workers tried to catch the leaking oil in a big metal box. The box did not work. More than one thousand people went to work on the problems caused by the leak. They worked in the days after the explosion. They kept working for weeks. They kept working for months. Small robotic submarines went down to the ocean floor. The submarines looked at the damage. They also tried to stop the leak. Crews used the small submarines to flip a switch. That switch was supposed to close the safety valve.

4Every attempt to stop the leak failed. As the first edge of the slick neared the shores of the Gulf Coast, people tried another plan. The plan was to build a large metal box and set it over the leaking well. Oil that gathered inside the box would be pumped into tanker ships. Those ships would carry the oil away. When the plan was tried, frozen crystals of natural gas blocked the box. Cold temperatures at the seafloor kept the box from working. High pressure at the seafloor did the same. A picture shows a Gulf of Mexico crew busy on a rig out at sea. Planners also decided to drill relief wells beside the leaking well. A relief well would meet the original well. Crews would then pump mud down the pipes. They might pump concrete instead. The goal was to plug the leak. The relief well met the first well at last on September 16. Mud and cement were pumped in to seal the well shut.

5On September 19, officials said the well was plugged from the bottom for good. The harm from an oil spill becomes clear when the oil nears the shore. The harm is even clearer when the oil reaches land. An oil spill can do tremendous damage to life in the sea. It can damage the environment. It can damage the economy. Birds can die in huge numbers when oil reaches the places where they hunt and live. Fish can die too. Other animals can die as well. Beaches can become contaminated with oil that is toxic. Wetlands can become contaminated in the same way. A wildlife refuge that shelters rare animals can be destroyed. Brown pelicans off the Louisiana coast are rare birds. The oil spill threatens them. Many towns on the coast depend on oyster beds for jobs. They depend on fishing. They depend on the shrimping industry. Other towns have beautiful beaches. They have fishing spots that draw tourists. Those places also draw people who come for recreation.

6Spilled oil can badly damage every one of these parts of the Gulf Coast economy. It can even destroy them. For families living where the spill was headed, the possible harm was enormous. Some estimates say the Deepwater Horizon spill did more than 5 billion dollars of damage to fishing and tourism. Shrimping boats that cannot work because of the oil help with the cleanup. The Development Driller III was the rig chosen to drill the relief well. It is similar to the Deepwater Horizon. Work on an oil rig always carries the risk of a leak. It always carries the risk of an explosion. A fire can cause an accident at any time. A mechanical failure can cause an accident. Severe weather, such as a hurricane, can cause an accident. Explosive natural gas often bubbles up from an oil well. If a cloud of that gas reaches the surface, one spark can set off a huge explosion.

7Oil can also leak from underwater pipes. Those pipes carry oil from wells at sea to refineries on land. Offshore wells have been part of large oil spills in the past. Oil tankers have been part of large spills too. Some of the biggest oil spills in the world began with shipwrecks. One of the most famous began when a tanker broke open off Alaska. Those coastal waters had been unspoiled. The ship lost over 10 million gallons of oil. The leak did major damage to the environment. Hundreds of thousands of animals died after touching the oil. People felt the effects of that spill for years. The worst oil spill of all was made on purpose. In 1991, soldiers from Iraq let oil run into the Persian Gulf. They hoped the oil would stop enemy soldiers from landing on their shores. As much as 520 million gallons of oil entered the water. The slick that formed covered 4,000 square miles. Cleaning an oil spill is difficult.

8It is also expensive. The owner of the well will pay billions of dollars to clean up after the Deepwater Horizon blast. Once oil is in the environment, there is no easy way to take it back out. Authorities used many methods against the damage. Absorption is one method. Special towels soak up oil that coats sand and other material along the shore. Burning is another method. Crews can burn the oil if the water stays fairly calm and the slick stays fairly thick. Fire takes oil off the surface. It also fills the air with harmful black smoke. Chemicals are another method. Planes fly over a spill and drop chemicals. The chemicals break the oil into tiny droplets. The droplets sink below the surface. Natural bacteria that live in the ocean then eat the droplets. Containment is another method. Booms are long tubes that float. Crews string the booms out to make a barrier. The barrier is meant to keep the oil from spreading.

9Microbes are another method. These tiny organisms eat oil. Crews spread them over the spill. The microbes eat the oil and digest it. Power washing is another method. Power sprayers blast oil off rocks and other objects that get coated when oil comes ashore. Skimming is another method. Ships with special gear skim oil that has collected inside floating booms. A diagram of this work shows tow ships and a skimmer. A picture shows President Obama talking about the spill on May 2, 2010. Pressure to seek fresh oil will remain while the world still runs on oil. Many people oppose drilling offshore. They also oppose using tanker ships to move oil across oceans. They say the risk of a spill or a leak is not a risk worth taking. Many other people argue a different point. They say growing needs for energy make it necessary to drill through the ocean floor. Scientists know that large pockets of oil lie under the ocean floor. Those pockets sit in many regions of the world.

10People who favor offshore drilling believe that proper safety precautions will cut the risks. The Deepwater Horizon accident shows something else. Other accidents like it show the same thing. We can never guarantee that a large oil spill will not happen. To absorb something is to soak it up or take it in. Contaminated means covered with harmful substances. Engulfed means surrounded completely. To guarantee is to promise or to make sure. An inferno is a blaze of fierce heat and great intensity. Marine describes something related to the sea, found in the sea, or living in the sea. To minimize is to lessen something as much as possible. An oil slick is a thin layer of oil floating on water. Precautions are actions taken to guard against events people do not want. Refineries are factories that purify raw materials. Toxic means poisonous and dangerous to life. Wetlands are areas of land that are marshy or swampy.

9. Forests Washed by Rain

1Soils in a rainforest are often shallow. Many tall trees there grow large supports that branch out. These supports are called buttress roots. They help the trees stay upright. Contents. A Place of Wonder. The Rainforest Biome. Tropical Rainforests. Climate and Soil. Plants. Animals. People in Rainforests. A Celebration of Abundance. Glossary. In Peru, scarlet macaws bring bright color to the sky. A Place of Wonder. Imagine trees so high they look as if they meet the sky. A tight net of leaves and limbs under those giant trees blocks light from the layers beneath. Birds squawk and flash brilliant colors. Monkeys hurry from one limb to the next. Their howls fill the air. Snakes slide up tree trunks without a sound. They hide and wait for prey. Far below, the forest floor is dark and damp. Small creatures hurry and crawl among the mushrooms. They move through dead leaves and fallen fruit. Larger animals leave cover to hunt once night arrives. Water is everywhere. It falls from the sky. It drips from leaves. It pools on the ground. It flows downhill into streams. Moisture hangs heavy in the air itself. Nature seems to honor water for the life it gives. It celebrates the abundant growth that water supports. This is a rainforest. It is a place of beauty and wonder that can astonish you. The Rainforest Biome. Different kinds of environments cover the surface of Earth. A biome is one of these regions, together with the plants and animals living in it.

2Deserts are biomes. So are oceans, grasslands, and tundra. Rainforests are biomes too. Many factors help create Earth's biomes. Two important ones are latitude and elevation. Latitude is how far a place is from the equator. Elevation is height above sea level. Both shape whether a spot is warm or cool. Climate, land, and water also help build each biome. So are plants and animals. What sets these forests apart is very high rainfall. No other biome receives as much rain. Plant and animal variety here is far beyond any other place on Earth. Antarctica is the only continent without a rainforest. There are two main types. Tropical rainforests hold over twenty thousand kinds of orchids. Flying frogs have webbed feet. Webbed feet let them glide between branches. Earth's Rainforest Regions. Tropical rainforests lie mainly in the tropical zones. The Tropic of Cancer bounds those zones on one side. The Tropic of Capricorn bounds them on the other. They are closest to the equator. They receive the most direct sunlight. Sunlight stays about equal across the whole year. The Amazon rainforest in South America is the largest tropical rainforest. It is the best known. Central America has more of them. Others are found in parts of Africa, in southern Asia, and in Australia. Tropical rainforests make up nearly the whole set. Temperate rainforests lie mainly along coasts. Cool regions of the temperate zone are the main home. Those regions sit farther from the equator. In North America, they stretch from Alaska to Oregon.

3Chile's coast has them as well. Norway has them, and so does the United Kingdom. They are found in parts of eastern Russia, in Japan, in Australia, and in New Zealand. Tropical Rainforests. Climate and Soil. Warmth and damp air last all year in a tropical rainforest. As a rule, average temperatures run from about 20 to 30 degrees Celsius. That range is 68 to 86 degrees Fahrenheit. Dampness in the air often stays at 80 percent or above for the whole year. A year brings at least 200 centimeters of rain, which is 80 inches. It can reach 1,000 centimeters, or 400 inches. Rain can be very heavy. As much as 5 centimeters, or 2 inches, can fall in an hour. Most tropical rainforests do not have a clear rainy season and a clear dry season. Rich soil is uncommon in a tropical rainforest. One reason is the plants themselves. The plants lock up so many nutrients that the soil keeps little. Rainforest soil is often waterlogged. Rain almost always arrives faster than water can turn to vapor. Rivers and streams carry the water as it runs down the slope. It carries nutrients with it. The Importance of Rainforests. During photosynthesis, plants give off oxygen as a waste product. The rate of photosynthesis in rainforests is very high. Because of that, this biome produces most of the oxygen on Earth. Humans depend on that oxygen to survive. Other animals do too. Rainforests also take in carbon dioxide.

4That keeps this gas from entering the atmosphere of Earth. It keeps the gas from adding to global warming. Enormous numbers of living things find a home in these forests. Help from these forests to the natural world does not stop at a short list. No biome has a higher biodiversity than the tropical rainforest. The portion of Earth's surface that they occupy is about 7 percent. Plant kinds of this planet have more than half their number in these forests. Animal kinds do too. Several factors lead to this result. Sunlight is almost constant. Temperatures stay warm. Those conditions support photosynthesis. Photosynthesis makes food for plants. It also feeds the animals that eat the plants, in a direct way or an indirect way. Water is another factor. Every living thing needs water, and here water is available. Tropical rainforests also offer many different kinds of food. They offer many kinds of shelter. Known plant species are more than two-thirds housed in this biome. Every year, scientists find animal species that are new to them. Lesulas live in Central Africa. Scientists did not know of them until 2007. Plants. Four layers of plants rise in a tropical rainforest. The emergent layer holds the tallest trees. Those trees can grow to more than 60 meters. That height is 200 feet. Standing over the other trees brings them the most sun. They get the most rain and the most wind. Heavy exposure to sunlight makes their water evaporate.

5These trees have adaptations for dry conditions. Their leaves are small. The leaves are waxy. The canopy is roughly 18 to 45 meters high. That range is 60 to 125 feet. Most plants and animals live there. Thick trees and vines cast shade on every layer under them. Most flowers grow in the canopy. Most fruit grows there too. The canopy also holds many epiphytes. People call them air plants. Other plants hold them up. Epiphytes get nutrients from rain. They get nutrients from humidity in the air. They do not get them from soil. A tree is a common perch, because sunlight is easier to reach up there. A diagram of tropical rainforest layers labels the top of the emergent layer. It labels the top of the canopy. It labels the top of the understory. It labels the forest floor. The understory gets much less sunlight than the higher layers. Sunlight arriving there is scarce. The low share is about 2 percent. The high share is about 15 percent. Plants here have adaptations for shade. They have large, wide leaves. The leaves capture sunlight for photosynthesis. The understory also holds young trees. Many climbing plants grow there as they search for sunlight. Mosses often grow on understory plants. Algae and fungi often grow there too. Down at ground level, the forest floor stays dark and damp. It receives only about 2 percent of the sunlight. Almost no sunlight means most plants cannot grow in this layer. The floor holds leaves that are decomposing.

6It holds fallen trees, fruit, and branches. Food chains in the rainforest lean on the rotting of dead matter. Mosses are common here. So are ferns, ginger, mushrooms, and fungi. Animals. Each tropical rainforest has animals that differ a great deal from the next. Certain adaptations are still common. Many animals are adapted for life in the trees. Those adaptations include flying, climbing, leaping, and gliding. Loud calls are a common way to communicate. Dense trees block the view. Many birds use bright colors to find each other. Other rainforest animals do not want to be spotted. Camouflage lets them hide from animals that hunt them. Because animals are so abundant, competition for food is widespread. Some animals handle this with a specialized diet. Some have specialized body parts. A toucan's bill is huge and long. The bill lets the bird reach fruit. The fruit grows on branches too thin to hold the bird's weight. Night is when many of these animals move about. This adaptation helps them avoid the heat of the day. It also lowers the competition for food. Grasping and Gliding. Some animals of the canopy have special adaptations. The adaptations help them cross large spaces between branches. A tail that can grasp is common, above all in Central and South America. That tail is called prehensile. The tail works much as an extra arm or leg would. Spider monkeys rely on that tail while they hurry from limb to limb. Howler monkeys use it too.

7They are high above the ground. In Asia, some animals glide across gaps in the canopy. Flying squirrels have this adaptation. Flying geckos have it. Several other animals have it too. They do not actually fly. They soar from a higher branch to a lower one. Skin flaps around the body catch the air. The flaps work somewhat like a parachute. At night, a picture shows a flying squirrel sailing from tree to tree. Algae grow in a sloth's fur. That growth helps the animal blend with green plants. Bats live in the emergent layer. Butterflies live in that top layer. Small monkeys live there. Eagles live there. Other birds live there. More animals live in the canopy than in any other layer. Some of them spend their whole life there. Sloths live in Central and South America. Toucans live there too. So do howler monkeys and spider monkeys. Orangutans live in Southeast Asia. Australia is home to sugar gliders, and so are parts of Indonesia. Parrots live in the canopy of every rainforest. Frogs, salamanders, snakes, and lizards live in the understory. Birds and insects live there too. Animals that stay on the ground keep to the forest floor. Tapirs live in the Amazon. So do deer, peccaries, boars, and capybaras. Elephants live in the rainforests of Asia. Tigers live there too. African rainforests have elephants as well. Gorillas live there. Pygmy hippos live there too. Every rainforest except those in Australia has some kind of cat.

8Those cats include tigers, jaguars, or leopards. Every rainforest except those in the Americas has crocodiles. Rainforests around the world have a great abundance of insects. They have spiders and earthworms too. Temperate Rainforests. Temperate rainforests get less rain than tropical rainforests. They still get quite a lot. Yearly rain is 150 to 500 centimeters, or 60 to 200 inches. Coastal fog adds 18 to 30 centimeters more water each year. That added amount is 7 to 12 inches. Mild winters are typical. Cool summers are typical. Rain falls through the year. Summers tend to be drier. Plants grow in three layers, not four. There is no emergent layer. The tallest trees in the canopy grow to roughly 90 meters. That height is 300 feet. Tree species are far fewer here than in a tropical rainforest. They have far fewer other plants too. Needle-leaf evergreens are a common canopy tree in the Northern Hemisphere. Mosses grow in the understory. Deciduous shrubs grow there too. Those shrubs drop their leaves in winter. Broadleaf evergreens fill the canopy in the Southern Hemisphere. The understory holds shrubs and trees that stay green. Shrubs and trees that drop their leaves grow there as well. Ferns and mosses are about the only epiphytes in these forests. Many animals in temperate rainforests live on the forest floor. Rain and wind reach them less in that spot. The animals include tree frogs, squirrels, and raccoons. They include deer, black bears, and bobcats. Wolves are among them, along with mountain lions and many insects and spiders.

9Northern California's temperate rainforest grows redwood trees and ferns. A redwood may last as long as two thousand years. The Huli people live in the rainforests of Papua New Guinea. Headgear and painted faces are what they are known for. About ninety thousand Huli live today. People in Rainforests. For thousands of years, Native peoples have made these forests home. About fifty million of them are estimated to live there still. European settlers caused changes. Most traditional ways of life felt a deep effect from those changes. Some groups no longer exist. The largest number of Native peoples who remain live in the Amazon rainforest. Many people have come into rainforests in recent years. They have cleared the land of trees. They start small farms and ranches. Logging has cleared wide stretches of these forests as well. Mining has done that too. Modern life draws a huge store of resources from tropical rainforests. Those resources include food, spices, and wood. They include medicines and minerals. Tourism in rainforests is very popular, especially in the Amazon. The reason is the diversity of wildlife. A Celebration of Abundance. Living things turn up in almost every part of Earth. Antarctica stands at one end of the range. The Sahara desert stands at another end. It also runs from the floor of the ocean up to Mount Everest. Conditions in many places still make life hard. Cold can last through much of the year in those places.

10They may be cold all year. They may lack shelter. They may lack a steady supply of water. Rainforests are different. Nothing is lacking. Water, warmth, and shelter are so plentiful that life breaks out in abundance. To sense the biodiversity of this biome, think about ants. One bush in the Amazon rainforest holds more kinds of ants than the whole United Kingdom. Rainforests are among the most precious jewels on Earth. In that biome, nature shows its full glory. Misty tropical rainforest in Borneo shows through the mouth of a cave. Southeast Asia is where this large island, Borneo, lies. Glossary. abundant (adj.): present in large amounts. adaptations (n.): changes in an organism, or in a species, that let it survive better in its environment. camouflage (n.): colors, patterns, or shapes on an animal's body that let it blend into the background or stay hidden. canopy (n.): the leafy roof made where the crowns of the trees crowd together. decomposing (adj.): rotting, or decaying. dense (adj.): packed close, with parts crowded together. Diversity means many different things gathered in one wide mix. evaporates (v.): changes from a liquid into a gas, usually because of heat. humid (adj.): full of water vapor in the air. Nutrients are substances a living body must have. They keep life going. They support health. They support growth. temperate (adj.): of a climate or a region with moderate, or mild, temperatures. tropical (adj.): of the hot, damp region that circles the equator.

10. From Sea to Sea: The Country of Canada

1Contents. Where Is It? People. Land. Animals. History. Celebrations. Conclusion. Glossary. Where Is It? Canada is the country farthest north on the mainland of North America. Only one country on Earth covers more land. A very large country often shares borders with several others. Canada has only one. That neighbor is the United States. About 8,893 kilometers is the length of the line between them. That length is 5,525 miles. No border between two countries anywhere is longer. The national motto has four words. The words are From sea to sea. On the west, the land meets the Pacific Ocean. The eastern edge meets the Atlantic Ocean. The Arctic Ocean lies to the north. Three oceans frame the country on a map. West meets one sea. East meets another. North meets a third. The motto fits that east-west reach. A single neighbor shares the long southern line. That shared line is remarkable because of its length. The coastline of Canada is 202,080 kilometers long. That length is 125,566 miles. It wraps the huge mainland. It also wraps more than 52,000 islands. It is by far the longest national coastline in the world. Laid out in a line, that shore could go around the globe five times. Ten provinces form part of Canada. Three territories form the rest. Each one has its own legislature. Voters choose those lawmakers in a democratic way.

2Canadians also elect people to send to Parliament. Parliament meets in Ottawa, the capital of the country. Ottawa has a lot to see. Begin at the Peace Tower. Stand on its observation deck. The tower is 92 meters tall, or 302 feet. Visitors looking out can see boats on the Ottawa River. They can see people hiking in the many parks of the city. In winter they can see people skating on ice on their way to work. They skate on the Rideau Canal, the largest skating area in the world. The city also has 267 other outdoor rinks. From the tower, visitors can view the grand buildings of Parliament. They can view museums, hotels, and theaters. They can view signs and advertisements in Arabic, in Spanish, and in Chinese. A picture shows people from Ottawa skating on the Rideau Canal. The canal is 7.8 kilometers long, or 4.8 miles. The Peace Tower stands in the background. People. One person in every five was born in another country, so Canada is diverse. Most Canadians do not think of the country as a melting pot. In a melting pot, different cultures blend into one. Many people prefer another picture. They see Canada as a mosaic. In a mosaic, immigrants keep their own culture. They make the nation stronger through that diversity.

3France sent some of the earliest European settlers into the land now called Canada. Others came from England. These settlers built separate colonies. Canada was not a country yet. Those colonies often fought one another. The groups had different languages. They had different religions. In time, they learned to get along. Because of that history, Canada has two official languages. They are French and English. The nation of Canada grew with help from France and from Britain. The new nation rose on land Indigenous peoples already held as ancestral home. Their life in that area already covered thousands of years. Indigenous peoples form under five percent of the population today. Most belong to three groups. One group is First Nations. One group is the Inuit. One group is the Métis. First Nations people live throughout the country. Métis people do too. The Inuit still live in the far north. Years have brought change to life in Indigenous communities. Many who live in them still keep up their traditional culture. Teaching their own history is one example. They learn to speak Indigenous languages. A picture shows Inuit children playing at school. Germany and other European countries sent many immigrants as the years passed. Others came from China, from India, from the Americas, and from the Middle East. The country is richly diverse.

4It is also thinly populated. Most of the people live within 160 kilometers of the southern border. That distance is 100 miles. Some estimates put that share at 90 percent. Better farmland and a way to reach waterways are the likely reasons. Many Canadians in cities work in service industries. Those jobs include tourism, transportation, and banking. Outside the cities, some workers draw resources out of the land. Those resources include oil, copper, and gold. Other people farm the land. Other workers cut timber. Vast forests cover the country and supply that wood. China is one distant buyer. Both resources and factory goods travel that far. The United States, just across the border, buys most of them. Regions of Canada. Land. Canada holds grand mountains. It holds vast plains, lakes, and forests that seem to go on without end. Ice and snow stay all year in parts of the north. Seven landform regions are the geographers' division of the country. The Appalachian region lies in the east. It has rolling hills covered in forest, with farms dotted among them. Waterways give shape to another region. Its name joins the Great Lakes with the St. Lawrence lowlands. It is the smallest region. It is an important one. People call it Canada's heartland. Large cities in this region hold about half of Canada's people. The Canadian Shield is a huge forested region.

5It wraps around Hudson Bay. Long ago, glaciers scraped the soil away. They left the rocky surface bare. Thousands of lakes appeared once the glaciers turned to water. Inside this wet region is a cold, marshy area. It is called the Hudson Bay lowlands. A picture is set in parks of the Cordillera region. Tourists camp there. They hike. They canoe. Grass covers the southern part of the interior plains. They are forests in the north. Today the grasslands are used to raise livestock. They are used to grow grains. The Cordillera region stands in the far west. Great chains of mountains stand there. The Rocky Mountains are the highest of those chains. Snow-covered peaks reach 3,954 meters, or 13,000 feet. Fertile valleys lie in the gaps between the mountains. Farmers there grow fruit and other crops. The Arctic tundra lies past these regions and past their forests. Freezing temperatures keep any trees from growing. Niagara Falls. In the Great Lakes-St. Lawrence lowlands, water drops 57 meters at Niagara Falls. That drop is 188 feet. The Canadian part of the falls is Horseshoe Falls. It is 670 meters long, or 2,200 feet. A picture shows a harp seal pup. Animals. White is the usual color of animals in this icy, snowy Arctic land. That color is camouflage.

6It helps them survive. Polar bears have black skin. Their fur looks as white as snow. White coats make harp seal pups hard to spot. Winter turns the coat of the arctic fox white. When the snow melts, it sheds that coat for a brown one. Beluga whales are white. The tundra swan is one of the white birds. South of the Arctic, forest animals share the colors of the ground beneath the trees. Moose are among them. Beaver are among them. Black bears are among them. Wolves are among them. On the grassy plains, small animals dig tunnels under the ground. They live in the tunnels and raise their young there. Ground squirrels do this. Pocket gophers do this too. In the mountains, the best way to survive is to be sure of your footing. Goats and bighorn sheep in the Rocky Mountains have hooves fit for running. The hooves are also made for jumping and for climbing rocks. All around Canada, animals survive the cold winter and the lack of food by migrating. Many birds migrate. So do some fish, some whales, and some turtles. Grazing animals migrate too. Even dragonflies migrate. Grazing caribou travel hundreds of miles in huge herds. One region of Canada is the start. Another region of Canada is the finish. Other animals go farther south. They leave Canada for a warmer climate.

7The champions of long-distance migration are the arctic terns. They are small, but they are swift. Their path runs from Arctic breeding grounds all the way to Antarctica. The trip takes forty days. It covers some 35,500 kilometers, or 22,060 miles. Half a year after that, the whole path is flown in reverse. Canadian Wildlife. Countless species share this huge homeland with the people of Canada. Those species include birds, fish, mammals, and plants. The country works to guard its land. It works to guard its air. It works to guard its water. The protection is for people. It is also for the wildlife that lives there. Pictures show a Rocky Mountain goat, an arctic tern, a caribou, and a humpback whale. History. First Nations people had a home for many thousands of years. That home is the land called Canada today. They governed themselves. It was home to the Inuit as well. Icelandic Vikings were the earliest Europeans to reach this land. Their ships set out from Greenland. More than one thousand years have passed since that voyage. Survival was hard. In time, they left. John Cabot was an explorer from Britain. The year 1497 brought him to the east of today's Canada. He claimed land for England. Later, French explorers claimed lands too. The French and the English found two resources.

8Those resources helped them make money back in Europe. The resources were codfish and fur. More traders came. Colonies grew. Alliances tied the foreign nations to the First Nations people. Beaver pelts were sold through a network they set up. France and England fought often between the 1400s and the 1700s. Their colonies were at war too. Canadian lands passed from the winner to the next winner. In the end, Great Britain kept control. As the colonies grew, they pushed First Nations people aside. They took over their lands. The growing colonies began to want more independence. Britain gave the colonies the standing of a dominion in 1867. The capital of its democratic government was in Ottawa. The year 1931 brought Canada full independence. Membership in the British Commonwealth of Nations came with that step. Pictures show Canada Day, the Calgary Stampede, and the Quebec Winter Carnival. Celebrations. Canada Day is the birthday of the nation. It falls on July 1. People celebrate with parades. They celebrate with barbecues and fireworks. Ottawa hosts the biggest of these celebrations. The Calgary Stampede celebrates the Old West of Canada. It has bull riding and barrel racing. It has steer wrestling and bronc riding. It even has chuck wagon racing. A famous boast calls this fair Earth's greatest show staged outdoors.

9It draws about a million visitors. Canadians have winter festivals too. Dogsled races are part of those winter festivals. Skiing is part of them. Ice-skating is part of them. No festival of that kind anywhere is larger than the Quebec Winter Carnival. There you can enjoy a contest of snow sculptures. You can visit a castle made of ice. A hotel built of ice and snow can even take guests. The hotel is built every winter. It melts away every summer. Conclusion. Few countries on Earth spread across more ground than Canada. It includes regions of towering mountains. It includes rolling plains and dense forests. It includes frozen tundra and rocky shores that seem to have no end. Plants differ in every region. Each region has its own animals, resources, and people. French may be the language of the people who live there. They may speak English. They may speak one of many other languages. They are all Canadian. They are proud of their cultural mosaic. Ice hockey began in Canada. It is the national sport of the country. A picture on the left shows the game. A top picture shows a public gathering. The city is Ottawa. A bottom picture shows children going to practice. They are from Nunavik. Nunavik is an Inuit village. They are going to hockey practice. Population: 38,000,000. Land area is 9,984,670 square kilometers.

10The same area is 3,855,103 square miles. Capital city: Ottawa. Official languages: English and French. Currency: the Canadian dollar. The Central Intelligence Agency in 2021 and the World Bank are the sources of these facts. Glossary. camouflage (n.): colors, patterns, or shapes on an animal's body that let it blend into the background or stay hidden. The Inuit are an Indigenous people. They live in the Arctic portion of North America. Métis (n.): a group of people whose ancestors include both First Nations people and Europeans. commonwealth (n.): states or countries joined in a shared association. To migrate is to move habitat. A move from one region to another counts as well. The timing is set, and it returns each year. democratically (adv.): in a way that gives people an equal vote or an equal part. provinces (n.): the largest areas into which some countries are divided. diverse (adj.): different from one another, or made up of different people or things. traditional (adj.): of a custom that has lasted a very long time. dominion (n.): land, or an area, that a government or a ruler owns. tundra (n.): a cold Arctic region with no trees, where the ground stays frozen. First Nations names the peoples living in today's Canada before any European arrival. Their descendants share that name.

11. Gatherings for Comic Fans

1This book welcomes you to a comic con. It tells how the gatherings began and how comics grew as an art. It compares big cons and small ones. It looks at costumes, famous guests, and the exhibit hall. It faces growing pains and new ideas. A word list closes the book. A picture from the 2014 New York Comic Con shows three characters together. Robin, the Joker, and Batman spend time side by side. You are packed in an auditorium with thousands of other fans. You wait for the stars to arrive. Your Chewbacca costume feels warm. You grow impatient. Suddenly the audience is on its feet. The crowd is cheering. You jump up to see. Robert Downey Jr. walks out onstage. He is playing the Iron Man himself. The Avengers panel has begun. This is the world of comic cons. The scene is colorful. It is crowded. It is built for fans of pop culture. A comic con has something for you if comics, movies, or video games are your interest. You may collect comic books. You may watch movies. You may play video games. These fan conventions happen all over the country. People call them cons for short. Shel Dorf helped start comic book conventions. He was a comic book artist. He was one of the first people to organize them.

2Dorf organized small gatherings of comic fans in Detroit. The gatherings were in the 1960s. He went on to found a larger convention in 1970. People now know it as Comic-Con International: San Diego. People also call it CCI. The first San Diego convention attracted about 100 fans. It now hosts over 130,000 people every year. Hundreds of other comic cons have sprung up across the country. They have sprung up across the world. Comic cons now take place on every continent except Antarctica. Months before the doors open, tickets for these events are gone. Some fans camp at ticket booths for days. They want to be sure they get in. A picture shows legendary writer Stan Lee. He was also an editor and a publisher. He often appeared at comic cons. Lee helped create many of the most popular superheroes at Marvel Comics. Those heroes include the Fantastic Four. They include the Incredible Hulk. They include Thor. They include Spider-Man. They include the X-Men. He died in 2018. Another picture shows college students. They browse comic books at a 2015 book fair. What has caused this explosive growth? One cause is a rise in comic-book popularity in recent years. Comic books had many readers during the Golden Age.

3That age was the 1930s and 1940s. For several decades after that age, far fewer people read them. In those years, many people treated comics as something only for kids. But a change came in the late 1980s and early 1990s. Comic book series such as The Sandman broke new ground. Watchmen broke new ground too. Critics praised them as serious works of art. Since then, interest in comic books has soared. Writers and artists continue to explore new topics. They continue to explore new styles. A modern comic might tell a traditional superhero story. It might tell a story about war. It might tell a story about family drama. Young adults like the comic books known as graphic novels. Graphic memoirs are another kind of comic book. They often deal with serious themes. For example, Art Spiegelman won a Pulitzer Prize for his graphic memoir Maus. A Pulitzer Prize is a highly respected achievement. His work was based on interviews with his father. His father was a Holocaust survivor. As topics for comic books have expanded, audiences have expanded too. Fans of every color can find something to relate to in comic books. Fans of every age can. Fans of every gender can. Comic cons are booming because of that wider interest.

4Second, recent movies have had enormous success. The movies are based on comic books. They come from Hollywood. They have brought many people to comic cons. The Dark Knight came out in 2008. Wonder Woman came out in 2017. Black Panther came out in 2018. Those movies attracted many new fans. A big part of comic cons now belongs to movies. Famous actors from comic-book movies often appear on panels. Writers appear. Directors appear. They draw huge crowds. A note says Spider-Man first came to the big screen in 2002. The film was a blockbuster. A picture shows artists at work in 2017. They draw manga-style illustrations. The place is the Libya Comic Convention in Tripoli. Movies are just the start. Comic cons around the world also celebrate video games. They celebrate novels. They celebrate television shows. Some cons now focus on just one art form because the audience is so large. For example, many fans attend cons for anime and manga. Those are animated films and comic books from Japan. There are cons for fans of classic comics. There are cons for collectors of classic comics. There are cons for fans of sci-fi. There are cons for fans of fantasy fiction. There are even cons for fans of steampunk. There are cons for horror.

5There are cons for humor. Many other kinds exist too. Comic cons happen in cities all over the world. Huge conventions fill San Diego and New York. Outside those two, the crowds are smaller. The fans are just as enthusiastic. Check a local newspaper to learn if a comic con is coming near you. You can also check online. You might be surprised at what you will find. Local fans mix with big-name cartoonists at many smaller cons. They mix with movie stars too. If you go, you just might see the star of your favorite show. You might see your favorite writer. You are also likely to meet local artists. You are likely to make new friends. A picture shows a brother and sister at the 2019 Edinburgh Comic Con. Each rides a dragon. The dragons are Night Fury and Light Fury. Another picture shows a battle at the 2016 MCM Comic Con in London. Captain America and Loki face each other. Many fans display their own creativity at comic cons. Some do this through cosplay. In cosplay, fans dress up in costumes. The characters come from comic books. They come from anime. They come from manga. They come from other fantasy genres. For fans worldwide, cosplay is serious business. Costumes can be bought.

6They can be custom-built. They can be handmade. Many cosplayers make realistic props. The props go with their characters. Some cosplayers act like their favorite characters. They do this while they are in costume. Costumes can be simple. They can be incredibly detailed. Figuring out how to make a costume is a big part of the fun. Building one is a big part of the fun too. Cosplay is a major part of comic cons. Hundreds of costumed fans fill the audience. Many conventions also hold cosplay contests. Cosplayers can pose for photos. They can act in skits based on their characters. At comic cons, fans can also meet creators in person. Stars often take part in panels. They speak about the experience of making comic books. They speak about films. They speak about shows. Audience members can sometimes ask questions during panels. They can have their photos taken with celebrities. They can get autographs. Many fans treasure these personal meetings as memories. A picture shows two actors at CCI: San Diego. They are Isaac Hempstead-Wright and Elle Fanning. They appear to promote their animated film. The film is The Boxtrolls. Another picture shows a giant room. Attendees flow through the exhibit hall at the New York Comic Con. The con is in New York City.

7The center of most comic cons is the exhibit hall. Fans can buy comic books here. They can trade comic books here. They can find action figures. They can find toys. They can find other merchandise. Rare editions of comic books are there for experienced collectors. Someone new to collecting can get an early start. There is often an author signing area in the exhibit hall. Fans can meet artists there. They can meet writers. Even if you are not looking to buy anything, there is a lot to see. Cosplayers wander by in colorful costumes. Movies are shown regularly. Movie trailers are shown regularly. Celebrity sightings are always possible. There is an endless supply of colorful merchandise to browse through. The popularity of comic cons has come with some controversy. Some comic book fans say the bigger cons have grown too large. In their view, comic books are no longer the main focus. Many comic cons could be described in a wider way. They look like pop culture conventions. They look less like comic book conventions alone. The audience for comic books has changed as well. The audience for comic cons has changed too. Comic con attendees used to be mostly male. About forty percent of recent CCI: San Diego attendees are female.

8At recent cons, more than half of the attendees are under twenty-nine. The increased diversity of the comic con audience brings larger crowds. For most fans, that change is a welcome development. Larger controversies can be discussed at comic cons too. Comic books have made a recent comeback. Authors have taken on serious topics. They write about race. They write about gender. They write about politics. They write about war. Not everyone likes these comics. Some comics can even become the subjects of banned-book campaigns. In those campaigns, a library is asked to take a book off the shelves. Spider-Man comics have been challenged in some places. Batman comics have been challenged too. The reason given is adult themes. Readers often discuss censorship on panels at comic cons. Teachers discuss it. Librarians discuss it. A picture shows stars from the film Black Panther. They attend a comic con panel in 2017. One result of these talks has been new labels. People use graphic novel for some comics. They use graphic memoir for others. The labels describe comics that are meant for adults. Some comics are still aimed at children. Comics as a whole are no longer kids only. Some comics are fictional. Some are not. Some are educational. Some are meant for entertainment.

9Some deal with adult themes. Others provide an escape into fantasy worlds. Comics are diverse. A question follows from that diversity. Should all comics be shelved together in libraries and schools? Another question asks what role comics have in our society. Panels at comic cons take up these questions often. Keeping up with the variety of comic books can be challenging. Overall, it is an exciting time for comic cons. The huge range of comics available today has changed the audience. Audiences for comics are bigger. They are wider. Audiences for conventions are bigger and wider too. Fans of every age can find themselves represented in a comic somewhere. Fans of every color can. Fans of every gender can. Heroes might look like Superman. A hero might be a young girl from Botswana. A hero might be a reporter on the ground in Bosnia. There is no limit to what can happen in a comic book. There is no limit to who can be its heroes. People are drawn to comic cons by that limitless range. The cons create worlds of change. The stories that inspire the cons do too. In those worlds, ordinary people can be transformed into heroes. Lots of people come back to that experience again and again. A picture shows a young girl at CCI: San Diego.

10She shows off a pink stormtrooper costume from Star Wars. These words help a reader follow the cons. An exhibit hall is a large room. It is used to display one or more objects to many people. Autographs are signatures. They are especially the signatures of famous people. Censorship is leaving out all or part of a work. It can also mean suppressing that work. The work may be a publication, a play, or a film. People do this when they consider the content offensive or threatening. Memoirs are written retellings of people's experiences. Merchandise means items that are bought or sold. A controversy is a disagreement over an idea or an issue. A panel is a group of people invited to discuss a subject. They discuss it knowledgeably. They do it in front of an audience. Conventions are large gatherings. They can last for several days. People discuss their work there. Or they discuss a shared interest. Pop culture means popular ideas shared through mass media. Those ideas are part of the mainstream experience of modern societies. Critics are people who find fault. They also carefully judge work for review. Themes are the main ideas of artistic works. Diversity is a wide variety of many things.

12. George the Third of Britain

1A map shows England, Ireland, and Scotland. Contents. A Historic Meeting. The Making of a King. Two Officials Clash. A Royal Family. The Colonies. America Fights Back. Illness and Revolution. A New King George. A Complex Portrait. Glossary. A Historic Meeting. In 1785, John Adams went to England. He was one of the Founding Fathers of America. His purpose was a meeting with King George the Third. Adams stood in front of the king. The king watched him with a doubtful look. Only nine years before, the United States had announced that it was free of Great Britain. Ties between the two countries were still uneasy. Adams made a humble offer of friendship. He talked about honor between the nations. He talked about warm feeling as well. He wished the king good health. He wished the king a happy life. The king gave a smile. He said that he took the offer. The short visit then came to an end. A picture shows John Adams on the left side. He is from the United States. He holds out an offering. King George the Third stands on the right. The king is the ruler of England. The scene is set in 1785. Great Britain was the country this king ruled. He loved that land. Britain had suffered when the American colonies were lost. George was set on a goal.

2He promised to guide his nation back to a high place. The Making of a King. On June 4, 1738, Princess Augusta of Wales had a son. The baby was George. His grandfather was King George the Second. His father was Frederick, the Prince of Wales. The king and Frederick were not on good terms. They held different political views. Those views brought strain into the family. Young George grew up inside that tense home. He was a sensitive child. He was emotional too. Schoolwork was hard for him. His parents still hired excellent teachers. He learned to read only when he turned eleven. A year after that, his father died. A lung illness was the cause. George was then next in line for the British throne. Lord Bute, his teacher, was the person he turned to. He would need that help soon. Portraits show the young prince on the left. Lord Bute is on the right. Lord Bute taught the future king how government works. He taught him about the wider world as well. A map shows British land in North America in 1763. Land Britain already held, before the war with the French and the Indians, is shown in purple. Red marks land gained after that war. Labels name the Great Lakes. They name the Thirteen Colonies. They name the Mississippi River, the Ohio River, and the Missouri River.

3They also name the Atlantic Ocean. In the middle of the 1700s, Great Britain held power in many parts of the world. Colonies in India were markets for British merchants. Colonies in North America were markets too. Merchants sold goods in those places. The British monarchy led the country. Parliament often came into conflict with the king. In 1760, George the Second died. The new king was only twenty-two. He took the name King George the Third. Britain was then in the middle of the Seven Years' War. The fight was for control of land. France and other European countries were the opponents. Fighting also happened in North America. People there called it the French and Indian War. Britain won. It took many territories that had been French. The country became bigger than it had ever been. Two Officials Clash. King George the Third was thrilled by the wins in the Seven Years' War. The British public gave him little praise. The praise went to William Pitt. Pitt served as war minister to the king. Lord Bute counted him as an enemy. Lord Bute was the king's mentor. Pitt and Bute argued as the war neared its close. Pitt wanted the fighting to spread into Spain. Bute did not agree. The king did not agree either.

4The quarrel grew so hot that Pitt left his post in the ministry. The king then made Bute prime minister. That job was the top post in the ministry. Bute closed the Seven Years' War in a formal way. A treaty was signed in 1763. Soon after that, he stepped down. William Pitt had been the main force behind Britain's win. Lord Bute still stood against him. He pressed George the Third to stand against Pitt too. The king picked Sophia Charlotte to be his wife. He had never met her. She came to England. She met the king. They were married on the next day. A Royal Family. George the Third had lost his closest advisor in politics. What he gained was a partner for all the years ahead. In 1761 he married Sophia Charlotte, a princess from Germany. The marriage went on for more than fifty years. It was a long success. The couple had fifteen children. Home life made George happy. He was set on being a strong leader. He studied the British Parliament with real effort. He wanted to see how it might be made better. He also kept a sharp eye on events around the world. A Simple Man. George held the rank of king. He still liked plain pleasures. Agriculture was a love of his. He spent many days looking over farms in his kingdom.

5Horseback riding was a favorite activity. Music was another. He liked going to the theater. He liked card games too. The Colonies. The American Revolution was the hardest test King George the Third faced. It grew out of a run of acts from Parliament. Those acts made the American colonists furious. Goods the colonists used were taxed by Britain. The Sugar Act put a tax on molasses. The Stamp Act put a tax on every piece of printed paper. The Townshend Acts put taxes on paint. They also taxed glass. They also taxed tea. The colonists were against the taxes. They started boycotts of goods from Britain. A group of colonists acted after dark on December 16, 1773. They dressed in the manner of Native Americans. They went onto a British ship. The ship sat in Boston Harbor. Crates of tea were on board. The colonists threw the crates into the water. The king's reply to the Boston Tea Party was a hard line. He said the colonies must win outright or give in. The Boston Tea Party brought the colonists together. They felt pressed down by the British taxes. America Fights Back. The American Revolution started in 1775. Shots rang out at Lexington and at Concord. Those fights were in Massachusetts. King George saw that he needed more soldiers.

6He wanted enough force to carry Britain to a win. He hired about thirty thousand soldiers from Germany. People called them Hessians. With the Hessians beside them, the king's troops beat the colonists in some early fights. A surprise strike late in 1776 was led by General George Washington. The British had to fall back. In 1778 a turning point came. France joined the American side. Those allies stood with the Americans. The war was won in 1781. King George feared the blame would fall on him. He said he was not guilty of the harms that had taken place. In 1776 some colonists declared that they were free of Britain. Some of them pulled down a statue of this king in New York City. At times the illness of King George the Third set off violent bursts. He had to be held back. Illness and Revolution. In the late 1780s, people around the king saw that his health was failing. This came after the colonies were lost. He seemed nervous. He said odd things. He spoke to people who were not there. He could turn violent. He shouted. He pushed people. Those were signs in his feelings and his mind. His body grew weak as well. His family was deeply worried. His ministers were worried too. They asked doctors for help.

7The doctors used care that was common in that time. They gave the king medicine to drink. They opened his veins and let blood out. They also set a strict plan of discipline. If he acted in a strange way, they punished him hard. The care seemed to help. Before long he was back at his work. A political cartoon is named The Rival Gardeners. It shows George the Third tending his own garden of lands. It shows Napoleon tending lands of his own. Around the same time, a revolution in Ireland demanded the king's notice. Religion was at the center of the fight. Ireland was a Catholic country. Parliament in Great Britain had passed laws that cut the rights of Catholics who lived there. The British army put down a rebellion. After that, in 1800, Parliament in Britain joined with Parliament in Ireland. The joined parliaments made one state. Its name was the United Kingdom. Great Britain and Ireland were both inside it. The king's trials were not finished. In the early 1800s, Britain was at war with France once more. A young general came to power in France after the revolution of 1789. His name was Napoleon Bonaparte. He wanted to take lands across Europe. Great Britain was one of those lands. England had to protect itself. Those wars, called the Napoleonic Wars, were over in 1815.

8Britain and its allies beat the old rival once again. A New King George. In 1809, Britain marked fifty years of George the Third on the throne. The reign had started in a rough way. Even so, the king stood at a new peak of public favor. Most subjects believed his guidance had been sound across those fifty years. Thousands of them cheered him. The next year brought a terrible loss. His youngest daughter died without warning. Tuberculosis was the cause. She was twenty-seven. The king was stunned. His heart was broken. Soon the old signs of illness came back. His body dropped into a steep fall. His mind dropped as well. By 1811 it was plain that he could not carry out his duties. Parliament named his oldest son, also called George, as regent. A regent is a ruler who serves for a time. In January 1820, George the Third died at last. The younger George rose to the throne. His father had risen in the same way many years before. George the Fourth was king from 1820 to 1830. A Complex Portrait. The story left by King George shows a leader who was hard to sum up. He was complicated. He was also strong. For some people, he is the ruler who allowed the American colonies to win their freedom.

9Other people remember him as the ruler who lost his mind. Many historians have started to look at him in a new way. Some of them think the signs of his mental illness tie to a disease of the body. As a leader, he put his country ahead of himself every time. He pulled the nation together. He led it to wins in the Seven Years' War. He led it to wins in the Napoleonic Wars. He also fought hard so Britain would keep its colonies in America. His life at home has been studied with more care. The record shows a man who worked hard. He was a faithful husband. He was a father who loved his children. His reign lasted sixty years. No ruler before him had held the crown for so long. The Life of George the Third. 1738: On June 4, George is born. 1751: Frederick, Prince of Wales, dies. He was the father of George. 1756: The Seven Years' War starts. 1760: King George the Second dies. He was the grandfather of George. George becomes king. 1763: The Seven Years' War comes to an end. 1775: Soldiers fire the opening shots of the American Revolution. 1781: At Yorktown, British forces give up to the American colonists. 1788: George the Third shows signs of a grave mental illness.

10A year later he takes up his duties again. He seems to be well. 1798: The Irish Rebellion is stopped by Britain. 1809: The king marks fifty years on the throne. 1815: The British beat Napoleon. 1820: Thousands come to the funeral of George the Third. Glossary. allies (n.): people or groups that band together for one shared goal. minister (n.): a person who holds an office in the government. monarchy (n.): rule by one person who was not chosen by a vote, such as a king or a queen. boycotts (n.): choices to stop buying something, or to stay out of it, so that a point is made or a change is forced.
