Parliament (n.): the group that makes laws for the United Kingdom. decline (n.): a continuing fall in how much there is, how good it is, what it is worth, or how strong it is. symptoms (n.): clear signs that show an illness or a hurt. legacy (n.): what is passed from an earlier time into the time we live in. treaty (n.): a written peace, or a written friendship, agreed by two nations or two groups. levied (v.): took in something, such as a tax, under the power of a government. tuberculosis (n.): a grave illness that attacks the lungs. mentor (n.): a skilled teacher who also gives advice.

13. Giant Snakes Loose in the Everglades

1A map shows where Burmese pythons now live loose in Florida. It marks the north of the state. It marks places where a single python was reported. It marks places where a python population is established, based on hundreds of reports. It marks Everglades National Park. Burmese pythons were first reported in that park in 1979. Now they are established inside the park. They are also established in the larger Everglades ecosystem. Scientists slog through ankle-deep water in the Florida Everglades as they approach their target. Without warning, one teammate leaps and seizes the tail of an enormous snake. A second researcher dives in and catches the snake just behind the head. Others quickly join the wrestling match. The struggle is between humans and a Burmese python. The snake is 16 feet long, which is 4.9 meters. It weighs 150 pounds, which is 68 kilograms. At last the python tires. The team hauls it back to the lab for study. The scientists win this time. A bigger battle is underway, though. Burmese pythons are not supposed to be in Florida at all. India's jungles are the native home of these big snakes. They are also native to marshy grasslands there. They come from other parts of South Asia as well. So what are they doing in Florida? For millions of years, natural barriers limited how animals and plants could move.

2Mountain ranges separated habitats. Deserts separated them too. Because of that, species could not spread. Oceans were especially hard to cross. That changed as transportation improved. The 1800s saw many people settle in lands that were new to them. They brought familiar livestock with them. They brought plant seeds too. Now and then an animal slipped aboard a ship and crossed the ocean that way. Those animals included rats, lizards, dogs, and cats. Today people move farther than ever. They also move faster. Other species move as well, whether people plan it or it happens by accident. In their native lands, species usually face competition. They usually face predators. Those challenges keep their numbers in check. Without those checks, a newly arrived species can increase fast. It may take over. It then becomes an invasive species. Kudzu vines from Japan are one example. Other plants in the southeastern states have been smothered by them. Guam's brown tree snake has destroyed a large share of the island's birds. The whole ecosystem can weaken when native species lose a food they have always used. The loss of shelter can do the same. The path Burmese pythons took into the Everglades ecosystem is still uncertain. The first invaders may have escaped from pet shops in Florida. Or pet owners may have released pythons when the snakes grew too big to care for.

3Once the snakes were loose, they thrived in the heat. They thrived in the humidity. They hid in the tangled underbrush. They gobbled up other animals. By then the python invasion had begun. Among some fans of snakes, Burmese pythons have become trendy pets. They are cheap to buy. People believe they are easy to control. Some owners are careless about their pets. They may not know how fast a python grows. They may not know how huge it becomes. In a couple of years, a snake that is 2 feet long can grow to 12 feet. That is a change from 0.6 meters to 3.7 meters. Snakes are escape artists. They will squeeze out of a cage that is not locked with care. They will squeeze out of an aquarium the same way. Now and then, a large pet python has attacked a person. Some of those attacks have killed people. A 2012 ban by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service stopped new Burmese pythons from entering the country. Owners must also put a microchip into a pet python. The chip makes the snake easier to trace. A photo shows a man holding his pet Burmese python. Reports of a Burmese python in the Everglades began in 1979. Sixteen more years went by before anyone found a second snake.

4Since 2000, though, reports of pythons have grown more and more common. Finding a nest of python eggs proved the fear was real. Wildlife biologists had feared that Burmese pythons were breeding in the wild. Pythons reproduce rapidly. A female usually lays more than fifty eggs a year. Within three years of hatching, the young pythons are ready to breed. Numbers in the Everglades then exploded. Year after year, more pythons lay eggs. Year after year, more pythons survive. A chart shows python removals in and around Everglades National Park. A rising python population was probably only one factor behind the dramatic rise in captures. The chart uses figures from the National Park Service. A newly hatched python can already crawl off. They start eating at once. They start growing at once. They reach adult size in only four years. A Burmese python averages about 12 feet in length. That is 3.7 meters. The largest may stretch more than 20 feet. That is 6.1 meters. They may weigh more than 200 pounds. That is 90 kilograms. Some people find these snakes creepy. Other people find them beautiful. The way a Burmese python kills is the stuff of nightmares. The snake group called constrictors includes pythons. These snakes track live animals with their sense of smell. They also sense heat. Then they surprise the prey.

5A wide mouth full of sharp teeth is how they grab hold. After the catch, thick muscles wrap the python's body around the animal. It squeezes until the victim suffocates. Finally the snake swallows the prey whole. The jaws spread wide enough to swallow prey up to five times the size of the head. A real census of Everglades pythons is still out of reach. They estimate the population at anywhere from 5,000 to 100,000. Researchers in 2012 caught the largest Burmese python yet recorded in the Everglades. She was 17 feet 7 inches long. That is 5.4 meters. Inside her were 87 eggs ready for laying. The Everglades has a nickname, the River of Grass. Few ecosystems in North America are as unusual. It is a region of wetlands in southern Florida. It consists mainly of marshes, swamps, and grasslands. Few hills rise on the broad, flat landscape. Even fewer buildings rise there. Even so, the land is rich with plant life and animal life. It is an important home for many native insects. It is home to fish, mammals, and other animals. Ask people about Everglades animals, and the American alligator may be the first they name. Length for this leathery reptile can reach 16 feet. That is 4.9 meters. It is a fierce predator. The Florida panther is rarer.

6Just a few dozen of these big cats remain free in the wild. Many smaller mammals live there as well. It is a paradise for birds. Those birds include the bald eagle, the great egret, and the roseate spoonbill. Many other wading birds live there too. Jaguars are gone from Florida. Pumas are gone too. The panther is the only big cat left, and its population is tiny. A Burmese python will gulp down any animal it can catch. In fact, people have called these snakes eating machines. In the Everglades, researchers found a python that had killed a deer and swallowed it. The deer weighed 80 pounds, which is 36 kilograms. Another find was a dead python whose mouth still held an alligator. The alligator was 6 feet long, which is 1.8 meters. Each animal died while killing the other. Worried that pythons might wipe out some native animals of Florida, one research team set out to learn whether that could be true. Where these snakes were numerous, the team counted small mammals. They studied marsh rabbits, raccoons, bobcats, and opossums. Road counts from 1996 and 1997, before pythons were common, were their baseline. Then they examined the same kind of data for more recent years. What the team found was shocking. The populations of all these mammals had crashed. There were no signs of marsh rabbits at all.

7There were no signs of foxes at all. Yet where pythons were rare, populations were unchanged. Where pythons were absent, populations were unchanged too. Many scientists think pythons are responsible. Either the snakes ate all these animals, or they wiped out the smaller animals that bobcats, foxes, and other predators eat. Park mammal counts from before 2000 were set beside counts from 2003 through 2011. As pythons rose, native mammal numbers crashed. Bobcats declined by 87.5 percent. Opossums declined by 98.9 percent. Raccoons declined by 99.3 percent. Marsh rabbits declined by 100 percent. Red foxes declined by 100 percent. Burmese pythons are endangered in South Asia, yet in the United States they threaten native animals that are already endangered. They may eat the prey of the Florida panther. They may simply eat wood storks. Their numbers in South Asia have been shrinking because their habitat is being destroyed. People also hunt them for their skins. People hunt them for their meat. People capture them and sell them as exotic pets. Nesting wood storks have increased since the 1984 listing of the bird as endangered. Protection of habitat has helped. Pythons may not help. Birds in the Everglades are also python food. One study found birds to be a quarter of what these pythons eat. Feathers of rare species have been found in the bellies of pythons.

8Bones of endangered species have been found there too. The wood stork is one of those species. What reveals a python's meals to scientists? In most cases, they do a necropsy. They slice open dead pythons. They study the dead animals they find in the stomachs. The remains have mostly been mammals. Birds that live in water are also at risk of being eaten. Birds that live near water are at risk too. These snakes are new here, and that newness may be why native animals cannot fight them well. Native species have evolved together. They have adapted to one another so they can coexist. Across 16 million years, Florida had no snake large enough to hunt many of these animals. Defense against this invasive hunter may be something the local animals have not learned. They may not know how to protect their young. In the Everglades, Burmese pythons sit at the top of the food chain. What is more, the ecosystem has no way to keep the invader in check. Alligators will eat pythons if they can. Once a python gets big enough, though, it will even attack alligators. A photo shows Skip Snow, a biologist at Everglades National Park. He pulls a bird from the stomach of a python. Finding pythons in the Everglades is tough.

9Hiding is easy for a python in this landscape. It makes it hard for humans to find them. A Burmese python may hide in the grass and the brush. It can stay underwater for up to thirty minutes. Even so, workers and volunteers in Florida are taking action. They are trying to bring pythons under control. At Everglades National Park, they track the snakes down and capture them when they can. They have taken almost 2,000 snakes in recent years. Hunters are also allowed to capture the snakes. They may kill them for skins and for meat. Unfortunately, hunters sometimes kill small native snakes by mistake. They think those snakes are baby pythons. One way researchers track pythons is with radio transmitters. The device goes into a living snake, and then the snake is set free. That snake might lead them to other pythons. One picture shows a radio tag being placed in a living Burmese python. New laws have been put in place so that pet pythons do not join the snakes already in the wild. Most experts now think Florida's python takeover can no longer be stopped. Biologists who study wildlife now fear a python spread into other regions. The snakes can swim to new territory. They can survive in most places where winters do not get too cold. Many southern states could be at risk.

10The risk runs from South Carolina all the way to California. Scientists continue to investigate the role pythons play in the changing ecosystem of the Everglades. They hold back from blaming pythons for everything until the evidence is stronger. Lessons from Florida's snake invaders may block a spread across the whole country. One picture shows a Burmese python coiled in park grass. Adapting means changing to fit a new situation or a specific environment. Native describes something natural to an area. A population is every member of one species living in one area. Competition is the fight organisms wage over scarce food, light, or territory. Predators are animals that hunt or eat other animals in order to survive. An ecosystem joins a community of living things with the habitat around them. A python is one of several big snakes that kill prey by wrapping it and forcing the air out. A food chain is a group of plants and animals linked by what they eat. To reproduce is to produce young that resemble the parent. An invasive species is a non-native species brought into an ecosystem that does harm to the other species living there. To suffocate is to cause death by making breathing impossible. A transmitter is a device that sends a signal, for example by radio or by television.

14. Goods Traded Before Writing

1In North America, the fur trade grew into a big business. This book opens with that idea, then follows a modern card trade into the distant past. It looks at trade across long distances. It looks at copper, at obsidian, and at shells. It looks at birds and animals. It closes with a conclusion and a glossary. When the bell rang, Robert shoved his homework for that night down into a backpack. He hurried to the steps at the front of the school. Day after day, Robert sat there with his friends. They played a game that uses trading cards. Robert, William, and Rudolfo liked the game. Sometimes they only sat and swapped cards. A photo shows people at a tournament for trading cards. William often received strong cards from a cousin in California. The family shop downtown supplied Rudolfo with cards he liked. Robert always enjoyed a swap. This day was different. A new student had just moved from Japan. Haruki liked swapping cards too. He wanted to swap with William. Haruki's cards came from Japan, and William had never seen cards like them. Every one of Haruki's cards used Japanese. Nobody at Robert's school had those cards. Haruki gave William cards William had never played. William gave Haruki some strong cards back. Haruki later shared those English-language cards with friends in Japan. Trade means giving one thing and getting another. A person might do work and receive an object. Or two people might swap items that both value. William and Haruki did that with game cards. Without trade, each family would have to raise all of its own food. Each family would have to sew all of its own clothes. Each family would have to build its own house.

2Humans have always needed to trade. It is basic to how we live. It lets people enjoy goods that come from every part of the world. Today, an ordinary object can start almost anywhere. A backpack item might have started in the town where you live. It might have come from Canada, Mexico, or Australia. It might have come from China, France, or even Brazil. Pause and think. Where do the things in your bedroom come from? Read the labels on clothes or toys. Then look at a map. How far did your clothing travel? A map shows trade routes from 6300 BC to AD 1200. It marks Asia, North America, and Europe. It marks the Atlantic Ocean and the Pacific Ocean, the second of those twice. It marks Africa, the Indian Ocean, South America, Australia, and Antarctica. The routes are only some of the paths ancient people used. They moved goods such as silk, gold, and olive oil. They moved gems and minerals, and more. A key shows sea routes and land routes. It shows miles and kilometers. Long before our time, by thousands of years, people also carried objects very far. In Europe, men spent months crossing oceans. They traveled up rivers. They went to trade swords or hairpins. Trade and daily life went on for thousands of years in both North America and South America. They did this before they met Europeans. Trade in prehistoric North America shows the pattern well. People carried valuable objects for thousands of miles. They swapped them for other special objects. Prehistoric means the time before those people wrote things down. Books are one kind of written record. How can anyone learn about trade from that early time?

3One path is oral tradition. Oral tradition is the group of stories a generation tells to the people who come after. Many storytellers like to add details. They want the tale to be more exciting, or easier to follow. Because of that, stories in an oral tradition change as years pass. Another path is archaeology. Archaeology studies people by studying what they leave behind. Remains of buildings can teach us. Graves can teach us. Trash teaches us a great deal about what people did. Archaeologists in a photo dig into buildings about 1,000 years old. Imagine trash from an old Colorado village. In it lies a seashell that is broken. The archaeologist who finds it learns something. The Colorado villagers had a trade link with ocean people. A map asks how Japanese trading cards might travel to Nevada. It shows Asia, North America, Japan, and the Pacific Ocean. It shows miles and kilometers. Think about William and his cards. Suppose his family moved. Suppose someone later found a Japanese card in the old room. That person would know William had a trade link with Japan, somehow. If the finder had never heard of Haruki, she might imagine many traders. She might think they passed the card along from Japan until it reached William. Japanese cards cannot be bought anywhere near William's hometown. So the card had to come from Japan. People often go farther when a commodity is rare and valuable. A commodity is something that gets traded. Gold is one example. A seashell is one. A trading card is one. In prehistoric North America, traded goods included copper, shells, and animals. People covered long distances on foot. They also went by boat.

4For that reason, trade goods were usually light. They were valuable. They would not spoil. Copper is a metal with a reddish color. Objects made from it turn up at many archaeological sites in North America. People left signs of life at an archaeological site. That is what the place is. Trash is one sign. Parts of buildings are another. An object from a site is an artifact if people shaped it or changed it. A photo shows an artifact of gold and copper from Peru, in South America. Graves at these sites have held copper artifacts that are still whole. Old garbage has held pieces that were broken. Fishhooks made of copper show up in North America. So do knives, jewelry, and bells. Some of the copper for those objects was mined near the Great Lakes. Lake Superior was an especially important area. In most of North America, prehistoric people found metal hard to make. Metal is often mixed into rock. That rock occurs only in certain places. Rock that holds metal is called ore. People must make the ore very hot to pull the metal out. That heating is called smelting. A map of native copper trade marks Lake Superior and the Great Lakes. It marks the largest deposits of native copper. It marks the Mississippi. It also marks Bedford Mound. That mound was a major center for prehistoric trade. It marks the places that would have seen trade in artifacts of native copper. It marks a possible major trade route. It also marks the Atlantic Ocean and the Gulf of Mexico. Lake Superior had huge amounts of native copper nearby. Native copper is special. It is very pure. It can be found in big chunks.

5It can also be found as sheets in cracks in rocks. Native copper does not need smelting. Miners took native copper from this area in a huge amount. The amount was hundreds of thousands of tons. Artifacts made from that copper have been found on both sides of the Mississippi River. They have been found far downstream as well. Several clues show that this copper moved across great distances. At many sites, archaeologists find copper artifacts, yet no natural copper is nearby. A distant source is the only way to explain that copper. A microscope can show the difference between native copper and smelted copper. Chemicals present in only tiny amounts can show the same split. Copper ore must be smelted before anyone can use the metal locked in the rock. Native copper can be used with no smelting at all. Sites in Arizona have copper bells. Sites in New Mexico have them too. Those bells were smelted copper. People in ancient Mexico made copper bells. People in ancient Central America made them too. The proof is the tools. Tools for smelting copper and for making bells like these have been found in Mexico and in Central America. Tools like that have not been found in Arizona or New Mexico. That is evidence the bells were traded. A photo shows an ancient copper bell made in Mexico. North American finds of copper artifacts have more than one starting place. Imagine that William, from the start of this book, owned cards in Spanish and cards in Japanese. A card that is not in English did not have to come from Japan. A Spanish card might come from Mexico or from Europe. A Japanese card would come from Japan.

6William's collection is quite a mix. Pause and think. Designs on artifacts help us learn where those artifacts began. Do you own a T-shirt or a sweatshirt whose words or pictures tell people where you have been? Obsidian is a volcanic rock that looks like glass. In prehistoric North America, many tools were made by knocking flakes off rock. The goal was a sharp edge. Obsidian beats ordinary rock for that job. It has been through heating. Blades made from it can be sharper than ordinary glass. They can be harder too. Obsidian occurs where volcanoes have been active. Obsidian from one area does not match obsidian from another. The difference is tiny amounts of chemicals. Those chemicals let us guess the source, just as chemicals do for copper. Photos show an obsidian sculpture and obsidian rock from Panum Crater. That crater is in northeastern California. Trace chemicals can teach us about many objects, not only copper or obsidian. The paper in William's trading cards might show where a card was made. The ink used to print the cards might show it too. People in the Northwest of prehistoric North America could find obsidian without much trouble. It came from places such as Washington and Oregon. It came from Northern California and from western Canada. Deposits often sit where volcanoes stand. Volcanoes make up a lot of the high peaks in the Northwest. Mt. Rainier, near Seattle, Washington, is one. Mt. Saint Helens is another. When a volcano erupts, the right conditions can turn lava into obsidian. The lava has to cool fast. A photo shows Mount St. Helens before the eruption of 1980. A trade map for obsidian marks Canada, Mt.

7Rainier, and Obsidian Cliff. It marks Mt. St. Helens and Bedford Mound. It marks Panum Crater and a possible major trade route. It marks the Mississippi River. It marks places with volcanic action during the last 2,000 years. It marks the Gulf of Mexico, the Pacific Ocean, and Mexico. Carriers took obsidian from one side of North America to the other. Those lands are the United States. They also include Canada and Mexico. In the United States, obsidian artifacts have been found through the valley of the Mississippi River. How the pieces look, and the trace chemicals in them, point to a source. The obsidian came from the northwest of North America. Ocean shells have a beautiful look. People can use them in many ways. Humans have used shells as jewelry. They have used them as small plates. They have even used them as musical instruments. Near the ocean, archaeological sites can hold huge piles of shells in trash heaps. Often the piles are leftovers. People ate the meat inside the shellfish and threw the shells away. A seashell in the Arizona desert is not leftover food. A seashell in the upper valley of the Mississippi River is not leftover food either. Carrying shellfish that far inland would take too long. The meat would rot. It would be dangerous to eat. A photo shows an archaeologist looking at seashells from an ancient trash heap. Shell earrings from prehistoric times were found in northern Arizona. How do we know some shells at a site were jewelry? Many shells have small holes. The holes are usually in the same spot. Thread the shells on a string, and they could become a necklace. Some people were buried wearing jewelry.

8Many people were buried with possessions they valued. If a site includes old graves, archaeologists sometimes find objects inside them. Those objects had been traded across great distances. In a grave that has not been disturbed, shells can lie in the shape of a necklace. The string may be gone. The shells still sit where a string would have held them. Shells can show who traded with whom. Some shellfish live only in certain places. Suppose the shellfish behind a shell lived only in the Gulf of Mexico. Suppose that shell is found in Colorado. Colorado's people, in that case, had a trading partner near that gulf. One photo shows a boat used for fishing. Another shows fish laid out on docks. Another shows a market selling fish. Pause and think. Many grocery stores sell seafood at a counter. What place supplied the fish on your plate? Read the labels. You can also ask at the seafood counter. Ask which place supplied the store's fish. A photo shows the bones of a dog. They were uncovered in an ancient cemetery in Israel. In every part of the world, people commonly trade animals. When Europeans first reached North America, they brought horses. A horse can carry a person. It can pull things and carry things. Once Europeans made contact, lots of animals joined the trade that crossed the Atlantic. Valuable animals were moved across the Atlantic Ocean. Bones of birds and of other animals sometimes turn up at archaeological sites. Bones of local animals are common, especially animals people ate. Deer bones are common in the east of North America at places ancient people occupied. A map of copper, shell, and parrot trade marks the Four Corners area.

9It marks the area of Ancient Puebloan culture. It marks shells from the sea. It marks Chaco Canyon. That canyon was a major center of prehistoric trade. It marks the Pacific Ocean, the Gulf of Mexico, and Mexico. It marks routes for copper and for parrots. Sometimes a site holds bones of exotic animals. An exotic animal comes from somewhere else. An elephant is exotic in North America. In parts of Africa, an elephant is ordinary. When an animal is exotic, that fact points to trade. An elephant in a North American zoo had to be traded from a distant place. Sites in the Southwest, including Arizona, sometimes hold parrot bones. A parrot is exotic there. Parrots normally live only in Central America or South America. Archaeologists think people wanted these birds for their colorful feathers. Parrot remains, and remains of similar birds, turn up thousands of miles north of the usual range. That find is evidence. It shows trade across a long distance. Some traders in prehistoric North America covered thousands of miles. Traders often used boats. They followed rivers, or they followed the coast. Traders depended on the Mississippi River. It was a major route. It tied together much of North America. Walking was the only choice at other times. A trip across a mountain range could take weeks of hard walking. The Rocky Mountains are one such range. The Appalachian Mountains are another. Today, heavy goods can move a long way more easily. Ships carry trade goods. Trucks, trains, and planes carry them too. Goods pass from country to country on those vehicles. A photo shows containers full of trade goods traveling by barge. Trade does not require a truck packed with costly objects such as televisions.

10Trade can be one person handing something over and receiving something else. Think back to William's cards and the way they arrived from Japan. The next time you travel, notice everything you take along. Later, notice what you left behind. Did you swap gifts with someone? Did you buy a thing you could not get at home? People in the prehistoric world did the same kind of thing. A closing map names Germany, Peru, Holland, and Mexico. These words have special meanings in this book. Archaeological sites are places where people find signs of past human activity. Trash is one sign. Parts of buildings are another. Archaeology learns about people by studying leftovers from their lives. Artifacts are objects that people have shaped or changed. A commodity is an item people trade. Gold is an example. A seashell is an example. A map near these words shows the USA, Mexico, Guatemala, and Indonesia. Copper is a soft metal with a reddish brown color. Exotic means out of the ordinary, and usually from far away. In a family, a generation is the set of people born near one time. It can also mean people whose parents are about the same age. Obsidian is rock that is glassy and volcanic. Oral tradition means stories passed along. One generation tells them to the people born later. Ore is rock that contains metals. Prehistoric names the era before writing. Books are one kind of record people had not made yet. Smelting uses very high heat. The heat pulls metal out of ore. To trade is to give something and receive something else. Transatlantic trade links groups across the Atlantic Ocean, one group on each side.

15. Hard Years of Dust on the Plains

1Contents. Introduction is on page 4. From Good Years to Hard Years is on page 6. Life with the Dust is on page 9. People Who Lived to Tell It is on page 12. Moving Away from the Dust is on page 16. Help Comes from the Government is on page 19. The closing section is on page 22. The glossary is on page 23. The index is on page 24. Introduction. Each year a hurricane strikes somewhere on Earth. Each year a flood strikes somewhere on Earth. Each year a forest fire strikes somewhere on Earth. Each year an earthquake strikes somewhere on Earth. People call these events natural disasters. In the late 1920s, two disasters of a different sort began. They ran on through the 1930s. They moved across the United States. They caused deep pain. They caused deep suffering. They went on for years. Most natural disasters last only hours. Others last only days. Hardship followed these long disasters. A vast number of people bore that hardship. The earliest blow was economic. Nature did not cause it. People named it the Great Depression. It began when stock prices crashed. The crash came in 1929. The stock market started downward. Then the fall stopped. Only about 20 percent of the old stock value remained. People lost money they had saved for a lifetime. People lost their jobs. People lost many belongings. Banks closed. Factories closed. The map shows the Dust Bowl region. The map names Canada. The map names the United States. The map names Mexico. The map names N. Dakota. The map names S. Dakota. The map names Nebraska. The map names Minnesota. The map names Montana. The map names Wyoming. The map names Iowa. The map names Missouri. The map names Colorado. The map names Kansas. The map names New Mexico. The map names Oklahoma. The map names Texas. The map names the Great Plains. The map names the Dust Bowl area. The map names the hardest hit area. Wheat fills fields that run farther than a person can see.

2The Great Depression reached far past the United States. Other countries around the world felt it too. It was the worst slump the world economy had ever seen. During the 1930s a second blow arrived. This blow dried the soil. Money had already dried up after the market fell. The soil dried up in that same way. The southern Great Plains took the blow. Those plains lie in the United States. Large parts of Texas were inside the stricken area. Large parts of Kansas were inside the stricken area. Large parts of Colorado were inside the stricken area. Large parts of Oklahoma were inside the stricken area. Harm also spread through the rest of the Great Plains. Canada's prairie land felt it too. People labeled the disaster the Dust Bowl. People labeled the time the Dirty Thirties. From Good Years to Hard Years. Before the dust years, farmers on the Great Plains prospered. That prosperity lasted for decades. World War I ran from 1914 to 1918. Farmers in Europe could not grow wheat while the war went on. The wheat on sale came from North American farms. Buyers received that wheat. They shipped the wheat overseas. Demand for wheat was high. The high demand pushed prices up. Farmers plowed extra grassland to grow still more wheat. European countries needed that wheat. Great Plains farmers kept their prosperity. Many other people were suffering at the same time. The Great Depression caused their suffering. Soon the good years on the plains would end. Extra wheat from plowed grassland caused two problems. The farmers had not expected either problem. First, too much wheat came onto the market. The price of wheat began to drop. Storage bins filled until they could hold no more. Farmers dumped wheat they had already harvested. Piles of it sat on the ground. Piles of it sat on the roads. The price fell after July 1930. It was still down in July 1931. One bushel sold for 68 cents in the first of those months. One bushel sold for 25 cents in the second of those months.

3Farmer Beck's wheat in 1930 covered 100 acres. Every acre produced 12 bushels. His wheat in 1931 again covered 100 acres. Every acre again produced 12 bushels. A picture shows a dust storm whipping over the plains. The dust is thick and choking. Second, a long drought arrived on the Great Plains. Fields that had been plowed then dried out. Too little moisture was left for crops. The fields stayed bare. Hot summer followed hot summer. The sun baked that bare soil. Winds grew stronger after the baking. Open dry dirt rose into dark clouds. The dust in the clouds choked anyone who breathed it. Those clouds swept across the land. Sunlight could not pass the thick rolling walls of dirt. Midday turned dark. People lit lamps in that darkness. Life with the Dust. The Dust Bowl held the Great Plains for year after year. Wind kept lifting the loose dirt. The wind carried that dirt high into the sky. Each lift became another dust storm over the plains. Some storms were called dusters. Other storms were called black blizzards. People tried everything to guard their homes. They tried just as hard to guard their lungs. They stuffed windows with newspapers and with rags. They stuffed doors the same way. Every person tied a rag across the face. Children went to bed with wet cloth on the face. The cloth covered the mouth. The cloth covered the nose. It was meant to keep dust from getting in. Dust pneumonia was a mysterious sickness. It infected thousands of people in the storms' path. The sickness killed men. The sickness killed women. The sickness killed children. The very young were hit hardest. The very old were hit hardest. A picture shows a farmer who puts a mask on before work. Dust still slipped into homes. Dust still entered every living creature. It clogged motors in cars. It clogged motors in trucks. Livestock wandered and could not see. The animals moved inside clouds of dirt. Many animals died.

4Dust had packed their lungs solid. Outdoors, dust piled into high drifts. The piles looked like snow left behind by a blizzard. A drift made of snow will melt. A drift made of this dust stayed in place. The drifts only grew taller. They buried tools. They buried farm equipment. They buried small buildings. People had to plow the roads. Trains stopped where they stood. Dirt heaped on the tracks was what stopped them. Wind carried dust east until it fell on distant cities. Chicago was one of those cities. Atlanta was one of those cities. New York was one of those cities. Some of that dust crossed the Atlantic Ocean. It fell onto ship decks far out on the water. A picture shows a tractor left unused. Dust has buried it. In winter, winds often mixed snow with dust. People named those mixed storms snusters. A picture shows Black Sunday's storm coming toward a town. The town is in the Texas panhandle. April 14, 1935, was the date of that storm. It may have been the worst day in the Dust Bowl. The morning started with sunrise. A clear blue sky filled the east. A gentle breeze came from the west. Then a gigantic wall appeared along the horizon. No warning came before it. The wall was made of dirt and dust. It raced over the rolling plains. Its speed was 60 miles an hour. That equals 96.5 kilometers an hour. The race headed east. Birds tried to fly faster than the wall. Rabbits tried to outrun the wall. The wall caught both. Animals fell to the ground. Some died from exhaustion. Some died because they suffocated. People ran for whatever shelter they could reach. Some got to sheds. Some got to barns. Some got to homes. Some got to cars. Another picture shows the same storm almost overwhelming two people. Some survivors recorded what they lived through. One of them was a boy named Melt White. He lived in Dalhart, Texas. He was still a child on that Sunday.

5A documentary film later included what he said. The subject of the film was the Dust Bowl. A hard wind was the first thing he remembered. He also remembered violent shaking in the house. He feared the whole building would be carried off. Dust outdoors filled the sky. Nearly all the daylight disappeared. A hand held in front of him could not be seen. Drawing the hand nearer still showed nothing. Even when the hand met his nose, it stayed invisible. Lawrence Svobida was a farmer in Kansas. He wrote a long record of what he experienced. The record later became a book. The book is about farming on the Great Plains. One part is the time before the Dust Bowl. One part is the time during the Dust Bowl. One part is the time after the Dust Bowl. Before the dust, he described a beautiful sight. He saw wheat fields running for many miles. The wheat stood waist high. A breeze made the wheat sway. No other view on earth seemed as lovely to him. Summer sun turned a wheat field gold. That gold view left him breathless. A picture shows the sight farmers wanted in the dust years. They hoped to see wheat blowing. They did not want to see dust blowing. During the drought his thoughts changed. The Great Plains was locked in that drought. He described wind mixed with dust. A person could see almost nothing. Dust filled people's eyes. Goggles did not help them see. In the documentary he describes how fierce the wind felt. It seemed the wind would never stop. He had never imagined a wind so strong. Nothing around him felt safe from being blown off. His fields were empty in every direction he looked. The book tells how his feeling about farming changed. Farming had once given him joy. Then he knew the crops were irrevocably gone. The feeling was like a death. Nature flouted the hope he carried of farming that ground. Nature also flouted the dreams he held about a farmer's life.

6He felt like quitting everything. He gave up trying to make any kind of life. A picture shows farmers who wait for rain. The rain will not come for years. Svobida still clung to hope. Many other people clung to that same hope. He believed rain might bring the drought to a finish. His book says he searched the sky each day. He was searching for rainclouds. He watched crops on neighboring farms die. The deaths came one farm after another. At last, five inches of precipitation fell. The fall of rain took two days. Water soaked down into the soil. That soaking stopped the dust. That soaking stopped the drought. Breathing dust for years badly harmed Lawrence Svobida. His health gave out. He accepted that he was beaten. He left the Great Plains. A picture shows Oklahoma in 1936. Cattle had little left to eat. Moving Away from the Dust. Settlers on the Great Plains had a hearty spirit. They took up land that held nothing built by people. Houses were absent. Water wells were absent. Roads were absent. Planted fields were absent. Hard times were already familiar. Many of them lasted out the Dirty Thirties. Each family found some way to keep going. They could not raise wheat. They raised thistles instead. They also raised a plant called soapweed. Chopped pieces of soapweed became feed for livestock. A picture shows soapweed. Early in the dust years, many farmers switched to dairy cattle. Farmers skimmed part of the milk. Pigs received that milk. Chickens received that milk too. Then the drought grew worse. There was not enough feed grown for the cattle. Other livestock went short of feed as well. No income was left for the farmers. Tiredness set in. Hunger followed that tiredness. Farm payments fell behind for a great many families. Moving away was the choice they made at last. The place they left was the Great Plains. A better life was what they wanted in some new region. The west pulled thousands of them onward. California was the place that drew the travelers.

7Work on rich farmland was what they hoped to find there. The number of jobs was smaller than the number of people. The Plains states lost 2.5 million people by the time the 1930s ended. California took in two hundred thousand of those travelers. John Steinbeck described that journey west. A novel of his became famous. Readers first got the novel in 1939. He called it The Grapes of Wrath. His account follows a crowd counted in the hundreds of thousands. Those people crossed a line of mountains. Their direction was toward California. Every possession still owned rode in the family car. Many of them slept in the car. Many of them slept in a tent. Some travelers joined into caravans. Cars in a caravan rolled as one group. Safety was the reason for staying together. A stop in one spot never lasted long. Movement was constant. The hunt for work was constant. They were desperate for food. A picture shows John Steinbeck together with his novel. Help Comes from the Government. Aid was required for people on the Great Plains. The land under them was being lost. The farms they worked were being lost. The hope they carried was being lost. Work had already disappeared for millions more. The cause of that loss was the Great Depression. A replacement job seemed impossible to get. Hunger reached every one of them. Poverty reached every one of them. Many lives were ending. A picture shows Franklin Roosevelt. Voters in the United States picked a president during 1932. Franklin Roosevelt was the winner of that vote. Soon he put forward a plan aimed at recovery. People knew the plan as the New Deal. New government agencies grew out of it. Farmers were among the people the programs served. Unemployed workers were served as well. One picture marks the day Roosevelt took office. Hugh Bennett was his choice to direct a fresh agency. Bennett's fame came from soil conservation. The agency carried the name Soil Erosion Service. New ways of farming were what Bennett pressed for.

8He wanted blowing dirt halted at the place it rose. Congress was urged by him to pass a law. That law is the Soil Conservation Act. The law's year is 1935. Further help reached the weary farmer. One piece of help was the Emergency Farm Act. It got its start in 1933. Dollars set aside by the act totaled 200 million. Farmers behind on land payments could use those dollars. A bank loan could be refinanced with the aid. Another piece was the Drought Relief Service. It got its start in 1935. The government purchased cattle from the farmers. Those sales kept bankruptcy away from the farmers. The price paid stood above a normal market deal. A further piece was the Soil Conservation Service. It got its start in that same year of 1935. The service built plans that would stop erosion. Pay went to farmers who chose soil-saving ways to farm. The last of these pieces was the Shelterbelt Project. It got its start in 1937. Pay went to farmers who set trees over the Great Plains. A tree row beside a fencerow would slow the wind. Wind that slowed would leave the soil where it lay. The plan called for four million trees. Canada's border was where the rows would begin. Texas was where the rows would end. Jobs were another aim inside the New Deal. People who lacked work were the ones to be hired. Several programs shared that aim. Biggest of the programs was the Works Progress Administration. The short name of that program is WPA. The year the WPA began was 1935. Millions of workers drew pay from it afterward. Writers set down stories of the dusty years. Songs were made about those years as well. Woody Guthrie wrote the songs listeners still know best. He was a popular singer of folk songs. He was a writer as well. Two titles stand out from the many songs he made. One title is So Long It's Been Good to Know Yuh.

9That same song is also called Dusty Old Dust. Dust Bowl Blues is the other well-known title. A picture shows him playing before a crowd. The crowd is gathered in New York City. The picture dates from the 1940s. Colorado holds land called Pawnee National Grasslands. That land is one of several areas the government guards. The guarding is there so a new Dust Bowl will not form. Conclusion. The Dirty Thirties were nearly finished. Clouds that carried rain moved in where dust had hung. A wide share of the Great Plains at last left the drought behind. The Canadian Prairie left the drought behind too. Those better skies arrived in the early 1940s. Wheat was planted on the farms again. Fields of gold moved in the wind, as in the years before the dust. Farm work was no longer done the old way. Governments reserved grassland. Plows were kept off the land they reserved. The reserved land added up to thousands of acres. They wanted that land to stand against one more Dust Bowl. Fighting began in Europe during 1939. The fight was World War II. North America entered the fight during 1941. Much of the world then started to mend. The Great Depression lost its hold as nations mended. Two clouds had hung over the Dirty Thirties. By the finish, neither cloud remained. Glossary. On page 19, conservation means saving natural resources, guarding them, and restoring them. On page 19, erosion means land wearing away slowly. Water does some of that wearing. Wind does some of it. Ice does some of it. On page 4, disasters means events that strike suddenly and cause terrible harm. On page 14, flouted means treated as if it had no worth. On page 8, drought means rain fails for a long time and water grows scarce. On page 6, grasslands means open country covered mainly by grass. On page 4, the Great Depression means a deep fall in the economy. It struck the United States and the wider world. The years ran from 1929 until 1939.

10On page 9, dust pneumonia means a lung illness brought on by breathing a heavy load of dust. On page 5, Dust Bowl means a disaster in the USA. The disaster came in the 1930s. On page 5, Great Plains means level country with hardly any trees, set in the middle of the United States and in Canada. On page 9, dust storm means a twisting wind that fills the air with dust. On page 17, income means money a person gains from a job or gains in some other way. On page 4, economic means tied to buying goods and services and to selling them. On page 14, irrevocably means the change cannot be undone. On page 6, prosperity means a time of success or of lucky fortune. On page 18, migration means people moving from one region into a different region. On page 19, recovery means a return to ordinary life after a setback or a loss. On page 15, precipitation means wetness that drops from clouds. Rain is one form of it. On page 19, unemployed means able to work and ready for work, but without a job that pays. Index. 16. How Animals Change to Live

1Think of a penguin. The picture in your mind is probably a black and white bird. It slides on snow. It dives into icy water. You probably do not picture it resting in the sun on a warm island near the equator. One penguin does live in a place like that. Warm islands are home to the Galapagos penguin. Those islands are in the eastern Pacific Ocean. This bird's species began in Antarctica. How can it last in so much warmth? Over time, it changes. Little by little it gains body features that fit the place. It also gains ways of acting that fit the place. Those changes are adaptations. The Galapagos penguin is not alone in this. Most kinds of animals are here today because they changed. They fit themselves to the places around them. No other penguin lives at the equator. Only the Galapagos penguin does. This book follows that story. It starts with the penguin surprise. It explains ways of surviving. It shows that change is slow. It looks at strange diets. It looks at animals whose blood runs cold. It looks at masters of disguise. It ends with how life finds a way. A glossary lists key words. The trip monarch butterflies make is famous. For some of them, the flight toward Mexico starts very far north. It can start in Canada. Being able to adapt is central to staying alive. A living thing must do well where it lives. If it cannot, it must change. Or it must leave. Or it will die. Adaptations come in two kinds. One kind is behavior. Behavior is how an animal acts.

2Some animals travel from one place to another. They are looking for better food. They are looking for better living conditions. That travel is a behavioral adaptation. It usually happens as the seasons turn. Monarch butterflies give a clear example. Fall comes to the northern places where they spent summer. Then they fly south. They go to Mexico. They go to other warm places too. In spring, temperatures climb. The butterflies head north again. Hibernation is another behavioral adaptation tied to the seasons. The animal does not leave. It stays and enters a state a bit like sleep. Frogs are true hibernators. Ground squirrels are too. Bats are too. People once thought bears hibernate. They thought skunks do too. Those animals actually enter a lighter sleep. That lighter sleep is called torpor. Both groups enter these states in the cold months of winter. Food is scarce then. In that state they use less energy. They need less food. Spring brings better conditions outside. Then they become active again. A whiskered bat spends the winter asleep in a limestone quarry. The quarry is in the Netherlands. That photo sits in a small frame. A larger photo shows a black bear mother in Minnesota. She sleeps in a den with her cub. Bats truly hibernate. Bears use the lighter kind of sleep. Structural adaptations are different. They are changes in the way a body is built. The new build makes life in that environment easier. The changes come slowly. They take many generations. A chameleon grew skin that can switch color. The new color helps it blend into what is around it.

3Predators then have trouble seeing it. Prey has trouble seeing it too. Foxes also use body features for camouflage. The Arctic fox wears a white coat. Snow makes that coat hard to spot. The desert fox wears a tan coat. Desert sand and that coat look alike. Both foxes have further body features. Ears on an Arctic fox are much smaller than ears on its cousin. A fox loses body heat through its ears. The Arctic fox lives where it is cold, so it must keep heat in. The desert fox lives where it is hot. It must stay cool. Its ears are very large, and those ears help heat leave the body. A walking shark takes a nighttime walk on the seabed. Some adaptations are stranger than others. Walking sharks are one example. They live in waters near Australia and near Indonesia. They are small. Their front fins are very strong. At times those muscular fins are not for swimming. The shark uses them to crawl over the ocean floor. Giraffes have odd adaptations of their own. Reach far down through a giraffe's fur and the smell will make you cover your nose. That coat defends the animal with chemicals. It is packed with antibiotics. It also holds other chemicals that smell strong. Those chemicals keep parasites away. Sleep is something giraffes need only in a very small amount. Most of them manage on just two hours of sleep in a day. Nearly all of that sleep happens while they stand. Both habits help guard them from predators. Even if a giraffe lies down, it usually holds its neck up high.

4Animal species do not sit and plan how to change. Change comes on its own as time passes. Long ago, some bears entered torpor. Those bears lived through cold winters. Bears that did not enter torpor died. Only the first group was left to reproduce. Something like that happened with foxes. Arctic foxes with small ears lived. Arctic foxes with large ears did not. The survivors mated. After enough time, every fox in that cold place had small ears. The useful feature had been passed down. A species changes through both kinds of adaptation, behavior and body structure, working together. The Galapagos penguin shows this well. These penguins reached the tropics by riding the Humboldt Current. That current brings cold water north from Antarctica. The water is rich in squid. It is also rich in fish such as sardines. The penguins feel at home in that cold water. They still must come onto land to nest. Tropical land feels much hotter than the sea beside it. A map of this route shows the equator and South America. It shows the Galapagos Islands and the Humboldt Current in the Pacific Ocean. These penguins have ways to handle the heat. They pant, and panting cools them. They stretch their flippers out. The flippers shade their feet. That shade keeps the feet from getting a sunburn. They build nests in gaps between rocks. The nests stay hidden from the Sun's rays. Other penguins shed old feathers once each year. Galapagos penguins do it twice. Losing feathers helps them stay cool. These actions and body features grew over time.

5Penguins without them died away. Good nest spots like the one in the picture are few. Researchers in 2010 made 120 nest sites that stay in the shade. The nests were for penguins. The sites were meant to help these endangered birds. Diet is a behavioral adaptation. Some species have eating habits that help them last. A kangaroo rat lives in a desert. The desert has little water and little rainfall. Even so, the rat gets by without ever drinking. The moisture it needs comes from its food. It eats grass seeds. It eats mesquite beans. This rat hops in a way that recalls a kangaroo. The kangaroo is much larger. The rat, though, lives in North America. A parrot called the scarlet macaw also eats seeds. The seeds this bird eats hold a deadly poison. Gnawing on rocks may be what keeps the bird safe. Those rocks contain clay. The clay soaks up the poison. Then the bird can digest the seeds without harm. Newer studies point another way. The birds may eat the clay so their diet has more salt. Either explanation works. The habit is instinctive. As a way of eating, the habit helps these birds stay alive. Tube worms are strange animals of the ocean. They have their own way of living with poison. They live in total darkness. They make their home on the floor of the Pacific. They gather near hot vents. Dangerous gases rise through the vents. The gases come from far below Earth's surface. Those gases would kill most living things. Tube worms survive anyway. They even do well. Their odd bodies are the reason.

6They have no eyes. They have no stomach. In simple terms, each worm is a sack filled with bacteria. The bacteria inside them use the gases as a source of energy. In the North Pacific, tube worms cluster beside a warm gas vent. Temperatures below freezing can kill an animal. The cells in an animal's body are mostly water. If a cell freezes, that water becomes ice. The ice expands. The cell bursts. Death follows. Animals have found different ways to stop this. When the air turns cold, some species travel to warmer places. Others sleep in dens that stay warm. A further group stays alive by making body fluids supercool. Many insects make chemicals that work like a kind of antifreeze. The chemicals stop ice crystals from growing. The cells become supercool. They never actually freeze. This adaptation lets cockroaches last for days in freezing air. It lets caterpillars do the same. The red flat bark beetle of Alaska can supercool its body fluids down to minus 50 degrees Celsius. That is minus 58 degrees Fahrenheit. A wood frog can last for weeks at a time. It can do this when the temperature falls as low as minus 14 degrees Celsius, which is 7 degrees Fahrenheit. Ice does form inside the frog. It forms between the cells, not inside them. The cells are then not in danger of bursting. Some animals simply get rid of the water in the body. The process is called dehydration. Antarctic midges are tiny insects. They dry out. They look frozen. They seem dead. Touch them with water, though, and they spring back to life.

7Among mammals, a single species can make its body supercool. That mammal is the Arctic ground squirrel. Its body temperature can fall as low as minus 3 degrees Celsius. That is 26 degrees Fahrenheit. Just how the squirrel does this is a mystery. It does not make its own antifreeze. It does not dump its fluids. Even so, it has adapted so it can survive very low temperatures. In Alaska, an Arctic ground squirrel finds a snack. A water bear is tiny, and it is not really a bear. It is a microscopic animal called a tardigrade. Its adaptations are impressive. It can stand cold down to minus 200 degrees Celsius. That is minus 328 degrees Fahrenheit. It can stand heat higher than 149 degrees Celsius. That is 300 degrees Fahrenheit. Strong radiation does not kill it. Radiation is a dangerous form of energy. It can also take pressure six times the squeeze found on the ocean floor. In many of these conditions, the water bear curls into a ball. It enters a state that is almost without life. It also makes many substances called antioxidants. Those substances keep vital organs whole. It makes a protein as well. The protein shields its cells from what radiation does. These adaptations make tardigrades nearly impossible to destroy. Scientists think tardigrades will last longer than humans. Their amazing adaptations are the reason. Camouflage is another structural adaptation. Some animals hide by looking like something else, and they do it extremely well. The leaf mantis is one. Parts of its body match dead leaves in shape. They match dead leaves in color too.

8That remarkable adaptation lets this insect hide on the forest floor. It can hunt there without being seen. The cuttlefish can also pretend to be part of the scene around it. At a glance, you might think it is a little squid. Its skin changes color. Its skin changes texture. The match to its surroundings is almost perfect. It has three layers of skin. It also has fleshy growths called papillae. It uses those growths to look like coral that is bumpy. It can look like stone. Prey can barely see it. Because it is hard to see, this skilled hunter is also hard to hunt. An adaptation helps only when it works. A change in habitat can turn a useful adaptation into a deadly weakness. An insect called the peppered moth went through exactly that change. In early times, the wings of most peppered moths were white. Dark spots marked those wings. The moths blended easily with tree bark that lichen covered. Birds and other predators could not see them. Some peppered moths were dark all over. Birds could spot those moths easily. Because of that, white moths far outnumbered dark moths. The 1800s brought the Industrial Revolution. Smoke from factory stacks brought pollution. The pollution killed the lichen. It turned the bark black. Dark peppered moths could now blend in and hide. White moths showed up against the dark bark. Birds could see the white moths with ease. The count of white moths fell. The count of dark moths rose. In the 1900s, controls on pollution took effect. Bark on the trees grew lighter again.

9The numbers of each moth color changed yet again. Dark moths became rare again. For some individual moths, the color difference was a deadly weakness. For the species as a whole, that same difference let it survive while the environment changed. Adaptation matters most where the environment is changing. For most living things today, the change is climate change. Animals such as the Galapagos penguin must adapt to the conditions around them once again. Ocean water is warming. Food is growing scarce. The penguins must change how they eat, or what they eat, or where they eat. Animals across the planet face the same need. Most will survive if they keep adapting as their environment changes. They can do that only if they have enough time. A parent Galapagos penguin gives food to its chick. The cool water that supplies their food may warm for good. The birds might then need to leave for a new home. Many species are being forced to adapt because of climate change. Adaptation needs time. In this race with the clock, some species have a better chance than others. Tawny owls come in more than one shade. Winters in recent decades have been milder. Researchers in Finland have seen more dark owls than before. Less snow means the dark owls blend in better. They are more likely to survive. They are more likely to reproduce. Polar bears may have come from brown bear ancestors. That split may go back as far as six hundred thousand years. The species needed time to fit its conditions. Black skin is one adaptation. White fur is another.

10Feet covered in fur are another. Together they suit the bears for life in the Arctic. Now their sea ice home is disappearing because of global warming. That loss is a dire threat. May 2008 was when these bears went on the list of threatened species. The listing was under the Endangered Species Act. An American pika is cute, and it is also tough. It lives high on mountains, so it has adapted to a cold climate. Warm air can make a pika overheat. Then it can die. The pikas already live so high that they have nowhere left to go. With no higher ground left, climate change could wipe them out sooner than many other species. These words have special meanings in this book. An adaptation is a change in an organism, or in a species, that helps it survive better where it lives. Inhabit means to live in a place, or to occupy it. Instinctive describes something natural that is done without thinking. Cope means to handle a problem successfully, or to get past it. Masquerade means to pretend to be something else, or someone else. Digest means the body breaks food down. The result is energy. Reproduce means to make young that are similar to the parent. Gnaw means to nibble or bite something again and again. Thrive means to grow well and stay healthy. A habitat is the wild place where a plant or an animal normally lives. Torpor is a state of low activity. It is a deep sleep, but hibernation is deeper. Hibernation is the very deep winter sleep of certain animals.

17. How Computers Share Across the World

1Try to picture daily life with no Internet. Belief comes hard, yet only a few decades back this network did not exist. It has changed our lives. It keeps changing them. Since the computer, maybe no other invention has changed life more. The Internet has changed how we communicate. It has changed how we gather information. It has changed how we shop, pay bills, and learn. This book starts with that introduction. It asks what the Internet is. It explains how information is sent. It explains how the Internet works. It tells how the Internet began. It tells how people use it. It ends with a conclusion, a glossary, and an index. In simple words, the Internet is awesome. It is a network. Billions of computers join it. Other devices around the world join it too. Any linked computer can swap information packets with every other linked computer. Because of those links, information races from one machine to another. A packet can leave one computer and reach a computer on the far side of the world. The trip can take only a few seconds. The packets are built from a line of ones and of zeros. Cables called fiber-optic are built to carry huge amounts of information quickly. Packets sent on the Internet can hold words. They can hold pictures, sound, and video.

2All of that information travels by wire cable or by fiber-optic cable. Copper is one metal used to make wire cable. Other metals are used too. Very thin strands are bundled to make fiber-optic cable. The strands are glass or plastic. Information can also travel without a wire, on radio waves. That wireless way is called WiFi. Inside a WiFi network, a receiver gathers the packets from those radio waves. The receiver then sends what it gathered onward. It uses the wire cable, or the fiber-optic cable, that joins the receiver to the Internet. How much information is moving on the Internet at one moment depends on bandwidth. Picture cars on a highway. The bandwidth of a cable lets information travel in that same way. A highway with more lanes can hold more cars. Greater bandwidth means more information can move through a cable. Heavy traffic on a highway slows the cars. In the same way, a cable gets slower when more information crowds into it. A photo shows fiber-optic cable beside copper wire. One phone call needs two copper wires. Twenty-four thousand phone calls need only two strands of fiber-optic cable. Bandwidth is higher in fiber-optic cable than in wire cable. What it carries can be thousands of times what a wire cable carries. A wire cable usually beats WiFi on bandwidth.

3It still lets a person move around freely and stay connected. We depend more and more on the Internet for information. Bandwidth matters more because of that. Sound needs more bandwidth than text. Pictures need more. Video needs more. So multimedia content needs greater bandwidth if it is going to move through the Internet quickly. A chart compares file sizes, measured in kilobytes, marked K. Text is about 50k. A photo is about 500k. Music is about 2,500k. Video is about 8,500k. The scale on the chart runs from 0 up to 9,000. Bandwidth counts how many units of information computers prepare and send. The count is how many of those units can pass through the Internet in one second. A bit is information in its smallest unit. Join eight bits, and they become a byte. The letter A, like any single letter in a text, equals one byte. A typical typed page holds 2,000 bytes. A short novel holds one million bytes. A megabyte is 1,000 kilobytes. A gigabyte is 1,000 megabytes. People often use those measures for how much a computer can store. Here is a math question. Count 1,000 bytes and you have one kilobyte. A photo that is 1 megabyte holds how many bytes? The answer is 1,000,000 bytes. Powerful computers form the Internet's backbone. They stay linked without a break.

4Other computers can connect to that network. A modem is the device a lone computer uses when it joins the network. The modem codes digital information. It also decodes that information as the information moves to your computer and away from it. You can get on the Internet with a modem. You log in with a username and a password. People usually buy Internet access from an Internet Service Provider, also called an ISP. They pay a fee each month. Fees climb higher for many broadband users out in the countryside. Those fees are for fast access that uses DSL or a cable modem. Satellite Internet service lets people connect from far-off places all around the world. A photo shows a cable modem. The Internet lets people share information. Pictures are part of what they share. After access is yours, information can go out to every other person who is hooked up. You can receive information from them too. Imagine a friend mentions a site on the web. The site has information about giraffes. She gives you the website URL, and the file name is part of it. You type that address. Then you press enter, or return, on the keyboard. Or you click the go button in your browser. In seconds, an article about giraffes shows on your screen.

5Here is a simple account of what happened. A browser is special software. It finds webpages and lets you look at them. The first step joined your computer, through the browser, to a server out on the network. Next, the browser asked for the website's information. The server located the requested material. It returned that material to your machine. After the browser had the page you wanted, you could see the page. You might see it on a computer, a tablet, or a phone. A small diagram shows three items. Item one is a webpage. Item two is a video file. Item three is a smartphone being used to watch a video file from the Internet. An expert in information technology keeps servers joined to the Internet. Every computer that makes up the Internet fits in one of two groups. The groups are servers and clients. A server is a computer that provides a service. Giving people a way to reach information is the service. Servers come in different kinds. To send email, or to receive it, you connect to an email server. To ask a website for information, you connect to a server. Computers that match yours make up the rest of the network. They are called clients. A client does not provide a service. It does send information. It does receive information.

6Every computer on the Internet has an IP address, whether it is a server or a client. IP stands for Internet Protocol. Each IP address is a series of numbers that belongs to that computer alone. The numbers sit in four sets. A dot separates each set from the next. A long string of numbers is hard for most people to remember. So computers also get domain names. A domain name is text. One example is a site where students read books a teacher assigned. That computer's domain name is words, not the number series. Most people think the Internet started in the 1960s. Leaders at the Department of Defense, in the United States, wanted a network people could count on. The network would be for communication if a disaster struck, or if a war did. The network that was built is called ARPAnet. Those letters stand for Advanced Research Project Agency network. It linked four computers to one another. The 1980s arrived with links among a huge set of computers. The count passed twenty thousand. Soon, universities built networks of their own. They wanted an easier way to share information. One of the biggest university networks was called NSFnet. Those letters stand for National Science Foundation network. People came to call that network the Internet. The first web browser came from the work of Tim Berners-Lee.

7He also wrote rules so different devices could speak to one another across the Internet. In the 1990s, this physicist changed the Internet forever. Before Berners-Lee, a network could talk to the many computers on it. It could not share information with a different network. Each network had its own language. It could not understand another network. It resembled travelers who could not understand the speech of another land. Berners-Lee fixed the problem. He wrote one shared language. Computers on many networks could then talk to one another. Email goes out from phones that are smart, from tablets, and from computers. They send text messages too. The messages arrive at once. A message can carry an attachment, such as a photo. An answer starts with a click on reply. Then that person types a message. Video calls are possible too. During the call, each person can watch the other person speak. A search box might show the words best hoverboards for kids. Nearly any question has an answer somewhere on the Internet. So much information is there that finding the right piece can seem impossible. A search engine solves that. It is a tool for locating the information you want. Millions of webpages get checked together when a search engine runs. You go to the search engine's website.

8You type one search term, or several. Those terms are also called keywords. Some companies even build smart speakers. A smart speaker lets you ask your question out loud. People also shop on the Internet. They pay bills there too. You can look at pictures of products you might buy. You can listen to music, buy it, and then play it on your computer. You can buy tickets for airlines. You can buy tickets for entertainment. Some shopping sites let you bid for a product, the way you would at an auction. You can buy computer games online. You can buy items to use inside those game worlds. Shopping this way became so popular that 2017 set a huge mark. The spending of shoppers worldwide passed $2.4 trillion. They were buying goods and services on the Internet. A girl in a photo wears headphones. She is listening to music on the Internet. Reading practice for students happens on websites. They practice other skills too. Police officers use the Internet from computers in their cars. They look up information about suspects. The Internet has let computers everywhere join one worldwide network. That network is for sharing information. It has changed how we do business. It has changed how we communicate. It has changed how we buy goods and services. The Internet will matter even more as more people connect.

9They will connect from homes, from schools, and from businesses. They will connect through wireless links. The Internet will keep changing. New technologies will let people interact in new ways. You can explore more on the Internet. Step one needs an adult's permission and help. Open a search page where your browser shows addresses. Step two is typing. Try the word Internet. Try the name Tim Berners-Lee. Try ARPAnet. Start the search. Third, read the colored links. Click one that looks interesting. When you want other links, use the back button on the browser menu. That button returns you to the search page. Fourth, try more searches. Use words from something you study in school. Use words from activities you like. Use names of animals you like, or of characters in books. These words have special meanings in this book. Bandwidth means how much information, at most, an Internet cable can carry. A bit is the smallest amount of information a computer can store, or that can be sent over the Internet. Broadband describes something that is part of a high-speed computer network, or related to one. A browser is a software program. With it, users can open Web pages and view them. Those pages sit on the World Wide Web. Clients are computers the public uses to reach what the Internet offers.

10Domain names are text names for website addresses. Each one is tied to a certain IP address. Fiber-optic describes cable. Thin glass strands make it, or thin plastic strands do. Those strands can carry signals. The Internet is a public computer network that spans the globe. An Internet Service Provider, or ISP, is a company that sells a way onto the Internet. Each server has its own number, called an IP address. Each client computer has one too. A modem is a device that connects a computer to the Internet. Use a search engine when you need to find facts online. A server is a computer that manages how other computers reach a service. That service may be a local network, or it may be the Internet. A URL is the whole address used to open a website. The address includes the domain name. URL stands for Uniform Resource Locator. WiFi names a network without wires. The network carries information across the Internet. An index of topics, without page numbers, includes these words. Server, access, and software. Tim Berners-Lee and the Department of Defense. Browser and email. Cable, multimedia, and bandwidth. Networks and wire. ARPAnet and fiber-optic. NSFnet and computer. WiFi and client. Shopping and domain name. Universities and IP address. Website and language. Modem.

18. Life in the Amazon Rainforest

1This book starts with a place full of life. It then climbs the layers of the rainforest. It looks at how the forest floor stays clean. It asks what the forest has to do with you. A last part tells where to learn more and explains useful words. A note on spelling comes first. Rainforest can be spelled as one word or as two. This book uses the one-word spelling. When this book says rainforest, it means a tropical rainforest. There is a place where monkeys swing. They howl. Jaguars leap from tree to tree. Insects create huge colonies underground. Bananas grow there in abundance. Pineapples grow there. Coconuts grow there. Chocolate grows there. Tiny frogs live in flowers. They climb trees. Pink-colored dolphins swim in the river. Storms come often to this place. The air is sweet. Some sunlight filters through the vines of the tall trees. It filters through the leaves. Mostly it is dark here on the ground. It is hot. It is humid. It is surprisingly still. Rainwater trickles down through the foliage. It moves from leaf to leaf. You hear a slow sound. The sound is drip, drip, drip. Your skin is sweaty. An insect lands on your neck. An ant quietly walks across your sandal. Suddenly a little brown monkey swings to a nearby branch. A bright yellow eyelash viper slithers up a tree. A bright green Amazon parrot flutters past. Welcome to the Amazon basin. It is a tropical rainforest. Rainforests are located all over the planet. Tropical rainforests are close to the equator. They lie between 23.5 degrees north latitude and 23.5 degrees south latitude. That band wraps around the planet. Outside the tropics, the forests are called subtropical rainforests. Both types of rainforests share characteristics. Subtropical rainforests have fewer species than tropical rainforests. They have fewer kinds of trees, plants, and animals. Rainforests live up to their name. They are forests where it rains almost every day. A typical tropical rainforest receives a huge yearly rainfall. The rain is between 150 and 400 centimeters. That depth is 59 to 157 inches. They are also warm. Their temperature averages between 25 and 35 degrees Celsius. That range is 77 to 95 degrees Fahrenheit. Rainforests are green year-round.

2Humidity there stays between 77 and 88 percent. Their rainy, hot, and humid climate suits tall plants. It suits tall trees, vines, ferns, and other plants. People call the really thick parts of rainforests jungles. Tropical rainforests cover a small part of Earth's surface. The share is about 6 percent. But more than half of the world's plant and animal species are found in them. That share is 50 percent. The total is close to thirty million species of plants and animals. Most of the world's rainforests are in Africa. They are in Southeast Asia. They are in South America. The Amazon rainforest lies in South America. Most of its area is in Brazil. The area is 5.5 million square kilometers. That area is 1.4 billion acres. Parts of the rainforest are also in other countries. Those countries are Peru, Venezuela, and Ecuador. They include Colombia, Guyana, and Bolivia. They include Suriname and French Guiana. It is the largest tropical rainforest in the world.
