It has more different types of insects than any other place on Earth. It has more types of plants. It has more types of animals. Scientists discover new species of insects there every year. They discover new species of plants there every year. Through the heart of this rainforest flows the Amazon River. It flows at a rate of up to 7 kilometers per hour. That speed is 4.3 miles per hour. The water is brown from all the silt it carries. The river is like a huge sea of fresh water. More than one thousand tributaries feed it. Catfish live in the Amazon River. Piranhas live there. Dolphins live there. This book will take you on a tour of the Amazon rainforest. The tour travels from the tallest trees to the rainforest floor. Along the way, you will meet an eagle. You will meet a monkey. You will meet a jaguar. You will meet an ant. You will even hear the words these Amazon natives might use if talk were possible. How big, then, is the Amazon River? The river can be more than 48 kilometers across at its widest point. That width is 30 miles. The wide point comes during the rainy season. It flows almost 6,437 kilometers to the Atlantic Ocean.

3That length is 4,000 miles. Within its mouth is an island. The island is the size of Switzerland. The river is home to more than forty thousand plant species. The river's arapaima fish can grow to be 3 meters long. That length is 10 feet. It can weigh 180 kilograms. That weight is 400 pounds. Put the next eight largest rivers together, and this river still holds more water. The Amazon is the world's widest river. It is the world's largest river. A yearbook page introduces slow neighbors. Sloths are slow-moving animals. They seem to just hang out in the trees. They hang upside down. A sloth may sleep for as many as twenty hours a day. When they are awake, they hardly move. That stillness allows algae to grow on their fur. The algae helps them blend into the forest. Sammy the sloth has a nickname. People call him Slowpoke. Sammy was voted most likely to survive. He was also voted least likely to succeed. The tapir is a large, hoofed animal. It is related to the horse. It is related to the rhinoceros. Tapirs often weigh well over 136 kilograms. That weight is 300 pounds. They like to swim in the river. They like to bathe in the mud. They are nocturnal. They prefer to look for food when it is dark. Tamara the Tapir was voted to have the best personality. The capybara is the world's largest rodent. It is approximately 1.2 meters long. That length is 4 feet. It stands 60 centimeters at the shoulder. That height is 2 feet. A yearbook line starts her name as Katie Capybara. Katie does not like to be called a big rat. Capybaras have slightly webbed feet. The feet help them swim. Katie was voted most likely to win a rat race. The poison arrow frog has super-bright colors. It has super-bright designs. This showy patterning warns other animals. The warning says its skin is poisonous. Some native people in the Amazon rainforest have used the frog's poison for centuries. They use it when they go hunting. Paulo the Poison Arrow Frog was voted the most dangerous amphibian. Scientists divide the rainforest so they can understand it better.

4They describe four layers, or sections. You can think of it like a four-story building. The highest trees make up the top floor. That floor is called the emergent layer. The next-highest trees make up the canopy layer. The understory layer sits below those trees. The rainforest floor is the bottom layer. A diagram names the four layers in that same order. The tallest trees of the rainforest are the emergents. They are like big umbrellas. They rise as much as 60.9 meters above the thick forest. That height is 200 feet. Emergents are exposed to sun. They are exposed to wind. They are exposed to rain. They are exposed to lightning. These giant trees stand on big roots. Rainforest soil is shallow. Because of that, tree roots often grow above the ground. The kapok tree is deciduous. Deciduous means it sheds its leaves during the dry season. It has tall roots that extend out like wooden walls. These roots are so big that a whole class could hide behind just one of them. Not many animals live in the emergent layer. Some birds visit. Bats visit. Monkeys visit. Butterflies visit. They come to eat leaves and fruit. One huge bird actually lives up there. The bird is the harpy eagle. It lives high above the Amazon. An interview begins with the harpy eagle. The interviewer asks if the harpy is the largest eagle in the Americas. The harpy thinks so. In human measure, the harpy is about one meter tall. That height is more than three feet. The interviewer notices big wings. The harpy says the wings came at birth. Stretched out, they are longer than most people. The wingspan is two meters across. That span is about six and a half feet. Flying fast really helps the harpy find food. Asked for a favorite food, the harpy makes a joke. The joke is pizza brought to the door. There is no pizza in the rainforest. The real hunt is for sloths. The harpy also hunts monkeys. The harpy hunts fellow birds. Monkeys are probably the tastiest. The big talons do the grabbing. They are thirteen centimeters long. That length is about five inches. These meat hooks can scoop animals right off tree branches.

5The animals do not expect it. The harpy does not fear revenge. A monkey uprising is not a worry. The harpy is the top bird around here. The harpy builds stick nests high in the tallest trees. The nests sit 27 to 43 meters up. That height is about 90 to 140 feet. The harpy chooses trees with widely spaced branches. The spacing gives a clear flight path in and out of the nest. That way the harpy can see what is around. Monkeys do not normally go up that high. The harpy spots a monkey and leaves the talk. The rainforest's real action is in the canopy layer. This primary layer is where most of the rainforest plants live. Most of the animals live here too. It is like a thick roof. Tree branches, plants, and vines weave the roof. The roof covers the two remaining layers. About 80 percent of the sunlight in the rainforest is absorbed here. The trees in this layer take that light. Very little light gets to the lower layers. Many trees in the canopy are covered with climbing vines. They are covered with epiphytes. Epiphytes are plants that grow on top of other plants. They receive most of their moisture from the air. They receive most of their nutrients from the air. Most of the rainforest's flowers are found in this layer. Most of the fruit is here. Most of the seeds are here. With so much good canopy food, animals hang out here. Many animals rarely come down to the ground. They prefer to eat high up in the trees. They sleep, hunt, and give birth there. Many of them fly to get from tree to tree. Others jump, glide, or hop. Sloths live in the canopy. Toucans live there. Parrots live there. The hardest residents to ignore are probably the howler monkeys. Their name fits their behavior. These monkeys howl. They scream. The howls keep other animals out of their territories. They are the largest of the New World monkeys. An interview starts after one loud and distinctive howl. The interviewer asks the howler to stop screaming for a moment. The howler says to hurry. If the howling stops for too long, other monkeys may come in.

6They would enter the territory to claim the food. Asked if the group always stays in the canopy, the howler says this is home. On the ground there are some really big animals. It is a jungle down there. For safety, the monkeys stay up. They are herbivores. Up here they can eat fruit. They can eat leaves. They can eat flowers. They can live in safety. The interviewer asks about the long tail. It looks prehensile. The howler agrees that the tail can grab on. The tail helps the monkey climb. The hands are very useful for climbing too. The feet are useful for climbing too. The howler can be heard from five kilometers away. That distance is three miles. The interviewer asks what makes the voice so loud. The howler has a special bone in the windpipe. The throat is like a hollow sound box. A big swelling sits under the chin. The interviewer gets a sharp-toothed surprise when reaching to touch it. The howler is then free to howl again. A picture shows a howler monkey. The understory is the third level of the rainforest. It is below the canopy. It is above the ground. The ferns in this layer stay fairly short. The plants stay short. The young trees stay short. They rarely reach much more than 3.6 meters in height. That height is 12 feet. It is dark in the understory. Very little light shines through the thick canopy. A label marks this layer as the understory. Plants in the understory grow leaves that are big. The leaves are wide. Big leaves give a better chance of catching some rays. The flowers these plants produce are brightly colored. They emit strong scents. The scents attract pollinators. Birds are pollinators. Insects are pollinators. The understory is not nearly as busy as the canopy. Many animals do live here. Poison arrow frogs of about 220 species hide in the foliage. Venomous snakes wrap around trees like vines. Nonvenomous snakes do too. Birds peck insects from the wood. They pull insects from the wood. Large cats leap between branches. Cougars do this. Jaguars do this. An interview with the jaguar starts with a warning.

7The interviewer asks to be put down and not bitten. The jaguar apologizes. It thought the visitor was a monkey. It says the visitor is lucky not to be as tasty as a monkey. The jaguar asks what the visitor is doing here. The interviewer came to talk with the biggest cat in the Americas. The interviewer asks if the jaguar is really 2.6 meters long. That length is 8.5 feet. The jaguar says that is what they say. The jaguar weighs 136 kilograms. That weight is 300 pounds. Not many animals mess with the jaguar. The jaguar says to use the name Jag. Asked about exercise, Jag says one sport is boring. Jag cross-trains. Jag runs, swims, and climbs. Jag fishes and hunts. Asked about prey, Jag names a hiding trick. The spots are called rosettes. They camouflage Jag. Prey will not see Jag coming until it is too late. Sometimes Jag climbs up in the canopy. There Jag eats sloths and monkeys. On the ground, Jag hunts peccaries. Jag hunts tapirs. Jag hunts capybaras and calls them tasty. On lazy days, Jag lies near the river. Jag catches fish with claws. When Jag is really hungry, the hunt gets harder. Jag may want a challenge. Then Jag kills anacondas. Jag kills caimans. Jag is one big carnivore. The interviewer does not want to be in those powerful jaws again. The interviewer leaves. A walk on the rainforest floor might surprise you. The floor is not one solid thick jungle. You would see roots if you could see at all. You would see ferns, herbs, and baby trees. The light is almost complete darkness. Most of the green would be high above you. Earthworms eat the fallen fruit. Termites eat it. Fungi eat it. Bacteria eat it. They eat fallen leaves and branches. They recycle that matter back into nutritious soil. On the rainforest floor, plants decay quickly. When a big tree falls, the insects quickly munch it. They break it into small pieces. A leaf that falls from a tree decomposes fast here. It takes a mere six weeks in the rainforest. In other ecosystems it could take up to a year. Big animals live on the ground too. Giant anteaters live here.

8Deer live here. Tapirs, peccaries, and capybaras live here. They are far outnumbered by smaller creatures. Spiders outnumber them. Beetles outnumber them. Ants outnumber them. Highways of ants crisscross the rainforest floor. One kind of ant is famous. It is the leaf-cutter ant. The ants parade around with leaves in their mouths. They carry flower petals. They create trails. The trails stretch more than 30 meters across. That length is 98 feet. A trivia question asks what group is largest in the Amazon rainforest. The choices are mammals, birds, insects, or reptiles. The answer is insects. Mosquitoes alone come in more than two hundred types. Butterflies alone come in well over one thousand different kinds. The ants add still more. An interview calls down to a leaf-cutter. The ant is the one carrying the big leaf over its head. The interviewer asks for a moment. Ms. Ant can talk, but she must hurry. She needs to get back to the fungus garden. The colony is low on food. The interviewer asks if she really climbed a tree and cut off that leaf. She says that is correct. She is carrying it back to the underground colony. Smaller ants there will chew it up. They will make little spongy bits. A picture shows leaf-cutter ants. The interviewer asks if the ants eat the leaf itself. Ms. Ant says no. The ants are herbivores. They use the leaves in another way. The leaves are a substrate. On that surface they cultivate a special type of fungus. Then they eat the fungus. She offers a taste. The interviewer declines. Lunch just happened. The interviewer asks what it is like underground. The nests are deep. They have hundreds of chambers. The society is stratified. Different ants perform different jobs. They work in different places within the colony. In the middle rooms, smaller ants tend the fungus gardens. The only queen is in a different room. She lays up to thirty thousand eggs in one day. That is why almost five million ants live here. Ms. Ant must sniff her way home. She asks the visitor to watch those feet. A step on the trail might alter the scent. Then she might get lost. A picture shows bromeliads.

9They are cone-shaped plants that grow on trees. Their cones catch a lot of rainwater. The water forms little swimming pools high above the ground. Tiny frogs live in these pools. Salamanders live in them. Crabs live in them. Mosquitoes lay eggs in them. Dragonflies lay eggs in them. The last chapter turns to you. Do you eat bananas? Do you like chocolate? Has a bicycle with rubber tires ever carried you? Bananas grow in the Amazon rainforest. Chocolate grows there. Rubber grows there. Many more things come from the Amazon too. Pain medicines come from it. Cancer medicines come from it. Woods for furniture come from it. Pineapples and other fruits come from it. Fresh air comes from it. Think of the Amazon as a rich treasure that is alive. Some insects that buzz past do not even have names yet. Trees grow. Trees fall. They decompose into rich soil. Frogs' swimming pools fill up when rainstorms come. Plants of the rainforest do important air work. They take carbon dioxide from the air. They replace it with oxygen. About 24 percent of the oxygen in Earth's atmosphere comes from rainforests. Humans need oxygen in order to live. Other animals need it too. A real place holds the Amazon rainforest. Far away or not, the Amazon is alive at this very moment. As you read these words, a jaguar slinks through the understory. A leaf-cutter ant marches back to a fungus garden. The scream of a howler monkey echoes throughout the forest. Can you hear it? Many living things have their home in the Amazon rainforest. There is much more to see in the Amazon rainforest. Research is bound to turn up very interesting animals. A librarian can help you find books about the Amazon rainforest. You can also look up the Amazon River. You can look up the capybara. You can study the place where you live, with no web address required. You might live in a rainforest. You might live in a desert, a tundra, or a pine forest. You might live on the coast. You might live up in the mountains. You might live out on the plains. You must live somewhere. If you live in a town or a city, the region can be hard to name.

10Clues are all around. Look at the kinds of trees and bushes that grow nearby. Look at the kinds of birds that fly overhead. Notice the weather. If you are not sure, you can ask a librarian. You can ask a parent or a friend. Once you figure the region out, you can make a report. You can draw a picture of the layers of your own area. Ask which insects, plants, and animals are native to your home. You could find this by walking around outside. You could ask other people. You could read a book. You will probably be surprised by the cool things you learn about your neighbors. These words help a reader follow the forest. To decompose is to decay, rot, or break down into small parts. To emit is to send something out. Epiphytes are plants that grow on top of other plants. They are not rooted in soil. They get their nutrients from air and rain. Foliage means plant leaves. Nocturnal means active at night rather than during the day. Peccaries are nocturnal mammals that look like pigs. They are found in the Americas. Pollinators transfer pollen from flower to flower. Prehensile means able to hold or pick up things. To slither is to move smoothly from side to side over a surface. A species is a group of living things that are physically similar. Members of a species can reproduce. A substrate is the surface or layer on which a living thing grows or is attached. Tributaries are rivers or streams that flow into larger rivers. To trickle is to flow in a thin stream. Unsuspecting means unaware of potential danger. Venomous means able to inject venom. Venom is a poisonous fluid. An animal may inject it by striking, biting, or stinging. A topic list from this book names the arapaima and the canopy. It names the capybara, the catfish, and the colony. It names the dolphin, the emergent layer, and the frog. It names herbivores, humid air, and the jaguar. It names the kapok and the monkey. It names the sloth, subtropical forest, and the tapir. It names tropical forest, the understory, and the viper.

19. Oil Under the Arctic Wildlife Refuge

1Robert Marshall was the first person to propose a refuge in Alaska. Picture a place that belongs to every American. You might live your whole life and never walk on that far-off ground. Does a place you may never visit still matter to you? You have a way to help choose its future. The Arctic National Wildlife Refuge is that kind of place. It sits in a far corner of Alaska. A refuge gives shelter. A wildlife refuge gives wild animals space. It gives them freedom to feed themselves. It gives them freedom to raise their young. Because the land is protected, the animals can do these things without clashing with people. The United States has 553 national wildlife refuges. Together they cover more than 150 million acres. Some refuges are tiny. In Minnesota, Mille Lacs National Wildlife Refuge covers just .57 acres. No other unit in the National Wildlife Refuge System is smaller. Among those refuges, the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge ranks first in size. A photo shows a white ibis at the J.N. Ding Darling National Wildlife Refuge in Florida. A map of the far north shows the Beaufort Sea and Prudhoe Bay. It shows the 1002 Area. It shows Canada, Russia, Alaska, and the United States. The Arctic National Wildlife Refuge is huge. It covers nearly 20 million acres. South Carolina is nearly the same size. In 1938, a man named Robert Marshall could look at the still-wild places on the planet. He understood how people would change those places over time. He proposed a permanent American frontier. That frontier would preserve a big piece of arctic wilderness in Alaska. In the 1950s, people who worried about lost habitat asked Congress for that same frontier. They also worried about pollution. Congress set the refuge up in 1960. A map of the wider region shows the Pacific Ocean and Mexico. A photo shows a rainbow over the Brooks Range in the refuge.

2Many Americans see the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge, or ANWR, as a national treasure. Under its tundra lies a different kind of treasure. That treasure is oil. The oil splits Americans over the highest purpose of ANWR. It also splits them over what should happen to the land. People disagree about how much oil is there. They disagree about how much money that oil would save at the gas pump. They disagree about how drilling would affect the refuge. The argument, in the end, is about the source of ANWR's value. A century back, American wells supplied 60 to 70 percent of all oil on Earth. The Lower 48 provided most of it. Leave out Alaska and Hawaii, and the remaining states are the Lower 48. Once World War II ended, American wells could not cover what people and businesses needed. More oil had to be purchased abroad. At that time, oil was cheap. It was also plentiful. Few Americans paid attention to the amount of oil they burned. They cared little about where it came from. Then the energy crisis of 1973 arrived. Several countries made a joint choice to halt oil sales to America. The stop lasted six months. Oil became hard to get. Long lines of cars formed at the pumps. Sometimes a station had no fuel at all. Americans paid more for oil than they had thought possible only a year earlier. A photo from 1973 shows how short the gas supply was in the United States. Even when drivers could get gas, they were sometimes forced to wait at stations for hours. America needed fresh oil, and a new supply turned up. The find was at Prudhoe Bay, on Alaska's North Slope. Daily output from Prudhoe Bay reached two million barrels by 1980. Because of oil from Alaska, gas prices in the United States fell for a time. After 1988, yearly output from the North Slope kept falling.

3Oil companies now want to drill other oil-rich places near Prudhoe Bay. ANWR is one of those places. A photo shows an oil rig and a pipeline at Prudhoe Bay. A photo shows a person who speaks for Alaskan wilderness. The protest is held on the front steps of the Capitol. The protest is against drilling in ANWR. The law blocks any drilling inside ANWR. Oil firms hope to drill on a coastal plain of 1.5 million acres. A coastal plain is flat, low land. This plain sits next to the Beaufort Sea. It is known as the 1002 Area. A vote by Congress could allow the drilling. A change in the law is what some Americans ask of their leaders. Other Americans do not. In 1998, geologists studied this area. They wanted to estimate how much oil could be drilled. Their estimate put the oil there near 10.4 billion barrels. Estimates put the other drillable American oil at 120 billion barrels. That makes the oil estimated in the 1002 Area about 8.7 percent of all the oil inside the United States. Americans drive millions of cars. They also drive trucks, boats, and planes. The United States still has oil. It does not have enough oil to meet its own demand. It must buy the rest from many different countries. That bought oil is more than half of the oil the country uses each day. Each year, those purchases cost America hundreds of billions of dollars. A chart tracks a century of crude oil brought into the United States, measured in millions of barrels per day. The world uses 88 million barrels per day. The United States uses 19 million barrels per day. More than half of the oil used in the United States is imported. The chart scale runs from 0 up through 10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70, and 80 million barrels per day.

4Billions of dollars would flow to the federal government if ANWR oil and natural gas were developed. One estimate puts the government's gain near 200 billion dollars. As many as 65,000 jobs could come from drilling there. For some Americans, ANWR is a chance for greater independence from countries in the Middle East. It is also a chance for independence from other countries that sell oil to the United States. The United States counts Canada as a good friend. No other country currently sells America even half as much oil as Canada does. Much of the oil still left in the world does not sit with close friends of America. Some of the billions spent on oil from other lands help countries that are not friendly with the United States. During 1973, a few of those countries refused to sell America any oil. Oil from other countries can also be stopped while it is on the way. Each day, millions of barrels move in trucks. They move in tankers. They move in pipelines. This transport system gives easy targets to enemies of the United States. Those enemies might try to steal the oil. They might only try to keep it away from the United States. All of these points are strong arguments for drilling in ANWR. Next, consider how drilling might change the refuge. Like mining, or like any work that takes resources out of the earth, drilling makes a footprint. A mining footprint can be as drastic as the loss of a whole mountainside. A drilling footprint is made of wells, roads, and buildings. Those footprints are shrinking as the science of drilling improves. A modern drilling job at Prudhoe Bay would leave a smaller footprint. It could be as much as 64 percent smaller. The limit would still be 2,000 surface acres, even after a yes vote in Congress. Those acres sit inside the coastal plain of 1.5 million acres.

5The disturbed share of the refuge would stay under half of one percent. A photo shows the Porcupine Caribou herd crossing the Tamayariak River in ANWR. A photo shows a mother grizzly and her cubs. They use an oil pipeline as a path at Prudhoe Bay in Alaska. Every year, Porcupine Caribou pass through ANWR in a huge group. Their migration covers 930 miles. The journey stretches across Alaska and Canada. It is the longest migration of any land mammal on Earth. These caribou have their babies on the coastal plain of ANWR. Other caribou have done well beside the oil wells around Prudhoe Bay. They have lived with the pipelines, the roads, and the buildings there. The herd at Prudhoe Bay has grown since 1972. It had 3,000 animals then. Today it is ten times that number. Rules for oil and gas work in ANWR have already been written by Congress. To protect the land and the animals, some work would be limited to winter. That winter period runs from November to May. Springs would be protected from the water used in drilling. Streams and rivers would be protected too. Some people want to drill in ANWR. Other people do not. Even ANWR oil would not let America drill its way to energy independence. Energy independence would mean that oil imports stop. Every bit of needed energy would have to come from inside the country. The billions of barrels under the coastal plain of ANWR add up to less than a one-year supply for the country. At its peak, oil from ANWR would add only about 0.8 million barrels a day to the supply of the United States. Each day the country would still import about 10.6 million barrels of oil. Imports would shrink only a little if refuge oil came online. Oil from ANWR would also have very little effect on oil prices around the world.

6Two decades later, a large share of that oil might finally reach drivers. Even then, oil from ANWR would save Americans less than four cents on each gallon. The drilling rules written to protect the refuge are law. Making a law does not mean that every person will obey it. Laws also do not stop accidents. Shock spread in the summer of 2010 after 200 million gallons of oil poured into the Gulf of Mexico. People had thought that drilling for oil was safe. They had thought the Gulf was safe. Sadly, it was not safe. Major damage was done. Prudhoe Bay sits sixty miles west of ANWR. People who oppose drilling say Prudhoe Bay has been trashed. A thousand square miles of tundra now hold 1,500 miles of roads and pipelines. They hold 1,400 working wells. They hold three airports. Those opponents say mountains of waste spoil the land. Scrap metal and garbage spoil it too. More than sixty waste sites hold lead. They hold fuel. They hold other forms of pollution. The sites often leak. A photo shows crews lighting fires on the Gulf of Mexico. They hoped the fires would cut the amount of oil in the water. Another photo shows an oil production site at Prudhoe Bay from the air. New science would make the drilling footprint in ANWR smaller. Yet the limit of 2,000 acres may not be what it seems. The oil in the refuge is not in one single large deposit. It is spread across the coastal plain. It sits in more than thirty spots. A huge web of roads and pipelines would be needed to reach every spot. One estimate says that system would sprawl across 1.5 million acres. That much sprawl could break up the habitat of wildlife. Wildlife might be hurt. Or wildlife might do well, as the caribou herd around Prudhoe Bay has done.

7We have talked about oil in ANWR. The reasons the refuge was founded have nothing to do with energy. Those reasons do not come with price tags. They do not come with dollar signs. That does not mean the reasons are worth less. ANWR is beautiful. It is also special. A dozen rivers cross it. The sharp peaks of the Brooks Range mark one edge of it. It crosses a vast reach of tundra. It reaches the Beaufort Sea. Winter turns the refuge cold and hard, because the land lies so far north. Even so, this wilderness is important habitat for many animals. Threatened polar bears need the border where land meets sea. Grizzlies raise their babies there. Arctic wolves raise their babies there. The endangered shaggy musk ox raises its babies there. The musk ox comes from the last Ice Age. Hundreds more species of animals depend on the refuge. Hundreds more species of plants depend on it too. A photo shows a female polar bear and her cubs standing on ice in the coastal plain. Climate change has already had devastating effects on wildlife in the arctic. Extinction is now a real danger for the polar bear. If the founders of ANWR had known about climate change, they would have seen why the refuge matters for that study. Over the past few decades, the poles of Earth have warmed at twice the rate of the rest of the planet. ANWR is a specially valuable laboratory for studying climate change. It has many habitats. Those habitats include taiga forest and scrublands. They include alpine meadows and glaciers. They include tundra and coastline. Size matters, because climate change will shift the zones where plants grow. The refuge is large. Its topography is varied. That mix will let plant life shift as the climate shifts. It will let animal life shift too. Species can adapt and still stay inside a protected area.

8Scientists ask what will happen to tundra plants when the permafrost melts. The Arctic is where those scientists are gathering to seek answers. The refuge is also valuable for another reason. Unlike so many other places on Earth, even in the Arctic, people have not modified this refuge. A photo shows flowers of the Brooks Range in ANWR. People sometimes call ANWR the last great wilderness. Nowhere else in the country is a tract both this big and this free of human change. That is the very definition of wilderness. A place like this is unlikely to come our way again. Yet few of the people who fight for the refuge plan to go there. They simply want to know that such a place exists. They want it free of people. Olaus Murie, one of the founders, once said he wanted a little portion of our planet left alone. A photo from 1953 shows Mardy Murie and Olaus Murie, two founders of ANWR. Americans love a frontier. They also love oil. We have discussed some costs of that oil. We have not discussed every cost to the environment. Pollution is one of those costs. Climate change is another. Those costs would grow if drilling went ahead in ANWR. It will not make the United States independent in energy. Still, some people argue that any oil from the United States is better than none. Oil use around the world keeps rising. China and India are the two countries set to raise oil use the most over the next few years. Together, those two countries are home to a third of all the people in the world. Their growing demand meets a shrinking supply. That meeting reminds us of the real problem. The supply of oil will eventually end, whether the oil is foreign or American. One picture shows a packed Shanghai street. That city holds roughly 23 million people.

9The population of China is 1.3 billion. There are ways to make oil go farther. There are ways to make it last longer. Some Americans do not want Congress to change the law about ANWR. They want Congress to change a different law. The law sets the distance a new car must go on a single gallon. A vehicle that uses less gas to go from place to place is more fuel efficient. Each barrel now does twice the work it did for America in 1975. That trend can continue. Congress can require car companies in America to build vehicles that are very fuel efficient. By 2025, such vehicles could shrink the need for foreign oil by more than one-third. Corporate Average Fuel Economy standards, also called CAFE standards, set how many miles a vehicle can travel on one gallon. Since its start in 1975, the CAFE program has kept billions of barrels of fuel from being burned. Japan and Germany, among other countries, still build the cars that waste the least fuel. Some cars get 50 miles per gallon. That is far better than the CAFE average. The CAFE average is 27.3 miles per gallon, starting in 2011. Because a car that efficient uses so little fuel, it is also the least polluting. A car now being designed is meant to travel 235 miles on one gallon. A photo shows a farm of wind turbines. A single wind turbine can power 500 to 600 American homes. Oil from ANWR will not make oil last forever. Better fuel efficiency will not do that either. Sooner or later, the United States will run out of oil. The world will run out too. Luckily, life without oil does not have to mean life without energy. Americans have ways to become independent in energy. Those ways can last more than a year or two. The change toward other forms of energy has already begun.

10Some of those forms come from the wind. Some come from the Sun. They can last as long as people do. If the United States chooses them, it can become independent in energy. The job total could beat the number drilling in ANWR would bring. The planet can gain from that choice too. There is cause for hope. In the 1970s, much of the electricity in the United States came from oil. Oil now supplies only 2 percent of that electricity. Most cars in America still run on oil. That can change. A sun-powered electric car may be in your future. What do you think? Do you want drilling permitted in ANWR? Its future can reflect what you choose. You can write the president. You can write your representatives in Congress. You can tell them what you want. The energy path America takes in coming years is also yours to influence. Remember this. ANWR belongs to you. The future belongs to you too. A crisis is a dangerous or unstable time that demands attention. Independence means standing free of another group's control, influence, support, or help. Endangered means in danger of dying out completely. A migration is a living thing's repeated move from one place to another on a known schedule. To estimate is to calculate something in a rough way. To preserve is to take care of something and save it for the future. Fuel efficient describes a vehicle that makes good use of fuel, measured in miles per gallon. Sprawl is a disorganized spread of development into an area. Geologists are people who study the origin, the physical nature, the structure, and the history of Earth. Threatened means at risk of becoming endangered. Imports are goods brought in from another country or state. Tundra is a cold region with no trees, found in the Arctic, the Antarctic, and on high mountaintops.

20. Rails Across the Continent

1This book asks who would build a railroad across the country. It asks who would win the building race. It asks who would get through the mountains. It asks where the tracks would meet. A close and a word list finish the story. A picture shows settlers on a long road. Many of them died. Before the 1860s, a trip could take six months. The trip ran from New York City to San Francisco, California. Over those long months, people had to carry all their food. Or they had to hunt and gather food from the land. The trails west crossed the lands of Indigenous peoples. Those peoples had already had many negative dealings with outsiders. They had learned not to trust them. Even so, thousands of settlers traveled to California every year. The West Coast offered rich farmland. It offered wonderful weather. It offered gold. In that era, railroad travel was the fastest way to go. Railroads crossed the eastern United States. They reached as far west as Chicago, Illinois. They reached Omaha, Nebraska. A direct railroad across the United States would change the trip. Settlers could get to California much more quickly. Settlers in the West could reach the East Coast. They could order goods. They could send and receive mail. They could visit loved ones.

2People had wanted a transcontinental railroad for years. A transcontinental railroad crosses an entire continent. This one would cross North America. No one had built one. A railroad of that size cost too much for any one person to pay for. It cost too much for any one company. Engineers had no calculators. They had no computers. They had no airplanes to chart the land. Also, the Northern and Southern states were fighting. The fight was the Civil War. Agreement over a railroad was impossible. A picture shows Civil War soldiers and the rails. Soldiers often destroyed railroad lines as part of the conflict. They often repaired railroad lines too. In July of 1862, Congress passed the Pacific Railroad Act. The act declared that a transcontinental railroad must be built. The government broke treaties with Indigenous peoples. The break let the government give land to both companies. The land lay on either side of the proposed tracks. The companies sold the land. The sales helped pay for the railroad. They also hired immigrants. They underpaid Black people. Those choices cut costs. A picture shows immigrants at railroad work. They did much of the work for the railroad companies. The plan set the companies against each other. That contest made sure the railroad would be built quickly.

3The company that laid the most track would receive the most money. The railroad work continued even though many workers died. Some died from accidents. Some died from natural disasters. The Central Pacific started work in January 1863. The start was just outside Sacramento, California. The Civil War kept the Union Pacific Railroad from starting. Financial trouble kept it from starting too. Construction waited for two years. The war was taking up all the iron the railroad needed. It was taking the gunpowder. It was taking the workers. Those supplies were needed for building track. Work got underway soon after the war ended in 1865. By 1866, the race looked easy to call. It looked as if the Union Pacific would win. Getting ready to lay track took many complicated steps. First, surveying crews studied the land. They put stakes in the ground. The stakes marked exactly where the track would go. The second crew to go out was the graders. They removed any vegetation. They filled in any low spots. They dug to make a flat, smooth track. On the flat plains, graders often found little work waiting. A picture shows surveyors. They carefully measure distance and elevation. Another picture shows Chinese workers. They lay track in Utah in 1869. Several groups of workers were needed to lay the track.

4First, a team laid down wooden timbers. The timbers are called ties. The ties went across the track. After the ties, other workers dragged the heavy iron rails into place. Then another group pounded in iron spikes. They pounded in bolts. The spikes and bolts held the rails to the ties. Finally, a last group carried in sand and gravel. That mix is called ballast. Ballast filled in around the new tracks. Using this system, the Union Pacific could lay a lot of track. The crew could lay 2 to 3 miles in a single day. That length is 3 to 5 kilometers. Right behind all these crews, a train rolled onto the new track. It was the very first train to cross the plains. The train carried all the wood the crews needed. It carried rails, iron spikes, tools, and ballast. It also carried sleeping cars. It carried a kitchen and food storage. It carried repair shops. It carried goods such as clothing. It was like a rolling town. The town built itself as it went. The citizens of this working town came from many places. Most were immigrants from Ireland. Others came from Germany. Others came from the Netherlands. Civil War veterans worked there. Formerly enslaved Black people worked there.

5They built the railroad across the plains with incredible speed. They quickly reached the Rocky Mountains. The railroad devastated three tribes. The tribes were the Sioux, the Arapaho, and the Cheyenne. The American bison could not travel across the tracks. People also call the bison buffalo. Indigenous people depended on the bison for their existence. Settlers killed thousands of bison. The animals were almost gone. The Indigenous peoples of the plains could not survive once the bison were gone. Tribes lost many people. Disease came with the killing of the bison. Hunger came. Conflict came. A picture shows settlers hunting bison. The hunt nearly drove the animals to extinction. A flood of settlers also reached the plains by railroad. The railroad companies sold tribal land to the settlers. The land belonged to the Sioux, the Arapaho, and the Cheyenne. The settlers began farming there. They began building their homes there. The trains also brought litter. They brought noise. They brought air pollution. They brought prairie fires. Sparks from the engines caused the fires. The engines burned wood or coal. A picture shows Indigenous people and a train. They raided some trains. They were trying to halt the railroad. As the Union Pacific advanced across the West, Indigenous people fought back. They fought the illegal seizing of their land.

6They stole supplies. They stole livestock. They even killed some workers. The Union Pacific demanded protection. The company wanted the army to protect the workers and the railroad. Soon, the U.S. government sent soldiers onto the plains. The soldiers engaged in many battles with Indigenous people. A picture shows workers in the mountains. They built the railroad through the rugged Sierra Nevada range. Work on the Central Pacific was going more slowly. Right away, the company had to cut a track into the Sierra Nevada. The mountains were steep. They were snowy. Trains at the time could not climb steep hills. They could not go around sharp corners. The workers had to change the jagged mountains. They had to make a smooth trail that rose gently. The workers cut away the solid granite mountainsides. They filled in gorges with rocks and soil. They filled in ravines the same way. They moved the rock wheelbarrow by wheelbarrow. It was slow, painful work. Very few people wanted to do it. Within a week, almost all the white men hired by the Central Pacific Railroad had quit. The Central Pacific reluctantly hired some Chinese workers. The company wanted to see if they would do better. Chinese workers faced racism. They faced discrimination. Yet Chinese workers often lasted a long time on the job.

7Their cleanliness helped them last. Their healthy habits helped them last. Chinese workers were most of the Central Pacific workforce by 1868. The railroad had to get over the Sierra Nevada. The Chinese workers had to cut fifteen tunnels. The tunnels went through solid rock. They used only hand drills. They used sledgehammers. They used loose gunpowder. Through the winter, snowstorms buried the mountains. The total was forty-four storms. The workers built tunnels under the snow. They built sleeping areas. They built workspaces. The snow averaged 18 feet deep. That depth is 5.5 meters. All their supplies had to be brought up the side of the mountain. Ox wagons hauled some supplies. Other supplies came by hand. A picture shows Chinese workers loading ballast. The ballast fills in the area beneath a track. The Chinese workers had a famous accomplishment. They cut a track into the side of an enormous cliff. They called the cliff Cape Horn. People who wrote about the project later described the method. Workers rode in woven reed baskets. A basket was big enough to hold two people. From the top of the cliff, the baskets were lowered with the workers inside. The workers drilled holes into the sides of the cliff. They stuffed the holes with black powder.

8They lit a fuse. The workers at the top hauled up the baskets as fast as they could. They hoped to pull everyone out of the way. They needed to do it before the cliff was blown apart. With these accomplishments came tragedy. Accidents crushed some workers. The cold weather froze many to death. Avalanches buried entire groups of people alive. Some were not found until spring. The Central Pacific Railroad did not keep a count. It did not record how many Chinese laborers were killed on this railroad. Some accounts estimate a number. They estimate that around 1,200 died. Finally, in 1867, the track broke through the mountains. The Central Pacific moved onto the deserts of Utah. A picture shows Chinese workers. They played an important part in building the transcontinental railroad. The Union Pacific had an easier mountain crossing. Crossing the Rocky Mountains was easier than the Central Pacific's work in the Sierra Nevada. The Union Pacific took a gentle route. The route was called the South Pass. They also moved quickly onto the deserts of Utah. On May 10, 1869, a crowd gathered. The place was Promontory Point, Utah. Workers came. Railroad owners came. Journalists came. Politicians came. They gathered for the completion of the transcontinental railroad. The race was over.

9The Union Pacific had laid 1,086 miles of track. That length is 1,748 kilometers. The Central Pacific had laid 689 miles. That length is 1,109 kilometers. The Central Pacific got extra money. The extra pay was for building through the Sierra Nevada. A ceremony marked the first railroad to cross the United States. The heads of the two companies would drive the last spikes. There were four last spikes. Special spikes were made of gold. Special spikes were made of silver. Leland Stanford swung a sledgehammer. He was the head of the Central Pacific. Thomas Durant swung a sledgehammer. He was the head of the Union Pacific. Each man missed. The crowd cheered anyway. The transcontinental railroad was complete. A map shows the route of the transcontinental railroad. A key marks the Central Pacific Railroad and the Union Pacific Railroad. The map names Promontory Point. It names Wyoming, Nebraska, and Omaha. It names Utah, Nevada, and Sacramento. It names Colorado and California. After all the years of labor and danger, travel times changed. California was only a few days' travel away from New York. Railroads stretched to Salt Lake City. They stretched to Oregon Territory. They stretched to the Southwest. More towns sprang up along the tracks. Goods could travel easily. Mail could travel easily.

10The railroad companies made millions of dollars. The money came from the sale of Indigenous land. The laborers never got any further payment. Yet a link was new. For the first time, a transcontinental railroad connected the country from east to west. These words help a reader follow the build. Ballast is a heavy material used to increase stability. Railroad crews place it around the foundation of the tracks. Indigenous means of or related to the first peoples to live in a place. Laborers are people who do physical work for payment. Discrimination is unfair treatment of a person or a group. It is based on gender, race, age, religion, or other differences. Settlers are people who make a new, permanent home on a frontier. Engineers are people who design, build, or repair large works. Those works include machines, buildings, bridges, or other structures. Racism is unequal treatment. It is based on the belief that some races are better than others. Transcontinental means extending across a continent. Financial means of or relating to matters dealing with money. Immigrants are people who come to live in a new country. They come especially for the purpose of settling there. Vegetation means the plants found in a certain area. Veterans are people who served in the armed forces.

21. Riders Who Made the Largest Land Empire

1In the 1200s, Mongol people moved from place to place in East Asia. Those nomads built the largest land empire ever created. The empire began when many tribes worked together. Genghis (JEHNG-gihs) Khan was their leader. At its largest, the empire reached across Europe and Asia. It lasted from 1206 to 1368. People could trade across Eurasia. Technology could travel that far too. Goods moved from one side of the empire to the other. Ideas moved along the same routes. A map of this story shows Europe, Asia, India, and the Indian Ocean. One part of the map marks land held by Genghis Khan in 1227. Another part marks land held by his heirs in 1279. A modern map of this region shows Russia and the city of Ulaanbaatar. It also shows Kazakhstan, Mongolia, and China. The Pacific Ocean, India, and the Indian Ocean appear on that map too. The Mongols were a nomadic people. Their number may have been about two million. They lived in felt tents called yurts. The tents stood on the enormous steppes of northeast Asia. A steppe is a grassland. That land is known today as Mongolia. The people were divided into tribes. A leader called a khan headed each tribe. Mongols were expert horse riders. They were expert warriors as well. Hunting was part of daily life. Fighting other clans was part of that life too. Fighting was necessary because resources were scarce. The livestock needed plenty of grass. The animals also needed plenty of water. Tribes traveled far to find grass and water. They made those trips often. Nomadic life was difficult. It was dangerous too. In some years, dry weather left few resources. Farmers then found it hard to feed the tribe. Later, the leadership of Genghis Khan would bring stability to these tribes. In 1162, a boy named Temujin was born. His mother was the wife of a Mongol chieftain. That chieftain came from a noble line. Temujin was nine when Tartars killed his father. The Tartars were a Turkish group.

2For most of his youth, Temujin helped his family survive. They had to live without his father on the harsh Mongol steppes. By his teen years, Temujin showed great intelligence. He also showed strong skill as a leader. He was ruthless when he defeated enemies. Temujin grew up and took the post of khan over the tribe he came from. He made alliances with other Mongol tribes. He built up an army. He trained that army to be a disciplined war machine. He then used the army to defeat every Mongol tribe that refused to join him. A work of art from the fourteenth century, made by Rashid al-Din, shows Temujin at the moment he became khan. People usually call the Mongol leader Genghis Khan. For a long time, the first part of the title has been spelled in more than one way. Genghiz is one of those spellings. Jenghis is another. Lately, several historians argue that every one of those spellings gets the sound wrong. They say Chingis is a better spelling. They also say Chinngis is a better spelling. People have also argued about what the title means. Many scholars think the name meant Universal Ruler. Other scholars do not agree. One suggested meaning is Invincible Prince. Another suggested meaning is Spirit of Light. A Chinese artist made a woodcut picture of Genghis Khan. By 1206, every Mongol group accepted Temujin as leader. The leaders of the tribes met that year. They proclaimed him Genghis Khan. The title meant Universal Ruler. One of his first acts was to issue a set of laws. The laws were meant to make the Mongols stop fighting one another. They were also meant to join the Mongols into one nation. Genghis Khan set up a military government. He led the Mongols out to conquer other lands. In 1209, he led his forces toward China. Some people in Mongolia still live in tentlike houses called yurts. Those yurts stand on the Mongolian steppes. When Genghis Khan rose to power, China was split into three major parts.

3The west held the Xi Xia (SHEE SHEE-yah) Empire. The north held the Jin Empire. The south held the Song Dynasty. In 1209, Xi Xia fell to Genghis Khan. He then attacked the Jin. In 1215, ferocious battles left many Chinese people dead. The Mongol army entered the Jin capital, Zhongdu. Zhongdu is the city now called Beijing. The army plundered Zhongdu. They left the city a smoking ruin. Mongol control then covered every Chinese land other than the Song Dynasty. Genghis Khan decided that taking the Song could wait. He turned toward the vast lands west of China. He was seeking wealth. His armies would soon spread havoc through that part of the world. In the years that followed, the Mongols beat every army they met. Any city that tried to keep them out was forced open. They conquered enormous territories. People asked why these armies were so hard to stop. The Mongol armies were large, but they were not gigantic. They usually totaled about 100,000 soldiers. The highest estimate for their size is 250,000. In most battles, the forces were divided. They struck enemies who were far apart from each other. That often left the Mongols greatly outnumbered. They won anyway. Historians believe training made the Mongols unbeatable. Discipline did too. They also used clever plans. They used military tools that were advanced for that time. This army was the first to shoot arrows while riding a moving horse. The soldiers lived off the land. That choice helped the armies move fast. They did not need long supply trains. People in Mongolia today show pride in this past. They act out important battles again. In open fighting, Mongols stayed on horseback. They wore armor made of leather. Their helmets were made of leather and metal. One main weapon was a small bow with great power. It could send arrows with strong force. It could also send them with high accuracy. For fighting up close, they used lances.

4They used battle axes. They used short curved swords called scimitars (SIM-ihtahrs). They also used weapons that needed gunpowder. Simple grenades were among those weapons. Firelances were among them too. A firelance was a bamboo tube that shot out flames and sparks. Archery belongs to Mongolian history and culture. It is still a popular sport. The Mongols learned from the Chinese how to make gunpowder. They also learned how to break city walls. They hired Chinese engineers. Those engineers built huge machines called siege engines. They operated the machines as well. Catapults were one kind of device. A catapult could hurl heavy rocks. Ballistas were another kind. A ballista fired huge arrows. No city could stand up to the pounding from these siege engines. Genghis Khan always tried to offer a deal to places he wanted to take. The offer had two sides. A place that surrendered would be spared. A place that resisted would be wiped out. A city that opened its gates was left unharmed. That city only had to pay tribute. Wealth was the thing Genghis Khan wanted most. He was content to get wealth without a fight. Cities that stayed behind their walls were punished. Punishment also fell on any city that marched soldiers out to battle. After the army broke in, the Mongols pillaged the city. Then they burned the city to the ground. They took as captives any people they thought could be useful. They killed everyone else. One picture shows an older Genghis Khan standing in a garden. This way of ruling created terror across a wide area. Because of that fear, many cities gave up at once. A map of the Mongol Empire in 1227 shows Europe and Asia. It marks Lake Baikal. It marks the Mongol homeland. It marks Liaoyang. It marks Zhongdu, also called Beijing. It marks the Caspian Sea and the Aral Sea. It marks Lake Balkhash. It marks the Yellow Sea. It marks the Empire of Khwarizm. Datong is marked as a place the Mongols conquered in 1221.

5The map also shows the Kara-Khitai Empire. It shows Urgench, Ningxia, and Balasaghun. It shows Samarkand. It shows the place where Genghis Khan died. It shows Bukhara, Kashgar, and Merv. It shows Bamian, Herat, and Peshawar. It shows the Southern Song Empire. It shows Multan. Lines on the map trace Mongol military routes. For a dozen years after the campaign in China, the Mongols took everything in their path. Genghis Khan led some of those armies. His four sons led armies too. In 1227, Genghis Khan turned sixty-five. By then the Mongols had built an empire. One end lay on China's northern east coast. The other end lay at the Persian Gulf. The Persian Gulf is the western edge of present-day Iran. The distance was about 5,600 kilometers. That is 3,500 miles. Across that huge area, vassal states paid tribute every year. Later conquests would make the empire still larger. Genghis Khan would not share in those later wars. His life was nearing its end. Genghis Khan died in the summer of 1227. He was leading another attack on Xi Xia. That Chinese vassal state was being punished. It had refused to send troops for a recent Mongol campaign. No one is sure what caused his death. Some historians argue that illness killed him. Others argue that he died while fighting. Others argue that he died while hunting. A tortoise statue marks one corner of Karakorum, the old Mongol capital. Soldiers put his body in a felt coffin. Three golden straps held the coffin closed. They carried the coffin back to Mongolia. The great leader was buried in a place kept secret. A Mongol legend tells what happened next. Horse riders, eight hundred of them, crossed the grave again and again. They did this to hide the site. Then those riders were killed. Everyone else who knew the gravesite was killed too. After that, no living person could reveal the burial place. Genghis Khan left the empire to his four sons. A wide territory went to every son, and each son would rule it as a khan.

6He wanted his third son to stand above the others. That son was Ogadei (oh-GAHday-ee). In 1229, the Mongols officially named Ogadei the Great Khan. Ogadei set a capital on the steppes. He built a palace there. Strong walls of black stone surrounded the palace. He called the capital Karakorum. The name meant Black Stones. It could also mean Black Walls. A painting from the fourteenth century shows Ogadei Khan. Ogadei began a new run of conquests. Mongol armies brought Russia under their control. They also took other parts of eastern Europe. In 1241, they were close to invading western Europe. Then Ogadei died. The invasion stopped until a new Great Khan could be chosen. A Persian manuscript from the 1300s shows Mongol soldiers around Baghdad, in Iraq, in 1258. Ogadei's death came only after his three brothers had already died. Grandsons of Genghis Khan fought hard over the title of Great Khan. Turmoil filled the Mongol world across a stretch of ten years. Order came back in 1251. A grandson called Mongke received the title Great Khan during that year. Mongke started the conquests again. He sent his brother Hulegu to bring areas of Central Asia under control. Hulegu was also sent into West Asia. In 1258, Hulegu attacked Baghdad. Baghdad was the Muslim capital in the land that is now Iraq. When the city fell, the army tore it apart. The soldiers destroyed the Grand Library. They destroyed its priceless artifacts. They killed many people. Baghdad stayed a ruin for several centuries. One force stayed in West Asia. A second force was busy inside China. Mongke was set on destroying the Song Dynasty at last. That was the empire Genghis had never defeated. Kublai, another brother of Mongke, led the attack on China. The campaign was going badly. Mongke joined the fight. In 1259, he died suddenly. A small group of Mongols then named Kublai the new Great Khan. People came to call him Kublai Khan.

7Most Mongols never accepted him as the top leader. They thought he was growing less Mongolian. They thought he was growing more Chinese. Unity inside the Mongol world was starting to slip away. In 1294, the Nepalese artist Araniko painted Kublai Khan. A major defeat made matters worse. A battle in Galilee in 1260 ended in defeat for one Mongol army. Galilee is part of present-day Israel and Lebanon. That loss ended Mongol conquests in the West. In China, Kublai Khan kept working to conquer the Song Dynasty. In 1264, he turned away from Karakorum, the capital in Mongolia. He settled in China and stayed there. He placed a new Mongol capital in northern China. It stood where Zhongdu, the ruined Jin capital, had been. He named the new city Khanbalik. The name meant City of the Khan. A huge palace rose in that city. In 1279, Kublai Khan won a last victory over the Song Dynasty. That same year, he founded a new dynasty to rule China. It was called the Yuan (yoo-WAHN) Dynasty. Mongol rulers now held every part of China. Marco Polo was a famous merchant and traveler from Italy. He spent seventeen years in China. Those years were 1275 to 1292. Kublai Khan was ruling then. Marco Polo was amazed by the capital, Khanbalik. Khanbalik is also spelled Cambaluc. The palace of the Great Khan impressed him most. He called it the greatest palace that ever was. He reached Khanbalik by the Silk Road. The Silk Road was a trade path leading from Europe to China. Kublai Khan settled into life as the ruler of China. He was still khan of all the Mongols. He no longer cared much about the rest of the empire. He gave all his time to the Yuan Dynasty. His government mostly kept native Chinese people out of power. Mongols filled the best jobs. Other foreigners filled those jobs too. One picture shows his Mongolian army attacking ships from Japan. Kublai Khan still wanted more land.

8He stretched his rule over some areas south of China. He did not manage to take Japan, a nation of islands. Two times, he sent large fleets toward Japan. Terrible storms destroyed both fleets. He dropped his plan to pull Japan into the empire. Conquests in the east were coming to a close. Conquests in the west were ending too. Set side by side, today's United States, Canada, Mexico, and Central America would still be smaller than that empire. For a time, Mongol rule left the world at peace. A map of the Mongol Empire in 1294 shows the Russian principalities and Lake Baikal. It shows Koryo. It shows the Kipchak Khanate, also called the Golden Horde. It shows Zhongdu, or Beijing. It shows the Black Sea and Karakorum. It shows the Yellow Sea, the Aral Sea, and Lake Balkhash. It shows the Great Khanate and the Yuan Dynasty. It shows the Caspian Sea. It shows the Chagatai Khanate and the city of Samarkand. It shows Hangzhou, Baghdad, Hezhou, and Chengdu. It shows the Il-Khanate. It shows the Southern Song Empire, Tibet, and Dali. It shows the Red Sea. It shows Thang Long, the city now called Hanoi. Kublai Khan died in 1294. Even before that death, the Mongol Empire had been fragmenting. The realm was simply too huge to be run from one place. When Kublai Khan died, the empire fell apart. It split into four large parts called khanates. Each khanate had its own khan. There was no longer one Great Khan. The largest khanate was the Yuan Dynasty, with its center in China. The Il-Khanate covered Persia and the land that is now Iraq. The Chagatai Khanate lay in central Asia. The Kipchak Khanate lay in Russia. People later called the Kipchak Khanate the Golden Horde. The khanates kept ties with one another. Even so, each one was independent. One after another, they were overthrown. Craftspeople traveled with the Mongol armies. Those workers helped form the nomadic communities. A Mongolian horsehead fiddle is a stringed instrument from that world.

9Paper money from the Yuan Dynasty of Kublai Khan is another example. Both things were adapted by people the Mongols had conquered. They became part of those other cultures. Pictures show a horsehead fiddle. They show a maker carving a horsehead to set on top of a fiddle. They also show paper money from the Yuan Dynasty. After the years of fighting ended, the Mongols were no longer hardened warriors. They grew corrupt. They grew weak. The people under their rule could sense that weakness. Those people revolted. Persia's Il-Khanate fell in 1335, and it was the earliest Mongol khanate to be overthrown. The Yuan Dynasty fell next. The revolt that won was led by Zhu Yuanzhang (Joo You-an Jung). In 1368, he founded a new Chinese ruling family. That family was the Ming Dynasty. Mongol power made its final stand with the Golden Horde. That group ruled Russia, Ukraine, and other lands in the northwest of the old empire. A Russian prince named Ivan III finally overthrew the Golden Horde in 1480. When the Golden Horde ended, the Mongols were defeated. In 2006, the president of Mongolia, N. Enkhbayar, unveiled a statue of Genghis Khan. The statue was part of a festival meant to draw tourists to Mongolia. A mausoleum holds objects from the reign of Genghis Khan. The place where he was buried is still unknown. People in Mongolia remember the empire with pride. They give special honor to the memory of Genghis Khan. He is a national hero. Historians and many peoples remember the conquests as a time of great bloodshed. Guesses about the number killed run from four million people to sixty million people. Genghis Khan is often named among the greatest conquerors in history. People say this because of his success in war. They also say it because of the many lands he took. Some historians also say the Mongol Empire brought good things to the world. They note that the empire, once it was in place, brought peace across Asia.

10It brought peace across the Middle East as well. People call this time the Mongol Peace. During the Mongol Peace, travelers could cross all of Asia in safety. The Mongols allowed people to follow their own religions. They encouraged trade. That trade helped people in the large cities of Europe. It helped people in Central Asia and East Asia. It also helped people in isolated places. Those groups could learn more about one another. The Mongol Peace encouraged people to share technology. It encouraged them to share new ideas. Those exchanges had good effects on Europe. Some basic parts of modern technology came out of the Mongol Empire. Some basic parts of modern warfare came from it too. Archaeologists have followed the path of gunpowder into Europe. They have followed printing the same way. They have followed other tools as well. This sharing happened while the Mongols ruled. Medicine grew stronger as doctors traveled widely through the empire. Science grew stronger too. Should the world feel thankful for the Mongol invasions? Like much of history, this question is complex. It has no easy answer. An alliance is a close link between countries or other groups that work as partners. Havoc means destruction, chaos, or disorder. Isolated describes something far from other people or things. Archaeologists are scientists who study what ancient cultures left behind. Nomadic describes people who move from place to place and keep no permanent home. A bastion is a stronghold, a place that is well protected. To plunder is to steal everything of value by force. A dynasty is a line of rulers in one country who come from the same family. Tribute is a payment one group gives another, often for protection or to avoid war. An empire is a set of nations or peoples ruled by one person who holds total authority. Vassal states are governments run by their own people but controlled in politics or war by a stronger power. Fragmenting means breaking apart into smaller pieces.

22. Riding the Wind

1This book begins with air and flight. It explains hang gliding and how a pilot starts. It tells what the sport takes. It then explains skydiving and how a parachute works. It tells what that sport takes. It ends with parasailing, a word list, and a way to look further. A picture shows skydivers. They jump from an airplane. Look, up in the sky. Is it a huge bird? Is it Superman? No. It is a regular person flying high. People call that catching air. The person may be hang gliding. The person may be skydiving. The person may be parasailing. Maybe you would like to try one of these sports. Wind powers them. You could soar through the wide-open skies. You could swoop through them. You need a sense of adventure. You need the right equipment. You need an understanding of how wind works. First, let us look at air. You cannot exactly see air. Air is a collection of gases. You cannot really feel air unless it is moving. Air has weight. Air has temperature. Air takes up space. That space is its volume. Air moves around. When air heats up, it becomes lighter. Then it rises. Air becomes wind in a simple way. Cooler, heavier air rushes in. It replaces the warmer, lighter air that is rising. A light wind can move leaves. A heavy wind can move the whole tree. A picture shows why hot-air balloons rise. The heated air inside the balloon is a higher temperature than the cooler air outside. People who love wind sports pay attention to their natural surroundings. They know that areas of land heat up faster than areas of water. They need moving air. So they like tall slopes. They like cliffs. Warm air rises from land. A cooler wind from the sea rushes in to replace it. When this wind strikes the face of a cliff, the cliff turns the wind. The wind is deflected upward. This strong updraft of air creates lift. The ride is a great one. Often, wind-sport riders look to birds for flying advice.

2Hawks may circle lazily overhead. Seagulls may do the same. They are riding a rising column of air. That column is called a thermal. Smart wind-sport riders will also try to catch thermals. They want the ride. A diagram shows wind, an updraft, and a thermal. Wind-sport riders catch updrafts. The updrafts give them a boost into the sky. Picture a hot summer afternoon. The sun sits high in the midafternoon sky. From the sea, a gentle breeze blows in. A woman runs down a slope. A triangular winged contraption rests on her shoulders. She leaps from the cliffs. The cliffs rise about 915 meters above the shore. That height is 3,000 feet. Yikes. What is she doing? The wing fills with air. It billows out. Up she goes. The pilot has stretched out flat. She hangs from a harness. The harness is like a cocoon. It is attached to the wing. She pushes or pulls back on a long metal control bar. That is how she climbs up or dives down. She leans to turn left or right. The edge of the wing tips down. The glider banks. It turns gracefully through the air. A picture shows a launch. The pilot leaves a cliff-top ramp and sails over the beach far below. A diagram explains the wing. Air moving over the top of a wing moves farther than air under the wing. It also moves faster. She is hang gliding. She may sail in the sky for hours if conditions are good. She also needs to understand a few principles. The study of how objects move through the air is called aerodynamics. Aerodynamics is said air-oh-die-NAM-iks. The special shape of the wing produces lift. Lift comes as the glider moves through the air. The curved top surface of a wing is a longer distance than the straight underside. Air moving over the longer, curved top has to move faster. It moves faster than the air moving under the wing. As that top air speeds up, the air molecules spread out. The spread decreases the air pressure.

3The greater air pressure underneath the wing creates lift. That lift causes the wing to rise. To take off, the pilot runs down a steep slope. The run builds speed. It creates airflow over the wing. It creates lift for the glider. He is off. He hangs from the harness. Some pilots use a special chair seat. The pilot steers along the winds. He catches one updraft and rides it. Then he catches another. This is not a place to relax and daydream. The pilot has to pay attention. He turns. He circles. He climbs. He dives. When the pilot pulls the control bar back, the glider gains speed. It loses altitude. Altitude means height. A steady drop comes with forward motion. For every 1.22 meters the glider moves forward, it also drops about 30 centimeters. That forward distance is about 4 feet. The drop is 1 foot. When he pushes the control bar forward, the wing edge tips up. The glider rises. It loses speed. Earth's gravity pulls downward in a natural way. Gravity will pull the hang glider down if it stalls. A stall means the glider stops moving forward. The pilot keeps the glider moving forward in order to stay up. Forward motion lets the pilot keep riding along the wind. A picture shows the glider's harness. It keeps the pilot's body straight. A straight body cuts wind resistance. A gear list tells what hang gliding takes. The equipment includes a harness, a helmet, and goggles. It includes a parachute and a glider. It includes flotation gear if the flight is near water. Clothing should be a heavy jacket, jeans, and gloves. Higher altitudes can feel very cold. Air temperature falls as you go up. It falls by about 2.2 degrees Celsius about every 300 meters. That drop is 4 degrees Fahrenheit about every 1,000 feet. Harnesses are designed for a set size. They fit a person who is 1.5 to 2 meters tall. That height is 5 to 6 and a half feet. They fit a person who weighs 40 to 113 kilograms.

4That weight is 90 to 250 pounds. The pilot must be able to carry a heavy load on the shoulders while jogging. The load is 18 kilograms or more. That weight is 40 pounds. Balance matters more than strength. Endurance matters more than speed. Good reflexes are a must. So is an ability to make fast decisions. A pilot must be age 16 or older. The pilot must be able to pass a training course. The course lasts three to six months. The cost to train and to purchase the equipment is $5,000 to $6,500. People have been fascinated by the dream of flight for thousands of years. A Greek myth tells of an inventor. His name is Daedalus. Daedalus is said DEH-duh-lus. The myth also tells of his son, Icarus. They were imprisoned on an island. Their only way out was to fly. Daedalus watched birds. Then he fashioned wings for himself. He fashioned wings for his son. Feathers made the wings. Wax held the feathers together. They escaped. But Icarus ignored his father's cautious warning. He flew too high in the sky. The heat of the sun melted the wax. Icarus fell to the sea. He drowned. Icarus is a good story. It is not good science. People could never flap winged arms fast enough. They could not generate enough power. The power would have to overcome the pull of gravity. A picture shows Lilienthal's first glider. A side note tells of a discovery in the 1800s. German inventor Otto Lilienthal studied wings. He found that a properly engineered wing would help a person stay aloft. The person could stay up for hours at a time. The Lilienthal glider from around 1895 is designed like a bird's wing. It has a curved upper surface. The Wright Brothers used information from Lilienthal's experiments. They used it to invent the first airplane. A list of hang glider facts follows. The longest hang glider ride lasted 36 hours. Hang gliders typically travel 160 to 320 kilometers. That distance is 100 to 200 miles.

5The farthest flight on record is just more than 700 kilometers. That distance is 435 miles. The flight was set on July 17, 2001. The place was Zapata, Texas. The pilot was Austrian Manfred Ruhmer. Flight heights can range widely. They can be as low as 1.5 meters above the ground. That height is 5 feet. They can be more than 4.8 kilometers high. That height is 3 miles. A small plane buzzes overhead. At an altitude of 3,048 meters, the passenger door opens. That height is 10,000 feet. A person crouches in the doorframe. The person leaps out. Arms and legs spread wide. Within 10 seconds, the skydiver is free-falling. The speed is 193 kilometers per hour. That speed is 120 miles per hour. That is twice as fast as a car zooming by on a highway. Astonished, you see the skydiver drop sharply. The drop continues. It goes on for the next 45 seconds. You think the parachute should open now. Finally, the skydiver pulls the ripcord. The height is about 762 meters above the ground. That height is 2,500 feet. Out comes the small drogue chute. The drogue pulls out the canopy. The canopy is the larger chute. It fills with air. The skydiver is jerked upward. He continues to fall. Now the fall is a leisurely 16 to 20 kilometers per hour. That speed is 10 to 20 miles per hour. For the next 3 minutes, he enjoys the view. He enjoys the ride. Then he lands on his feet in a clearing. A picture shows a skydiver at the start of her free fall. Gravity pulls the free-falling skydiver down toward Earth. Speeds reach 193 kilometers per hour or more. That speed is 120 miles per hour. When the parachute opens, it fills with air. It slows the skydiver's speed. Air trapped in the canopy presses up against the chute. The canopy is the umbrella part. The air provides resistance. Resistance slows the fall. The skydiver glides to Earth. A small test shows the same idea. You need a four-holed button.

6You need four strands of thread. Each strand is 15 inches long. You need scissors, a plastic bag, and tape. Cut a square from the plastic bag. The square is 20 centimeters on each side. That length is 8 inches. Fasten one end of each thread strand to a corner of the plastic with tape. Pass each strand through its own hole in the large button. Tie the threads together. Gently hold the top side of the plastic in its center. You can pinch it. Let the button dangle. Drop your homemade parachute from a high spot. A deck edge will do. See the button glide down to the ground. A picture shows a diamond formation. Skilled skydivers can free-fall in formations. They hold their arms and legs outstretched. The position is called spread-eagle. They join hands. This pose decreases their free-fall speed. Multiple bodies provide more wind resistance. The divers can float for a longer time. Skydivers can also increase free-fall speed. They learn to fall in a standing position. That position is vertical. Now that you know how a skydive unfolds, a new question comes. What is next? Experienced skydivers compete in events. People also call them sport parachutists. They compete in landing accuracy. They compete in free-fall speed. They compete in freestyle. They put on shows. They do stunts in the air. They may pass a baton to each other. They may create group formations in the sky. A gear list tells what skydiving takes. The equipment includes a jumpsuit, a helmet, and goggles. It includes a main parachute and a reserve parachute. It includes a harness with an attached backpack. The backpack holds both parachutes. An automatic activation device can open the reserve chute. It opens if something goes wrong. The skydiver needs enough strength for the gear. The gear weighs about 16 kilograms. That weight is 35 pounds. The skydiver must control the toggle switches that steer the chute. The skydiver must be able to deal with the shock of the chute opening. The skydiver must deal with extreme changes in temperature.

7Harnesses fit a set size. They fit a person who is 1.5 to 2 meters tall. That height is 5 to 6 and a half feet. They fit a person who weighs 40 to 113 kilograms. That weight is 90 to 250 pounds. A student must be age 16 or older. The student must be in good shape. The student must pass eight training levels. The levels include many jumps with instructors. A student must make 20 solo jumps.
