A student must pass a written test. The cost for training and equipment is $3,000 to $6,000. Some people want a jump that is not from a plane. They can parachute from a tall building. They can jump from an antenna tower. They can jump from a bridge span. They can jump from a cliff high above Earth. The first letters of those four places spell BASE. This sport is called BASE jumping. Most BASE jumps are made from around 305 meters high. That height is 1,000 feet. That height is considerably less than the height at which skydivers open their parachutes. BASE jumpers have little time in the air. They have just 20 to 30 seconds. In that time they must open their chutes. They must land in a small safety zone. Maybe that sounds wild. Only expert skydivers may attempt these daredevil feats. They need a minimum of 200 jumps. Many places that could be used for BASE jumping have made it illegal. The reason is the danger. A picture shows a BASE jumper. The jumper leaps from a cliff. A list of skydiving facts follows. Expert skydivers can land on a tiny target. The target is about the size of a tennis ball. The highest and fastest skydive on record occurred in 2012. BASE jumper Felix Baumgartner made the jump. He jumped from a capsule. The capsule was attached to a high-altitude balloon. The balloon floated at about 39 kilometers. That height is 128,000 feet. The height is over 24 miles. During his free-fall descent, time passed. The descent lasted 4 minutes and 22 seconds.

8Felix reached speeds of over 1,340 kilometers per hour. That speed is 833 miles per hour. He was the first person to break the sound barrier in a special way. He did it without using an airplane. He did it without using any other vehicle. A picture shows Felix Baumgartner. He leaps from the capsule. The free fall that follows sets a record of over 24 miles. A 10-year-old girl stands on shore. She straps a harness over her life jacket. She adjusts her helmet. She gives the driver of the speedboat a signal. The signal is a thumbs-up. The motor roars. The boat scoots forward. The towrope tightens. The rope connects the girl to the boat. She takes a few steps. She begins to glide on the water. Is she water-skiing without skis? No. She is parasailing. The boat zooms into the bay. A colorful parachute drags behind her. It fills with air. The girl rises like a human kite. She rises 61 meters into the sky. That height is 200 feet. The boat moving forward provides thrust. Thrust is the power to take off. Lift comes from air rushing into the parachute. The flyer sits back as long as the towrope stays tight. She enjoys the ride. She enjoys the admiration of all those watching from the ground. A picture shows a parasailor soaring aloft. He launches from a beachside float. When towing a parasailor, the speedboat cruises at a set range. The range is between 24 and 48 kilometers per hour. That speed is 15 to 30 miles per hour. The boat needs to go fast enough to create lift. It must not go so fast that a wave will make it lose control. The boat needs to speed up in two cases. One case is a turn. The other case is a strong gust of wind from behind. A loose towrope will make the parasail drop. Parasailing is not recommended in a strong wind. The limit is a wind blowing stronger than 24 kilometers per hour. That speed is 15 miles per hour.

9A picture shows parasailing high above the water. The height a flyer reaches depends on two things. One is the speed of the boat. The other is the length of the towrope. On average, recreational parasailors reach a familiar height. It is the height of a 20-story building. The maximum height over oceans is 183 meters. That height is 600 feet. The maximum over large lakes and bays is 91 meters. That height is 300 feet. Some special-attraction parasail rides can go higher. They can go as high as 305 meters. That height is 1,000 feet. Parasailing is unlike hang gliding. It is unlike skydiving. Parasailing has no minimum age requirement. There is no special training for the parasailor. The boat driver needs to have experience. Parasailing can be done in many ways. You can strap on a harness. You can sit in a specially designed chair. You can ride alone. You can ride with a friend. Parasailing is usually done on oceans. It is done on lakes. It is done on bays. Flyers take off from the shore. They take off from a barge in the water. They take off from a boat equipped with a winch. Rides from parasailing companies typically last around 10 minutes. A ride can cost $50 or more. A basic parasail with accessories can cost $1,000 or more. A picture shows a Delta kite. A time line tells the history of parasailing. In 1918, the idea developed during World War I. A German U-boat captain wanted a better view. A U-boat is a submarine. He wanted to see above the fog when his boat surfaced. In 1948, Dr. Francis Rogallo developed a kite. He called it a Delta kite. That kite is the basis for the kind of parachute used in parasailing. In the 1960s, parascending was invented. It was an inexpensive way to teach parachuting. A parachuter and a towrope were attached to a car. The car was driven fast enough to raise the parachuter. The height was 305 meters. That height is 1,000 feet.

10Then the parachuter was set free. In the 1970s, parasailing became a recreational activity. Paragliding combines elements of three wind sports. The sports are parasailing, hang gliding, and parachuting. Paragliders wear a wing-shaped parachute. Some attach a frame harness with a motor. The motor looks like a big fan. They may take off from a tall cliff. They may take off from a snowy mountainside. Often, paragliders compete. They see who can cover the greatest distance. They see who can make the best time. The course is a specific path in the air. They may take photos of reference points along the way. The photos prove they passed a designated spot. Paragliders can stay in the air for hours at a time. They can travel great distances. These words help a reader follow the sports. Aerodynamics is the study of how objects move through the air. Air pressure is the force of the gases that make up air. Gravity joins that force. Altitude is height above a surface. A canopy is a large parachute. It slows a fall to the ground. A drogue is a small parachute. It pulls out the canopy. Free-falling means falling rapidly through the air. Lift is an upward aerodynamic force. Resistance is a force that slows motion. To stall is to stop moving forward. A thermal is a rising column of air. Thrust is a forward aerodynamic force. A winch is a crank with a handle. It is used to lift a person or an object. The same topics return if you look them up in a library. Useful search words from this book include paragliding and BASE jumping. They include altitude and parachute. Other names and ideas in the book are air, lift, and the automatic activation device. Otto Lilienthal belongs on that list. So do a parachute, BASE jumping, Felix Baumgartner, and parasailing. So do Daedalus, a reserve chute, free-fall, skydiving, a glider, training, gravity, an updraft, and hang gliding. So do wind, a harness, a wing, the Wright Brothers, Icarus, a helmet, and a jumpsuit.

23. Rosalind Franklin and the Spiral of Life

1A picture shows the double-helix shape of DNA. Have you ever seen this elegant form? It is a double helix. You may not know the shape when you see it. Even so, it is an important part of you. DNA has the shape of a double helix. DNA is like a blueprint kept inside the cells of every living thing. It is a plan for building you. DNA in the cells of a cat carries the exact instructions for making that pet. DNA in the cells of a dog does the same job. DNA from a single cell of an apple tree holds every detail needed to grow the whole tree. DNA is a short way to say deoxyribonucleic acid. The full name is long. DNA itself is so small that an ordinary microscope cannot show it clearly. Scientists did not know this molecule was real until 1869. After that, they understood that it mattered. They also understood that it was complex. They still could not see how its atoms were arranged. Finding the double helix took many people. It took a great deal of work. Most of all, it took the determination of one brilliant woman. Rosalind Elsie Franklin was born in England in 1920. Even as a small girl, she was remarkably smart. Some people felt a little afraid of her mind. At the age of six, she liked to do math problems for fun. She always got those problems right. In England during the 1920s, many people thought math was a waste of time for girls. They thought girls should marry and have babies instead. A photo shows Rosalind when she was young. Rosalind loved school. She was more than a person who only read books. She loved sports as well. Girls at her school played hockey, cricket, and tennis. Rosalind was good at every one of those games.

2She liked hiking with family and with friends. Mountain climbing was the activity she liked best. All through her life, nothing relaxed her more than going to other countries to climb mountains she had never tried. Travel to other lands was a joy for Rosalind Franklin. A photo shows Rosalind beside her younger sister and her brothers. She would not sit quietly and listen when she knew someone was wrong. She loved her father. She also thought his ideas were old-fashioned. She thought he was too conservative. When she believed he was wrong, she said so without waiting. Sometimes the two of them fought. Most of the time they debated. They talked through their disagreements and did not grow angry. Climbing mountains stayed her favorite thing to do. At the age of twelve, Rosalind decided she would become a scientist. Her father did not approve of that choice. Once college age arrived, her father had learned that arguing with her was useless. Rosalind always finished what she promised to do. In 1938, she went to Cambridge University in England. She won a scholarship. Her score on the chemistry exam was the highest. At Cambridge, women had to live with gender discrimination. For every nine men the university let in, only one woman could enroll. A double standard was in force. Women had to follow different rules from men about where they could go. They also had different rules about what they could do. Rosalind had to bear unequal treatment. Even so, she was thrilled to study chemistry. She studied physics and math as well. She became an expert in X-ray crystallography. That method uses X-rays to look at molecules. A photo shows Rosalind around that time in her life. World War II began in 1939. Most men in Britain joined the military.

3People who could not fight still wanted work that would help England beat Germany. Even Princess Elizabeth worked as a mechanic in the war years. By 1942, Rosalind was nearly done with school. She found a job in the coal industry. Coal was an important fuel. People used it to heat homes. Factories used it for power. Machines ran on it during the war. With X-ray crystallography, Rosalind learned new things about how coal is built. She studied how the atoms in a coal molecule fit together. Her work pushed science forward. It helped industry. It also helped the war effort. Other people who studied molecules admired her skill in the lab. She finished her Ph.D. in 1945. A Ph.D. is the highest degree a student can earn. A picture shows Princess Elizabeth, who would later be queen of England, working as a mechanic for the war. After the war, in 1947, Rosalind took work in France. She liked France more than England. People in France did not seem as old-fashioned as her father. Many other people in England seemed old-fashioned to her too. The people she worked with believed men and women should have equality. She got along well with them. They worked hard. They also made time for fun. Each day they ate lunch at a cafe they loved. Then they made coffee in the same lab tools they used for experiments. That habit may sound odd. To Rosalind, cooking was only another kind of chemistry. She loved France. She would have stayed there for the rest of her life. Her family missed her. They begged her to return to England. A photo shows her in the Tuscany region of Italy. The calm style of life and work in France suited her. Italy suited her in the same way. In 1951, Rosalind found a new job at King's College in London.

4That college was very different from her workplace in France. Gender discrimination was treated as a normal part of college life. The campus even had dining rooms that women could not enter. Rosalind could not settle into this place. The men played pranks on one another. Rosalind disliked the jokes. Lab work was a serious matter to her. She also disliked the nicknames the men gave their coworkers. They called her Rosy. She hated that name. Her boss was John Randall. He was a brilliant scientist. He was not a strong boss. He knew how to gather talented scientists. He did not know how to keep them happy. Even so, he supported Rosalind's work. In 1951, scientists were tired of war and of death. They found hope in studying molecules that come from living things. They wanted to learn where plants and animals keep their genes. Genes hold the biological information that guides growth. They also guide development. Some people thought genes sat inside DNA. DNA is found in every cell of every plant and every animal. John Randall asked Rosalind to study DNA. He wanted her to test this idea. Maurice Wilkins was a scientist in the same lab. Rosalind and Maurice had equal schooling. They had equal experience. They did not think they shared much else. They were supposed to be peers, which means equals. They did not get along. They could not work well as a pair. John Randall did not improve the problem. Some of his choices seemed meant to push them into a fight. Some people thought Maurice treated Rosalind unfairly because she was a woman. Other people remember a different fact. Maurice was the person who first told John Randall to ask Rosalind to study DNA. Whatever the two of them felt about each other, everyone on the project saw one thing.

5Rosalind took the finest X-ray pictures. King's College needed her talent if it was going to uncover the secrets of life. Rosalind stayed determined. She poured herself into the work. When a tool failed, she improved it herself. When a method failed, she fixed that too. Making an X-ray picture of DNA was not simple. One picture took one hundred hours. Sometimes the picture came out blurry. Sometimes the angle was poor. She persevered. Her pictures grew better and better. More people began to notice them. In 1952, she made Photograph 51. No one had ever taken such an amazing picture of DNA. Photograph 51 was a beautiful X-ray image. For each photo, Rosalind spent days on math so she could understand the result. She wrote everything down in notebooks. She did not worry about the time. She wanted to be sure of her conclusions before she told the world. She did not know every answer. Even so, she was learning more about the DNA molecule than anyone before her. Rosalind Franklin was not alone in the search for the shape of DNA. Maurice Wilkins was searching too. A famous American chemist named Linus Pauling was searching as well. James Watson and Francis Crick kept up the same hard pace at Cambridge, which was not far off. Rosalind was an experimental scientist. She ran tests under controlled conditions. She tried to prove scientific theories. She also tried to show that some theories were wrong. Watson and Crick were theoretical scientists. They read experiments done by other people. They talked through ideas. While Rosalind used X-rays on DNA in the lab, Jim and Francis built large models. The models were made of metal pieces that stood for atoms. The models looked like the building toys many children use before they start school.

6Rosalind doubted that models could teach them much. She and Jim argued often. He made things worse by teasing her. Behind her back, he called her Rosy. A photo shows Francis Crick and Jim Watson together. It was sad that Jim and Rosalind argued so much. Jim truly cared about her research. He knew her photos could help him. Being first to grasp DNA was the goal Jim and Francis had set. They wanted fame for the discovery. They wanted honor too. They believed they were in a race with Linus Pauling. They believed they were racing Rosalind Franklin. They believed they were racing every other scientist. No one else thought of the work as a race. A picture shows a modern kit for building models of molecules. Jim Watson had a hunch that the answer was nearly in Rosalind's hands. He went to a lecture she gave about her work. He visited her lab. They fought there, as they usually did. While Rosalind was not looking, Maurice Wilkins showed Jim Photograph 51. Jim was astonished. He hurried back to Cambridge to tell Francis Crick. Later, the two men collected more of her research. They also got a copy of her photograph. They never asked if they could see it. They knew she would say no. So they acted in a sneaky way. Her photo plus aid from one more chemist let them assemble a double-helix model. That model matches the shape we know today. A picture shows Jim Watson using Rosalind's research to find the shape of DNA. Jim and Francis wrote an article to tell the world about the double helix. Rosalind's own work came out in the same magazine. Jim and Francis did not acknowledge that their model rested on her research. They hinted that they had made the discovery with no help from her.

7They hinted that her work only proved ideas they already had. They grew famous for finding the structure of DNA. Rosalind never learned that they had taken her work. No one else knew either. Jim and Francis gave her no credit. As soon as she could, Rosalind found a different job. Birkbeck College had less money. Its buildings were falling apart. Its equipment was inadequate. She still liked it more than King's. She was the boss there. A team of talented scientists worked under her. They treated her with respect. The team studied viruses. A virus is a disease that attacks a living thing. It can sicken that living thing. Sometimes it destroys the living thing. Because her discoveries mattered, other scientists asked her to lecture across Europe. Travel and speaking always made her glad. She felt honored to visit America. Many scientists there wanted to hear her ideas. As always, she made time to hike. She made time to climb mountains that were new to her. She did this along with her science and her learning. In every place she visited, people found her extremely knowledgeable. They found her incredibly talented. She still grew angry when she heard something she did not like. She kept making new friends. She even forgave old disagreements. Friendship grew later between her and Jim Watson, and also with Francis Crick and Maurice Wilkins. On her second trip to America, Rosalind started to feel ill. Death came in 1958, when her age was only 37. She had cancer. No one can say for sure why the cancer came. Perhaps the cause was written in her DNA. We do know that too much contact with X-rays can cause cancer. Her careful, determined pictures of DNA may be why she died so young. Pictures of other molecules may have played a part too.

8A note beside the picture gives her age at death as 37. Linus Pauling was a scientist known around the world. He also tried to understand how DNA is built. While she was ill, Rosalind kept working. She spent time with the people she loved most. Most of those people were her family. She even took a vacation with Francis Crick and his wife. After her death, Jim and Francis began to say in public that her work had mattered. They admitted they could not have solved the complex structure of DNA without her photograph. She died without learning that her research had been used in secret. The Nobel Prize in Chemistry went to Jim, Francis, and Maurice four years after her death. The prize honored their work on DNA and on genetics. Rosalind might have won as well if she had still been alive. Only a living person can receive the Nobel Prize. A picture shows some of the people who won that prize in 1962. Most people forgot Rosalind. Jim and Francis became famous for the work. Jim Watson brought out a book in 1968 that told the story of the discovery. He wrote rude things about Rosalind. At the same time, the book showed how much he had needed her research. He made fun of her looks. He made fun of her clothes. He insisted that she never understood DNA. He said this because she had disagreed with him. In truth, she stayed quiet until she was fully sure a thing was true. Jim was the opposite kind of person. He loved to talk about his ideas. He talked even when he could not prove them. Before the book came out, Jim Watson showed it to other people. Maurice Wilkins told him not to write about Rosalind in that way. Francis Crick told him the same thing.

9Linus Pauling told him the same thing. They said the writing was mean. They said it was not true. Jim did not want to change the story. He added one paragraph at the end. In that paragraph, he said he no longer believed the terrible things. He admitted that he and Francis would not have understood DNA without Rosalind. He granted that being a woman forced her to labor twice as hard. People who had known her felt a single paragraph could not set the record right. They did not think it did her justice. Partly because of the awful words Jim Watson wrote, Rosalind became more famous. Today, more people honor Rosalind and the work she did. Cambridge gave her name in 1984 to a new Design, Technology, and Engineering Workshop. In 2000, King's College gave a new lab a name. It is called the Franklin-Wilkins Building. The name honors her and Maurice. Books have been written about her life. Movies have been made about her life too. Buildings in several institutions carry her name, and the University of Cambridge is among them. Proving that women could excel in science was never the aim she chose. She simply knew that she loved the work. Sometimes she resented people who stood in her way. Sometimes she argued when a compromise might have been wiser. Nothing ever stopped her from doing her job. Even while she was sick, she worked until her life ended. Her will to do the work well, even while facing discrimination, led to one of the greatest discoveries in the history of science. A picture shows Rosalind looking through a microscope. To acknowledge someone is to thank that person or to give credit for what the person did. Atoms are the tiniest units of chemical elements. Cancer is a disease that makes cells in the body grow without control.

10Cells are the smallest parts of a living thing that can work on their own. Chemistry is the science of how elements and compounds change when they are heated, cooled, or mixed. Complex describes something with many different parts, or something hard to finish or to understand. Conservative describes a person who likes old ways and resists change. Determination is the will to work hard until a goal is reached. DNA is the material in the nucleus of a cell that carries genetic information. A double helix is a spiral formed by two strands. A double standard is a set of rules used one way for some people and another way for others, which is unfair. Equality is the state in which every person has the same rights. Gender discrimination is unfair treatment of a person or a group because that person is male or female. Genes are the basic units of heredity that pass traits from one generation to the next. Inadequate means not enough for the job at hand. Industry is the work of making goods or services, selling them, and moving them. To lecture is to give a talk that teaches a topic. A molecule is the smallest bit of a substance that can exist alone, and it is made of one or more atoms. A person who persevered kept going on a task even when problems stood in the way. Pranks are practical jokes. A person who resented something felt anger because a person or a group had done wrong. A scholarship is school money awarded to a student. Theories are possible explanations in science that have not yet been shown to be true. Viruses are tiny living things that infect the body, and the word also names the diseases they cause.

24. Salmon Against the River Dams

1Salmon know how to travel. By human standards, they do not live a long time. A salmon lives only a few years. In that short life, a salmon makes a journey more epic than most people ever will. The life cycle of a salmon has clear steps. Parent salmon use their tails to dig the nest that starts the cycle. They spawn and leave behind thousands of eggs. Then both parents die. Next, newly hatched salmon, called alevins, stay in the nest. They live on food stored in their egg sacs. In time, alevins become fry. Food is outside the nest, so the little fry have to go out. They must always watch for predators. When fry are ready to change from fresh water to salt water, they swim downstream toward the sea. These silver young fish are called smolts. In the ocean, salmon grow and become mature. Some of them migrate great distances. When they are mature, the adults return to the place where they were born so they can spawn. Once they are near the home stream, salmon can follow smell to within yards of that birthplace. Birth for Pacific salmon happens in fresh water. Atlantic salmon begin the same way. They travel to the open ocean in order to grow up. Very few creatures can live in salt water and in fresh water. Fewer still swim 3,000 miles at sea and then climb as many as 1,000 miles against the current. The goal of that swim is the precise place of their hatching. On the way home, salmon must get past fishing nets. They must get past sea lions. They must get past dams and more. It is a long race. It is a hard race. It is a desperate race. A return to the birth stream may be managed by just one salmon in a thousand. Salmon do all of this only to spawn and then die. Yet in many rivers of the United States, salmon die before they can spawn.

2People have changed those rivers. The Columbia River, in the Pacific Northwest, holds some of the largest of those changes. Those changes are also some of the biggest challenges to salmon survival. A photo shows a sea lion catching a Chinook salmon as the fish migrates up the Columbia River. Many wild animals depend on salmon for food. The list runs from tiny insects to grizzly bears. A map of the Columbia River watershed shows the Columbia River. It shows British Columbia and Alberta in Canada. It shows Washington, the Clark Fork, and Seattle. It shows the Pacific Ocean and the Yakima River. It shows Montana, Portland, and the Willamette River. It shows the United States, Idaho, and Boise. It shows Oregon, the Snake River, and Wyoming. It shows Utah, California, and Nevada. The Columbia River is the biggest river that flows into the Pacific Ocean from North America. It drains a basin of 259,000 square miles. That basin is roughly the size of France. The basin includes land in seven Western states. The river begins in the Canadian Rockies of British Columbia. Feeder streams reach as far east as Yellowstone National Park. The last 300 miles of the river form the state line between Washington and Oregon. A photo from the late 1800s shows people pulling in nets of salmon from the Columbia River. Records show that the Columbia River Basin once held six kinds of Pacific salmon. Those species are Chinook, coho, sockeye, chum, pink, and steelhead. One of those six kinds is gone, and the other five remain. Pinks are extinct in that river. Scientists worry that other species may die off too. A legend says salmon were once so thick in some tributaries of the Columbia that a person could walk from one bank to the other on their backs. Washington settlers in the 1850s claimed a night's catch of 1,200 pounds for two men, a boat, and a net.

3Yearly returns of adult salmon to the Columbia then stood at about 10 to 16 million. Today, only a tiny fraction of that number comes back. What happened? For thousands of years, salmon fed the Native Americans of the Columbia River Basin. They also lived with the salmon. White settlers did things differently from the Indian tribes. Settler arrival was the start of changes to salmon habitat. They always changed it in ways that hurt the fish. Their first step was pulling huge log piles out of the rivers. Salmon had long relied on those logs for shade and for protection. Next, the settlers began logging the forests. Logging sent dirt into the rivers. Salmon need beds that are clear, cool, and rocky if they are to lay eggs. The dirt choked those spawning beds. It destroyed them. Later, the chainsaw and the bulldozer let people log the forests faster. That only made matters worse. A photo from 1917 shows loggers posing with a giant tree. An engraving from an illustration of the 1880s shows a salmon cannery. Astoria, Oregon, at the Columbia's mouth, was the site of that cannery. Farmers used pesticides widely to keep insects away. They used fertilizers to grow bigger crops. Those chemicals polluted the water. Irrigation for crops took water out of streams. Little water was left for fish. Fishing hurt Pacific salmon too. Overfishing was the real problem. A fish can vanish if people take it too often and in numbers that leave almost none behind. Canneries, once built, kept fish from spoiling on a longer journey. Nets could be filled to the limit, because the catch would keep. They did not have to fear that the fish would spoil before they were sold. Canned salmon was shipped everywhere. Yearly catches in the 1860s reached millions of pounds. Salmon made up a large share of that catch. The Chinook catch from the Columbia hit its high point in 1883.

4The peak was almost 43 million pounds. The totals started falling in the 1900s. Then the dams arrived. Today, dams are the greatest threat to the survival of salmon. Fishing in the ocean kills far fewer salmon. Fishing in the river kills far fewer too. The dams have destroyed thousands of fishing jobs. They have done it by killing millions of fish. One hundred years ago, the Columbia had no dams. Fourteen dams now stand on the river's main stem. Three of those dams are in Canada. Eleven are in the United States. Add the side rivers, and the system holds over 400 dams. Some of the dams make hydroelectric power. Some do not. Some dams are humble in size. Others are huge. The salmon's decline comes from these dams more than from any other cause. Here is why. During their life cycle, young salmon must travel downstream. Adults must return upstream. A dam blocks salmon whether they are heading downriver or upriver. Today salmon are at risk for many reasons. The biggest reasons fall into three groups. Each group begins with the letter H. Habitat means the decline of the home of the salmon. Harvest means overfishing. Hydroelectric power means the dams. People are the cause in every one of those cases. Adult fish can climb past a dam by leaping up a series of low steps. Enough water must spill over the steps or the fish will not come to the ladder. Too strong a flow will push the fish back down the river. It also cannot exhaust them so they cannot continue upriver. The dam, all by itself, is the problem everyone can see. Every dam between an upstream adult and its birthplace has to be passed. When engineers built the dams, they gave many of them fish ladders. The ladders were meant to let adults return and spawn where they were born. A fish ladder is a great invention. Even so, some salmon never find the ladder.

5Others fall back down after they have passed above the dam. The less obvious problem is worse. Each dam backs water up behind it. Where a river once ran, the dam creates a reservoir. Salmon know how to find their way in a river because water in creeks and rivers always moves downstream. Adults heading upstream lose their way in a reservoir. The water there is slack. No current is there to guide them. Young fish coming downstream have it far worse. On the old trip to the ocean, juveniles got a big boost from spring snowmelt that washed them downstream. Reservoirs have replaced the river behind the dams. A trip that once took a week now takes thirty days. As fish drift with no current, some fall ill and die. Others are caught in the turbines of the dams. These reservoirs also breed predators. Pikeminnow are native to the Columbia. They love the still, warmer water that dams create. Certain fish that are not native love that water too. Both groups have a taste for young salmon. Estimates say these fish eat 2.7 million young salmon a year in a single reservoir. Pikeminnow take most of them. The dams kill far more young salmon on the way to the ocean than adults on the way back from it. Young salmon simply exist in greater numbers, so more of them die. A fish that never reaches the ocean will never come back. Saving the salmon is what people hope to do. In just the five years from 1997 to 2001, governments spent 1.5 billion dollars on efforts to save them. Those governments were federal, state, and Native American. Programs are in place to control water pollution. Programs work against overfishing. Programs work against mobs of pikeminnow. Programs also promote healthy habitat for salmon. People even give barge rides to young salmon headed downstream. Here is how barging works. Millions of young fish are taken from the river at selected dams.

6Pipes blast those young fish into waiting barges. A barge is a boat with a flat bottom. The barges carry the fish downstream. Below the last dam, the government dumps the fish back into the river. A photo shows thousands of young steelhead salmon being released to start the trip to the ocean. Upwards of 90 percent of young Snake River salmon rode barges by 2005. The Snake is the largest tributary of the Columbia. Since 2006, that share has dropped to less than 50 percent. Why did it drop? Death comes more often to barged salmon than to salmon that pass the dams on their own. Some dams now spill extra water so young fish can ride it downstream. When water spills over a dam, fish spill too. That means fewer fish are collected. Fewer fish are barged. Water spilled for salmon cannot also spin the turbines. Even so, sending fish over the dams, rather than around them, gives juveniles a higher rate of survival. Barging once seemed like a great idea. In the same way, Congress funds hatcheries. The cost is 80 million dollars a year. The hatcheries breed fish and raise them. Release into the rivers comes once the fish have grown enough. The goal is to replace miles of spawning ground that dams blocked or flooded. Each year, hatcheries raise the count of salmon that are born. There are no predators in a hatchery. Food is easy to find. Because of that, more young salmon survive in a hatchery than would survive in the wild. A separate set of problems comes with hatcheries. Here is how a hatchery works. People capture salmon that return to spawn in hatcheries. They also capture salmon in rivers and streams. They collect the eggs and watch over them. Once the eggs hatch, people place the young fish in holding tanks so they can grow. Release into the river waits until the fish have grown enough.

7If they reach the ocean, they spend the next 1 to 3 years there. Then they return to the hatchery or to the spawning grounds. For one thing, hatchery fish are not considered truly wild. Their early lives are easier than the lives of salmon spawned in nature. They are less fit. They are less experienced. They are therefore less apt to survive than wild fish. They are also more prone to illness. Maybe worse, they compete with wild salmon. They even prey on wild salmon. Scientists still question whether hatchery programs help wild salmon. They question whether the programs threaten wild salmon instead. Critics say extra hatchery fish only add millions more bodies to a system that may kill them. To critics, the rescue steps now look as unnatural as the altered habitat itself. Maybe worse, critics say these steps do not solve the root problem. They argue that the real problem is the dams. A photo shows tourists on a paddle-wheel riverboat. The boat waits at Bonneville Lock and Dam while the lock fills, then heads up the Columbia. The dams were built for a reason. Dams do many great things. They let ships pass. They help control floods. Early Northwest settlers had to deal with those same problems over and over. Dams let more inland places ship goods downriver to seaports and farther still. Wheat is one of those products. Paper is another. Other products can travel upstream. One of the West's biggest irrigation projects gets its water from these dams. More than half a million acres receive that water. Those lands in central Washington are rich but dry. With river water, the farms there grow important crops. The crops include apples, corn, and potatoes. They include hops, beans, and sugar beets. Bonneville Dam was built in the 1930s. For Native Americans living upstream, Bonneville Dam was a disaster. Cheap power from the dam was welcome news to other people. The Columbia is also a great source of hydroelectric power.

8Fourteen hydroelectric dams stand on the Columbia. Many more stand on its tributaries. Together they generate more hydroelectric power than the dams on any other river in North America. Power bills in the Pacific Northwest are among the lowest in the nation. High-tech companies enjoy that cheap power too. The dams do not pollute. They do not add to global warming either. None of those benefits is free. Salmon have paid much of that price. Extinction is now close for them. A map shows four lower dams on the Snake River that people have proposed to remove. From west to east, the dams are Ice Harbor, Lower Monumental, Little Goose, and Lower Granite. The map shows Washington, the Columbia River, Montana, Oregon, Idaho, and the Snake River. Once salmon were listed as endangered, calls to act on the dams grew louder. Spilling water has helped salmon. People still talk about taking dams out. They may not mean all of the dams. They may not mean most of them. They do mean some. Four Snake River dams draw more debate than the rest. The four dams together leave one long reach of water with almost no flow. The stretch runs for about 140 miles. Upstream travel is hard for salmon in that still water. It makes the downstream trip tough too. Scientists say the best way to bring the salmon back is to let the lower Snake River flow as a natural river. Pictures show a dam site before removal and during removal. Dam removal has happened before, and the Pacific Northwest has seen it. Marmot Dam stood on the Sandy River in Oregon. It was removed in 2007. For the first time in ninety-five years, the Sandy could flow free from the glaciers of Mount Hood to its mouth at the Columbia River. Taking the four Snake dams down would be pretty simple. The reservoirs would be drained.

9The rivers would run again. Local people still have several fears about taking those four dams out. Members of Save Our Wild Salmon appear in a 2004 photo. They gathered at Bonneville Dam. They fear the loss of cheap power for the Pacific Northwest. They fear replacement energy would make the air dirtier. They fear it would worsen global warming. A weaker local economy is another fear if those dams come out. They fear it would hurt farmers. They fear thousands of lost jobs. Is that fear justified? People who want the dams removed do not think so. They say that careful planning can bring electricity bills down along with the dams. They say pollution can come down too. They say spending on saving the salmon can come down as well. They say new jobs can follow once the dams are gone. They explain that farmers can find other ways to get crops to market. They explain that farmers can find other ways to get water for irrigation. Each side answers the other, and the debate goes on. Scientists have known for decades that salmon in the Pacific Northwest are in trouble. The Endangered Species Act now lists every salmon type still left in the Columbia River Basin. The fish have been listed for years. Habitat protection is one of the strongest tools the Act gives a species. No species lasts if its home is gone. But the habitat of Columbia River salmon went through huge changes before the fish were ever listed. Some people argue about how to protect that habitat. Others insist that the real question should be how to restore it. An endangered species is a plant or animal with only a small population left. They may be lost forever unless quick action stops their decline. Congress created the Endangered Species Act in 1973 for American animals closest to extinction. Saving those species, the Act says, must follow the best science on hand. Having that law on your side can be powerful.

10Thanks to the Act, the bald eagle has been saved. Other species have been saved too. The salmon species still named for the Columbia River Basin are Chinook, coho, sockeye, chum, and steelhead. According to scientists, the best course is to take out some dams. If the best science calls for dams to fall, the Endangered Species Act says they should fall. Money and politics can shove science out of the way. Salmon and species like them get shoved aside in the same moment. Talk of pulling the dams down is, for some people, time wasted. They say most people do not care enough about the fish to tear the dams down. Up to now, events look that way. A few salmon still reach the river bend of their birth. They lay their eggs before they die. Then their whole amazing story can begin again. For now, the salmon is a legend that is still alive. Enough public care could keep them in that living state. They do not have to become the sort of legend that starts with once upon a time. Extinct means no longer in existence, completely wiped out. Hatcheries are places where animals are raised from eggs. Hydroelectric power is electricity made when moving water turns turbines that are connected to generators. Irrigation means bringing water to land so crops can grow. Juveniles are young animals that have not reached maturity. A reservoir is a large tank or lake used to collect and store water for people or for farms. To migrate is to shift habitat or region on a yearly schedule. To spawn is to make and lay eggs. A species is a group of living things that look alike and can reproduce. Native describes something natural to an area. Predators are animals that hunt and eat other animals in order to survive. A tributary is a river or stream that joins a bigger river.

25. The Amazon Forest and the Hunt for Gold

1A map marks the region of the Amazon rainforest. Millions of animal and plant species live among those tangled trees. Imagine walking through a beautiful forest of giant trees. Vines hang there. Large flowers show bright colors. Strange sounds from birds fill the air. Monkeys and other wild animals add to the noise. Big snakes of bright color crawl along the branches. Frogs crawl there too. Insects crawl there as well. The temperature is high. Raindrops sprinkle down through the leaves overhead. Years must pass before a rainforest heals after an illegal mine tears it up. One such mine sits near the border of Brazil. Suddenly you come upon a bare area. No plants grow there. No animals live there. An enormous, deep hole has been gouged into the ground. The grinding of machines replaces the wild forest sounds. The yells of people replace those sounds too. Many workers hurry in the hole and around it. They are mining for gold. This place is the Amazon rainforest of South America. It is home to many Indigenous societies. No other place on Earth holds as many kinds of plants and animals.

2Even so, outside influences are destroying this rainforest. The Amazon River covers about 4,000 miles on its way out of the Andes toward the Atlantic. That distance is 6,437 kilometers. In the river valley, temperatures stay warm all the time. Large amounts of rain fall there. Those conditions allow amazing biodiversity in the rainforest. The forest looks too huge for any harm to matter. Even so, deforestation wipes out huge pieces of the rainforest year after year. Deforestation happens for many reasons. Loggers cut trees to make paper. They also cut trees for wood products such as furniture. Ranchers clear land for cattle. Farmers use land to grow soybeans. They grow corn and other crops too. Photos from satellites record what logging did to one Amazon section. The photos run from 2000 to 2006. In one picture, a scientist cradles a young hyacinth macaw. The hyacinth macaw is endangered, and the Amazon rainforest is its native home. Tens of thousands of plant species live in the Amazon region.

3Billions of insects live there. Many fish live there. Amphibians, reptiles, birds, and mammals live there too. Destruction of the forest has wiped out thousands of plant species. It has wiped out thousands of animal species. Scientists might have studied those extinct plants and animals. They might have learned things that could help people. Hundreds of Indigenous communities also live in this rainforest. The Yanomami are one of those peoples. The Munduruku (moon-doo-roo-KOO) are another. Mining moves onto Indigenous lands. Logging, ranching, and farming do the same. Those operations bring violence. They bring disease. They destroy Indigenous cultures. They destroy Indigenous knowledge. Plants store large amounts of carbon. They use that carbon to make energy. Burning the rainforest sends large amounts of carbon dioxide into the air. Scientists think that in the future, cutting the Amazon will produce more carbon dioxide than the forest removes. Global climate change gets worse as this rainforest is cut. Rainforest plants take carbon dioxide out of the atmosphere for photosynthesis. Photosynthesis is the process by which plants make their own food.

4Less plant cover leaves more carbon dioxide hanging in the air. Heat stays in Earth's air when carbon dioxide builds up. That trapped heat adds to global warming. Gold prospecting is not the greatest cause of deforestation. Gold mining is not the greatest cause either. Even so, they cause damage that lasts a long time. They also affect more than the land right around the mines. The high price of gold fuels demand. Because of that demand, the damage will go on for years. Gold is very valuable. It is difficult to find in nature. The ground under the Amazon Valley holds a great deal of it. Jewelry is the main thing people make from gold. Many computers contain gold wires. Cell phones contain them too. Televisions contain them as well. Gold on the outside of a spacecraft keeps heat from the Sun from damaging equipment. Gold has many uses, and more uses are expected. Because of that, the price of this rare metal is often high. Demand for it around the world is often high too.

5A chart shows the price of gold per ounce from 2000 to 2022. To meet the high demand, many companies offer miners more money than those miners can earn in jobs that are kinder to the environment. Companies based in Canada make such offers. Companies based in the United Kingdom make them. Companies based in Australia make them too. Deforestation contributes to high levels of poverty. Because of that poverty, many families are forced to choose jobs such as mining for gold. One picture shows miners in Peru hunting gold inside a protected forest, against the law. The methods used to prospect for gold harm people. They harm the rainforest too. The same is true of the methods used to mine gold. Many of the gold mines are small. They are illegal. They sit in parts of the forest that governments protect. Many governments in South America do not have enough money to protect the land. They do not have enough officers either. Because of that, these mines wipe out more of the forest every year.

6To reach buried gold, prospectors cut down all the trees. Sometimes they burn the trees instead. Then they dig a deep pit with machines. Sometimes they blast a pit out with dynamite. Depth in the pit can reach 50 feet. That is 15 meters. Powerful water cannons are turned on the pit walls. The ore that holds the gold breaks free of the walls. The loosened mix of mud is sucked up into dump trucks. The trucks empty the mud into containers. A person can be lifted off the ground by the force of those cannons. A miner pours a mixture of mercury and gold into a tub so it can be processed. Handling mercury is extremely dangerous. A small picture shows what one miner found after processing tons of soil. He holds the gold from a single day. Workers mix the mud with mercury. Mercury is a toxic metal. Sometimes they use their bare hands. Sometimes they use their bare feet. Mercury pulls the gold out of the ore. Some smaller mines use copper pans to separate the gold-and-mercury mix from the mud.

7Some use sheets. Others use sluices. In a sluice, a filter captures the heavier mix of gold and mercury as the watery mud runs down. The mercury is then removed from the gold. Often it is burned away. Buyers can purchase that gold next. Mercury from this work poisons the forest. In the soil, mercury poisons forest plants. It poisons farm crops. In rivers, mercury enters the bodies of fish. Mercury builds up in a big fish each time it eats a smaller one. At last those larger fish become poisonous. When people drink contaminated water, they are poisoned by large amounts of mercury. The same thing happens when they eat contaminated plants. It happens when they eat contaminated fish. Other animals are poisoned in the same ways. Gold miners work very close to mercury. They often become poisoned when they breathe its fumes. They become poisoned when they touch it with bare hands or bare feet. A diagram shows three steps. Plants and tiny animals carrying mercury become food for small fish.

8As larger fish eat smaller fish, the mercury level in their bodies rises. As humans and other animals eat those larger fish, the mercury in their own bodies rises until it can become poisonous. Mercury poisoning can cause many health problems. Those problems include lung disease and kidney disease. They include shaking and blindness. They include trouble walking and trouble talking. A person who has been poisoned may be in great pain. That person may be so sick that moving is barely possible. It can take years to recover from mercury poisoning. Some people will never heal. A photo shows illegal miners clearing rainforest to make their camps. Many people are working to protect the rainforest and to preserve it. This work is difficult. Gold mining is profitable. Logging and ranching are profitable too. Other harmful activities are profitable as well. These activities are widespread through the Amazon region. More money is now going from some Amazon governments into law enforcement. They are closing illegal mines. Gold mining will still tempt people who are poor, jobless, and short of money.

9They may need to mine it. Better chances to earn a living may be the strongest way governments can stop the forest from being destroyed. Brazil's government, for example, runs programs that build education. The programs also improve job skills. They serve people who cannot otherwise afford school or training. Those people can then get jobs other than the jobs that harm the rainforest. Protection of the rainforest is not left only to governments. Conservation organizations help them. These groups work with government agencies. They work with businesses. They work with other organizations. Together they study natural places. They also conserve those places. Continued effort by governments is necessary if illegal mining is to stop. Continued effort by conservation organizations is necessary too. Both are needed to protect the rainforest. Harm to the Amazon does not stay local. The whole world feels it. Is gold jewelry, or any other gold object, worth harming the planet's health? Are they worth damaging the health of the people and animals who live there? A photo shows a farmer checking a young tree.

10The tree is part of a project in Brazil to plant forest again. Biodiversity means how many kinds of life share Earth, or one habitat, or one ecosystem. Mining is the removal of minerals by digging them out or blasting them free. Conservation is the protection of wild lands and of the living things found there. Ore is rock that carries minerals or metals people can use. A thing is contaminated when a harmful or unwanted substance makes it unsafe or useless. Prospecting is the search for valuables such as oil or deposits of minerals. A rainforest is a thick, usually tropical forest, wet with rain and full of many animals and plants. Deforestation is the clearing away of trees and other plants in an area. Illegal means against the law, or forbidden by a rule. A sluice is a built channel for water, often fitted with a valve or a gate. Mercury is a silver metal, liquid in a normal room, and highly poisonous. Toxic means poisonous and dangerous to life.

26. The Country of Egypt

1A picture shows villages in Egypt with buildings painted in bright colors. This book asks where the country sits. It then looks at people, land, and history. Later pages visit landmarks and animals. A short close and a word list finish the book. A map shows seas and neighbors around Egypt. The map names the Mediterranean Sea and the Red Sea. It names the Gulf of Suez. It names Israel, Jordan, Saudi Arabia, Libya, and Sudan. It names Alexandria, Cairo, Luxor, and Aswan. It names the Giza Pyramids and the Aswan High Dam. It places Egypt where Africa meets Asia, with Europe to the north. The Arab Republic of Egypt is in the northeastern corner of Africa. Africa is a continent. A small part of the country lies in Asia. That part is the Sinai Peninsula. Sinai is said SEYE-neye. The Sinai Peninsula shares its border with Israel. It also shares its border with Palestine. To the north of Egypt is the Mediterranean Sea. Mediterranean is said MEH-duh-tuh-RAY-nee-uhn. To the east of much of the country is the Red Sea. The country has 2,450 kilometers of coastline. That length is 1,522 miles. Sudan is to the south of Egypt. Libya is to the west. Cairo is Egypt's capital city. Cairo is said KEYE-roh. The city is located in the north.

2The Nile River flows through Cairo. The river is on its way to the Mediterranean Sea. Cairo is one of the largest cities in Africa. Its population is more than twenty-one million people. The city still stands on the site it occupied more than one thousand years ago. Muslim Arabs conquered Egypt in 640 ce. They named the city Al-Qahirah. Al-Qahirah is said el-KAH-hih-rah. The name means the victorious in Arabic. Before that time, other ancient civilizations built towns on the site. Some built towns near the site. In Cairo, the ancient world meets the modern one. Mosques reflect Egypt's long history. Other buildings do too. Many buildings date back to times of outside rule. Romans ruled Egypt in one of those times. Arabs ruled in another. Turks ruled in another. Near Cairo are the Pyramids of Giza. The pyramids are monuments of ancient Egypt's pharaohs. Pharaohs is said FEH-rohz. Modern hotels line the Nile. The Pyramids of Giza lie southwest of Cairo. More than 106 million people live in Egypt. Many of Egypt's cities and towns are located along the Nile. The river runs through the eastern part of the country. The rest of the country is mainly desert. Because of that desert, a large majority of Egyptians live near the Nile. People use ground water for cooking.

3They use it for washing. They use it for drinking. They use river water for irrigating crops. A picture shows Egyptian farmers at work. They use hoses to pump water into canals. The water irrigates their crops. Many farmers grow cotton. The cotton supplies Egypt's large textile industry. They grow it in the fertile land along the banks of the Nile. Farmers also grow wheat. They grow rice. They grow corn. They grow beans. They grow fruits. They grow vegetables. They dig canals. The canals channel the river water to their crops for irrigation. In Egypt's cities, many people work in service industries. Tourism is an important part of the country's economy. In addition, people work in factories. The factories produce textiles. The factories process food. Some form of Arabic is what most people in Egypt speak. Most Egyptians are descended from people who first lived in the Arabian Peninsula. That peninsula is in Asia. Other Egyptians are of African descent. Many of these groups have their own languages as well. About 90 percent of Egyptians are Muslim. Muslim means they follow the religion of Islam. Nearly 10 percent are Christian. Christianity was the dominant religion in Egypt before Islam arrived. Islam spread to the region around 640. Many Egyptians in cities wear western-style clothing. Many Egyptians also wear long-sleeved tunics.

4The tunics are called galabayas. Galabayas is said gal-ah-BAY-ahs. Men often wear white ones. They often wear blue ones. They often wear gray ones. Unmarried women usually wear bright colors. Muslim women typically wear a veil. The veil is called a hijab. Hijab is said hih-JAHB. It covers their heads. A picture shows Muslims at a mosque in Cairo. They attend a lecture there. A landform map names the places that shape Egypt. It shows the Mediterranean Sea and the Red Sea. It shows the Libyan Plateau and the Qattara Depression. It shows the Sinai Peninsula, the Sinai Desert, and Mount Catherine. It shows the Eastern Desert, the Western Desert, and the Sahara Desert. It shows the Nile River, the Gulf of Suez, Cairo, and Lake Nasser. Most of Egypt is covered with deserts. The large Western Desert covers two-thirds of the country. The Eastern Desert lies next to the Red Sea. The smaller Sinai Desert covers the Sinai Peninsula. The Sahara includes every one of these deserts. No two of these deserts share the same set of traits. The Western Desert looks like an endless sea of sand dunes. Many sharp rocks break that sea of sand. The Eastern Desert has wadis. Wadis is said WAH-deez. A wadi is a dry bed of a seasonal river.

5The bed fills with water only after a heavy rain. The Eastern Desert also includes rugged mountains. Those mountains are in the east. The Sinai Desert has tall mountains. It has deep canyons. Green oases appear in each of the three deserts. Pictures of this land show Lake Nasser. They show the Sinai Mountains. They show the Western Desert. The fertile Nile River Valley is a border. It lies between the Western and Eastern Deserts. The Nile is one of the longest rivers in the world. It flows from south to north. It ends at the Mediterranean Sea. Near its mouth, the river spreads out. The spread land is the triangle-shaped Nile Delta. This well-irrigated land holds rich soil. Some of the most fertile soil in Egypt is here. The Nile Delta stretches about 160 kilometers. That distance is 100 miles. The stretch runs between Cairo and the Mediterranean Sea. In the south of Egypt, the Nile forms a lake. Lake Nasser is the world's largest artificial lake. The lake was created in the 1960s. Workers built the Aswan Dam then. Some historical sites stood on land that the lake would flood. Those sites were moved. They were reconstructed on safer ground nearby. The temple of Hatshepsut stands across the Nile from Luxor. Hatshepsut is said haht-SHEP-soot. Luxor is a present-day city.

6The temple is near the site of Thebes. Thebes was an ancient Egyptian city. Few known pharaohs of Egypt were women, and Hatshepsut is one of them. Her reign ran from around 1473 to 1458 bce. Ancient images show her wearing a false beard. They show her in traditional male clothing. History in Egypt reaches back thousands of years. One of the world's first civilizations developed along the banks of the Nile. The seasonal flooding of the river provided rich land. The ancient Egyptians were among the first people in the world to grow crops. They were among the first to use a plow. For about three thousand years, pharaohs ruled ancient Egypt. Thirty different dynasties held that rule. Some ancient Egyptians believed the pharaohs were living gods. They built huge stone monuments for them. They built huge stone tombs for them. The ancient Egyptians were also polytheistic. Polytheistic means they believed in many gods. The pharaohs led the building of elaborate stone temples. The temples honored different Egyptian gods. Between about 2700 and 1000 bce, Egypt stood among the greatest civilizations in the world. Over the centuries, several outside forces have conquered Egypt. Some occupied the country. In 332 bce, Alexander the Great invaded Egypt. He was an emperor. He made Egypt part of the Greek Empire.

7Greek rule in Egypt lasted about three hundred years. Alexander founded the city of Alexandria. Alexandria still sits on the northern coast. It is the country's main seaport. Centuries of Roman control followed. Centuries of Persian control followed too. In 640 ce, a Muslim army conquered Egypt. The army came from the Arabian Peninsula. Muslim dynasties then ruled Egypt. The Fatimids were one of those dynasties. The Mamluks were another. The Ottomans were another. They ruled until the 1700s. In 1798, France attempted to invade Egypt. That attempt motivated the British to become involved in Egypt's government. The British did not want France to gain control of Egypt. The Ottoman Empire regained control of Egypt through the next century. British influence remained. French influence remained. During World War I, Britain made Egypt a British protectorate. The war lasted from 1914 to 1918. A protectorate is a country that another country protects and controls. Egypt gained independence from the British in 1922. Landmarks draw visitors every year. Millions of people visit Egypt to see its ancient landmarks. One of the most famous landmarks is the Valley of the Kings. It is in southern Egypt. Some of Egypt's most famous pharaohs were buried here. They lie in more than sixty tombs. Countless treasures were buried with them. Long ago, robbers took many of these treasures from the tombs.

8The tomb of Tutankhamun still had its treasures when it was found. Tutankhamun is said toot-an-KAH-muhn. The tomb was discovered in 1922. Many of these amazing treasures are displayed in the Egyptian Museum. The museum is in Cairo. The temples at Abu Simbel are another popular tourist site. They stand along the banks of Lake Nasser. Two of the temples were built by Ramses II. He was pharaoh of Egypt from 1279 to 1213 bce. Four giant stone statues of Ramses II sit at the entrance to the Great Temple. The Great Temple was carved out of a cliff. Inside this temple, three hallways extend into the cliff. The hallways include more statues. They include painted scenes. The hallways lead to a shrine at the temple's center. The shrine is dedicated to some of the Egyptian gods. A picture shows the temple at Abu Simbel. Camels fit Egypt's desert conditions especially well. They can survive on very little water. People in Egypt's rural areas raise many animals. They raise sheep. They raise goats. They raise donkeys. They raise water buffalo. Some people also raise camels. They use camels for work. Harsh desert conditions are home to some wild animals. One desert animal is the fennec. The fennec is a small fox with large ears.

9The fennec spends most of its day underground. It stays in a burrow. It comes out at night to find food. Many snakes live in the deserts. Many lizards live there. Many scorpions live there. The ibex is another animal of these deserts. Ibex is said EYE-beks. It is a sturdy wild goat. It can survive in the deserts' difficult conditions. Many kinds of animals also live in the Nile and in its valley. Hundreds of different types of fish swim in the Nile's waters. Crocodiles can be found in the southern part of the river. A picture shows a ride on the Nile. People ride in a felucca. A felucca is a small sailing boat. The ride is a popular tourist activity in Egypt. Egypt is an amazing country filled with history. Its rugged deserts are home to animals and to people. The Nile River cuts a path through the desert. The river provides life-giving water. It provides fertile land for crops. Throughout history, various civilizations found a home in Egypt. They shaped its people. They shaped its culture. They produced knowledge that benefited the world. Egypt's many historical sites hold secrets. They hold treasures. Those secrets and treasures should be important to everyone. A fact box gives figures for Egypt. The population is 106,156,692.

10The land covers 995,450 square kilometers. That area is 622,272 square miles. The capital city is Cairo. The primary language is Arabic. The primary religions are Islam and Christianity. The currency is the Egyptian pound. The figures come from the World Population Review. The review is dated 2022. These words name ideas from the book. A delta is an area of land shaped like a triangle. Sediment forms it at the mouth of a river. Mosques are places of worship in the Islamic faith. Oases are fertile places in a desert. They have water. Dunes are hills of sand. Wind forms them. Pharaohs are titles given to kings in ancient Egypt. Dynasties are series of rulers from the same family in a country. Pyramids are large buildings with three or more sides. Each side is shaped like a triangle. An empire is a collection of nations or people. One person or one government rules them. Textile is fabric. It is woven or knitted. Irrigation is the practice of supplying water to land or crops. The water is meant to promote growth. Tourism is the business of providing services for travelers. The travelers are people who go somewhere for pleasure. Islam is the monotheistic faith of Muslims. It is based on the teachings of the prophet Muhammad.

27. The Great Race on Snow

1This book starts with a hard journey. It tells what inspired the race and how the race grew. It follows the route, the mushers, and the dogs. A close, a way to learn more, and a word list come last. A picture shows a musher and a sled-dog team in the wilderness. It is hard to imagine a trip like this one. The traveler would cross almost 2,000 kilometers of Alaskan wilderness. That distance is 1,242 miles. The trip comes in the dead of winter. It is even harder to imagine the way the traveler moves. A team of dogs pulls a sled, and the person rides behind it. Yet every year since 1973, dozens of people have taken part. The event is known as the Last Great Race on Earth. The race is better known as the Iditarod. It starts in Anchorage, Alaska. The start is the first Saturday in March. Few sporting events ever held are more grueling. The early Iditarod Trail belonged to the Indigenous Athabaskan peoples. It has been in use for hundreds of years. Americans did not map it until 1908. They did not mark it until that year either. That was the year gold was discovered. The discovery was in the place that became the town of Nome. In the past, the Iditarod was mainly a winter route. People used it for mail. They used it for supplies. Supplies were carried by a Native Alaskan practice. The practice used teams of sled dogs. Mushers led the teams. A musher is a sled driver. They crossed the snowy terrain. Over time, the Iditarod Trail grew. It became a large route system. The trails connected Nome to Anchorage. A picture shows a dog team in Nome. Technology improved. Modes of transportation improved. The Iditarod Trail was used less and less. Alaskan sled dogs were needed less because of that change.

2Several people living in Alaska began to consider a problem. They wanted ways to preserve the skill of dogsledding. Saving the Alaskan sled dogs was one of their goals. A side note corrects a common mistake. Some people give the serum run of 1925 credit for inspiring the Iditarod. The serum run happened after a terrible disease struck Nome, Alaska. The disease was called diphtheria. Only an injection of serum could treat the disease. A fierce storm blocked other ways to move the serum. It could not go from Anchorage to rural Nome. So dogsledding teams rushed the serum to the town. They saved many lives. The events were amazing. They were not the origin of the Iditarod. A picture shows Balto. Balto was the lead dog of one sled team. That team took serum to Nome. The practice of dogsledding grew from mixed skills. Some skills were Native. Some skills were Russian. For centuries it was the main mode of transport in rural Alaska. But by 1967, dogs were being replaced. Snowmobiles took their place. Cars took their place. Trucks took their place. Two Alaskans wanted to act. They were Dorothy Page and Joe Redington Sr. They wanted to help save this tradition. The tradition was 6,000 years old. Dorothy was an Alaska history buff. Joe was a dog breeder and a musher. They decided to hold a sled-dog race. The race would follow the Iditarod Trail. That route saw more and more use during the Alaskan gold rush. The trail got its name from Native Alaskans. They called the surrounding hunting area Haiditarod. The name means The Distant Place. The route of the first race was about 90 kilometers. That distance is 56 miles. The race took only two days to complete. A picture shows an Alaskan museum. The museum is named after Dorothy Page. Joe and Dorothy wanted to attract mushers.

3They raised $25,000 in prize money. The prize brought 58 mushers into the race. But the next year, the race was canceled. A mild winter led to a lack of snow. It also led to a lack of interest. Another race was held in 1969. Organizers could only raise enough money for a $1,000 prize. Only 12 mushers entered. A picture shows Joe Redington Sr. The lack of interest did not discourage him. He was determined to make the race longer. He was determined to make it more challenging. In 1973, he extended the route. The new length was around 1,800 kilometers. That distance is 1,100 miles. It now stretched from Anchorage to Nome. It was the longest sled-dog race in the world. Joe managed to increase the prize money. The prize was about $50,000. In 1973, 35 mushers entered. This was the first official Iditarod. It took the winner 20 days to complete the race. The last-place finisher reached Nome 12 days later. The 1925 serum trail is shorter than today's race. Mushers who carried serum covered only about half of today's northern Iditarod race trail. The current trail uses two routes. One is the northern route. One is the southern route. The routes split off in the middle of the race. The split lasts for about 500 kilometers. That distance is 300 miles. On even-numbered years, mushers take the northern route. On odd-numbered years, they take the southern route. The official opening of the race is a bustling ceremony. The ceremony is in Anchorage. The dogs make a ceremonial run. They run from Anchorage to Eagle River. Then the dogs are trucked to the official start. The official start is in Wasilla. This way, the dogs are not competing on city streets. The streets of Anchorage are dangerous. The dogs also do not have to cross Cook Inlet. That crossing is treacherous.

4A picture shows the busy start in Anchorage. A map shows the Iditarod Trail. A picture shows legendary musher Susan Butcher with her sled dogs. She set a record. She won four times in five years. The teams do not all start together. Teams take off at two-minute intervals. The gap keeps teams from running into each other. The differences in time are erased at a required stop. The stop lasts 24 hours. Officials hold the teams back for a few extra minutes there. The race takes over a week to complete. There are 26 checkpoints along the route. Mushers and dogs can rest at the checkpoints. Before the race begins, mushers send supplies ahead. They send dog food. They send other supplies. The supplies go to the checkpoints. Then the mushers will not have to carry them on the trail. The average distance between checkpoints is 74 kilometers. That distance is 46 miles. The gap is longer than the entire 1967 race. The greatest distance between checkpoints is 150 kilometers. That distance is 93 miles. Off they go. The dogs can keep a steady speed. The speed is about 19 kilometers per hour. That speed is 12 miles per hour. They can hold it for about 4 to 6 hours. Then they get tired. This time is usually long enough to reach the next checkpoint. The dogs also rest on the trail. So the mushers do not always stop at every checkpoint. The wilderness has no clear track of its own. The trail is marked so teams stay on course. Markers are orange stakes. Reflective tape marks the way too. Additional markers are set up in the mountains. They are set up along the coast. Volunteers set out about 12,000 stakes every year. Getting lost could cost a musher their life. It is important that the trail be well marked. The Iditarod Trail is filled with beautiful scenery.

5Some of the most beautiful scenery in the world is here. The trail crosses the Alaska Mountain Range. It crosses at a gap. The gap is known as Rainy Pass. During the Iditarod, the pass is more likely to be snowy. The trail also follows the Yukon River Valley. It travels along the coast of the Bering Sea. Then it reaches Nome. Along the way, it passes through thick forests. It passes over frozen tundra. The teams must cross frozen streams. They must cross frozen rivers. Thin ice can be deadly. Wild animals have attacked dogs. Moose have been known to attack and kill dogs. Racers can encounter fierce storms. The storms bring arctic winds. They bring driving snow. A picture shows Rainy Pass. It is the highest elevation on the race trail. The trail ends in the small city of Nome. During the winter, the sun rises for only a few hours a day. The race is a welcome celebration for the residents. Citizens of Nome line the streets. They watch the finishers come in. Usually 55 to 75 mushers start. Every year several drop out. Some leave because of sickness. Some leave because of injury. Some leave because of simple exhaustion. Today, winners of the Iditarod usually finish in 10 to 12 days. A record time was set in 2011. The time was 8 days, 18 hours, and 46 minutes. The racer was John Baker. He is Inupiat. He is Jewish. A full week can pass between the first finisher and the last. A side note tells of a red lantern. During the early days of dogsledding, a red lantern was hung outside a checkpoint. It helped the musher find shelter. The lantern stayed lit until the musher arrived. A red lantern now hangs over the Iditarod finish line. The lantern is lit at the start of the race.

6The last musher to cross the line puts it out. Mushers come from all walks of life. They include professional athletes. They include tradespeople. They include authors. They include doctors. They include teachers. They include lawyers. From 1974 on, people of all genders have competed in the Iditarod. The race has been won by two women. One of them is Susan Butcher. She has won four times. Many mushers are Alaskans. Others come from all around the world. They come from many of the 50 states. They come from Canada. They come from Russia. They come from Norway. They come from Japan. They come from New Zealand. They come from Great Britain. They come from Italy. A musher must be at least 18 years old to enter the Iditarod. The musher must meet a race rule from the previous two years. The musher must have competed in a previous Iditarod. Or the musher must have finished two qualifying races. Each of those races must be at least 805 kilometers. That distance is 500 miles. Sled-dog racing is a solo sport. All mushers compete in the Iditarod alone. They have no crews waiting at the checkpoints. No crew is there to help them feed their dogs. No crew is there to help them care for their dogs. Most mushers feed their dogs first. They make straw beds for the dogs. Only then do they feed themselves. A side note tells of another race on the same trail. From 1986 on, the Iditabike Race has been held alongside the Iditarod Trail. In this race, mountain bikers ride 322 kilometers. That distance is 200 miles. A hearty group rides over the snow and ice. They brave temperatures below zero. They brave strong winds. The costs of competing in the Iditarod have risen over the years. Feeding the dogs costs money. Caring for the dogs costs money.

7Sled equipment costs money. The rising cost has led to difficulties. Indigenous Alaskan mushers in smaller rural communities find it hard to enter. In 2018, fewer than 10 of the 67 mushers were Indigenous Alaskan. Still, some mushers try to keep the activity alive. Ketil Reitan is one of them. Peter Kaiser is another. A picture shows musher Ketil Reitan and his sled-dog team. What would cause someone to face such danger? Temperatures can fall as low as minus 51 degrees Celsius. That reading is minus 60 degrees Fahrenheit. Strong winds blow. Snow blows. Wild animals wait. Ice is treacherous. Sleep is very short. The winner's prize may be an incentive. The prize is $50,000 or more. Most racers do it for the challenge. They do it for the love of the sport. They do it for the love of their dogs. Simply finishing the race thrills most participants. The Iditarod is really about the dogs. The dogs who run the race are called huskies. They have been specially bred to pull sleds. In a similar way, collies have been bred to herd sheep. The huskies are sturdy. They are tough. They can pull a sled that carries supplies and the musher. The total weight is 136 to 182 kilograms. That weight is 300 to 400 pounds. This may seem like a lot. Huskies love to pull. Breeders also shaped huskies so they can withstand extreme cold. Their fur grows in two layers. The outer layer is made of thick hairs. It protects the dog from wind. It protects the dog from water. Sheep's wool is a lot like that inner layer. It serves to insulate the body from cold temperatures. It keeps body heat from escaping. Some people even use husky hair to make warm hats. They use it to make gloves. A picture shows that huskies cannot hide their enthusiasm.

8The dogs do not lose heat the way humans do. Humans sweat through their skin. Huskies pant. They sweat through their feet. These adaptations let huskies run in deep cold. They run comfortably at temperatures below minus 51 degrees Celsius. That reading is minus 60 degrees Fahrenheit. A side note tells about booties. Booties often cover the feet of sled dogs in the Iditarod. The booties keep the dogs' paws dry. They prevent cuts from the sharp ice. They prevent cuts from the rocks on the trail. During one Iditarod, a dog team may use as many as 1,000 booties. This can get pretty expensive. The booties cost about fifty cents each. Breeders also shaped huskies to work as a team. Teamwork matters. The whole team must work together to pull the sled. As many as 16 dogs can run on one Iditarod team. Usually, an experienced lead dog runs at the front of the team. The lead dog helps set the pace. Huskies are extremely friendly dogs. Like most dogs, they love to please their owners. A picture shows lead dogs. They are tough. They are enthusiastic. They are experienced. Another picture shows a vet. The vet checks a resting husky. Care of the huskies matters deeply. It matters before the race. It matters during the race. It matters after the race. Mushers treat that care as utmost. So do the officials who run the Iditarod. Veterinarians wait at each checkpoint. They examine the dogs as they come in.
