Dogs that suffer from frostbite are pulled from the race. Dogs that suffer from exhaustion are pulled. Dogs that suffer from injury are pulled. In fact, most mushers drop off one or more dogs. Those dogs become tired. Or they do not want to run anymore. There are three mandatory stops during the race. The stops let the dogs rest.

9The first stop is 24 hours long. The musher chooses the checkpoint for that stop. The other two stops are 8 hours long. They are made at specific checkpoints. A musher who mistreats the dogs is disqualified. The dogs eat a lot during the race. The food keeps their energy high. A single dog may take in up to 10,000 calories a day. The dogs' diets are rich in fat. They are rich in protein. The food comes from meat. It comes from pure fat. It comes from dry dog food. The dogs also need a lot of water. The mushers warm the water. Warm water keeps the dogs from losing heat. The dogs typically eat three meals a day. They eat during rests. They also get lots of snacks on the trail. A meal chart lists the food. Dry dog food at one meal is 0.4 kilograms. That amount is 12 ounces. Meat at one meal is 0.5 kilograms. That amount is 1 pound. Fat at one meal is 0.25 kilograms. That amount is 8 ounces. Water at one meal is 1 liter. That amount is 1 quart. Like any athletic team, the dogs train hard before the race. The training is rigid. Training for the pull starts when sled dogs are puppies. Practice runs come between September and February. The runs happen before the Iditarod. Teams make practice runs of up to 2,253 kilometers. That distance is 1,400 miles. They also compete in smaller races. Those races are 322 kilometers or more. That distance is 200 miles. Usually the winner is the team with the best training. The Iditarod keeps the practice of dogsledding alive. The Iditarod is also known as the Last Great Race. It is a grueling competition between sled-dog teams. They race across the Alaskan wilderness. The race lasts 10 to 12 days. The prize is more than $50,000.

10Competitors come from around the world. Journalists come to cover the race. Television networks come. Newspaper reporters come from across the globe. The money is not what attracts the mushers. The fame is not what attracts them. The mushers do not really race their teams against one another. They race against the hardships of the wilderness. They race against themselves. Any musher will tell you who the true heroes are. The true heroes are the huskies. The dogs run almost 2,000 kilometers. That distance is 1,243 miles. They cross some of the harshest land on Earth. They cross some of the most beautiful land on Earth. A reader who wants more can keep going without a web address. Ask a librarian for books on the Iditarod. Books on dogsledding will help too. So will books on huskies. So will books on life in Alaska. Many adventure stories have been written about this rugged state. Other fiction books have been written about it too. Some of the most amazing adventures are true. Other subjects sit close to this race. Susan Butcher is one. Huskies are another. Dog training is another. These words help a reader follow the race. Ceremonial means done only for show. It is not official. Insulate means to prevent loss of heat. Mandatory means required. It is something a person has to do. Checkpoints are places along a racecourse. Racers' times are recorded there. Mushers are people who drive a sled-dog team. Exhaustion is extreme tiredness. Serum is special medicine. It is given in a shot. Frostbite is damage to body parts. The damage comes from being in extreme cold. Tundra means huge, flat arctic plains. It is too cold there for trees to grow. Grueling means extremely difficult. An incentive is something that causes a person to take action. It is motivation. Indigenous means native to a particular place.

28. The Hunger We Do Not See

1States issue a card called an Electronic Benefit Transfer card. Families use the card when they buy food. People shorten the name to EBT. The card looks like a credit card. Paper food stamps once let shoppers get groceries with public help. The EBT card has taken over that job. Hunger in this book is easy to miss. One part calls it an invisible epidemic. One part asks why people cannot afford food. One part shows how food assistance works. One part looks at the politics of hunger. One part shows neighbors filling the gap. One part shows that kids can make a difference. One part asks how people can help. A glossary explains hard words at the end. School ends today. A long summer break is about to begin. Almost every student feels excited about the weeks off. A boy named Aaron cannot share that feeling. His stomach rumbles. Today the school lunch was his only meal. The cupboards where he lives hold no food. He is already sure of that. His mom works two jobs. Those jobs still leave the family without food money at the close of the month. A sigh leaves him. He thinks about the summer waiting ahead. The break will feel long to him. Empty shelves are what he expects at home. One lunch at school will not cover those free weeks. Millions of children in America go hungry on ordinary days. Aaron's story matches theirs. A reader of this book might be hungry too. Staff at the USDA counted hungry households for 2019.

2USDA means United States Department of Agriculture. Homes labeled food-insecure held 35.2 million Americans that year. In a food-insecure home, meals do not always cover every person who lives there. Children numbered 10.7 million inside that total. About one child in every seven in the country belonged to those homes. Hunger causes pain. Hunger causes stress too. Researchers at the CDC compared children who do not eat enough with children who do. CDC is short for Centers for Disease Control, and the agency also works on prevention. Children without enough food have more health problems. They miss more days of school. Learning gets harder when a student is hungry. A shortage of food can slow a child's body as it grows. The same shortage can slow how a child's mind develops. The harm can last well past childhood. Fields in this country produce lots of food. Public programs exist for people who cannot pay for meals. An epidemic of hunger remains anyway. The hard question is why. An all-you-can-eat buffet makes a nation look full of food. That picture of plenty leaves some people out. Families short on money face grim choices. A bill and a meal may compete for the same dollars. Millions of adults work a full week. Some of those workers still go hungry. Women meet this problem more often than men. Counts from the USDA look at adults who get food assistance. Women are above 60 percent of that adult group. Census takers make an official count of the people.

3Women are the caregivers in more than 80 percent of homes where one parent raises the children. Poverty is twice as common for those mothers as for single men raising children. A child often lives in poverty when the mother does. One picture shows a mother at the store. Government assistance pays for the fresh food in her cart. During the Great Depression, officials handed out surplus food. Hungry Americans ate what the government had left over. Help with food from the federal government started in 1933. The first aim was support for farmers. Buyers at the USDA purchased crops that farmers could not sell. People who needed food received those crops. Stamps entered the plan in 1939. The stamps were real paper. People spent them on that extra food. Rules widened by 1964. Shoppers could choose any type of food. Leftover farm crops were no longer the only choice. The year 2008 brought a new name for this help. Leaders retitled the old Food Stamp Program. The new title was the Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program. People say SNAP for short. The name pointed to a different goal. Nutrition was that goal. A chart shows who receives SNAP benefits. Elderly people, together with people who have disabilities, are 23 percent. Children are 44 percent. Other adults are 33 percent. Look at people who got SNAP in 2018. Nearly two-thirds were children, or were elderly, or had a disability. The chart credits a study group. That group is the Center on Budget, and its full title ends with Policy Priorities.

4SNAP reached a huge number of people in 2019. More than thirty-five million people in America received that help. A typical food stamp benefit for a month is small. It averages less than $5 a day. A second federal program serves pregnant women. Children younger than five can use it too. The foods are chosen because they are nutritious. Milk is one item. Baby formula is another item. Baby food is another item. Public schools also serve lunch through the National School Lunch Program. People call it the NSLP. More than thirty million children take part. Some of those lunches are free. Some cost less than the regular price. Family income does not decide who may receive this school lunch. Hungry Americans can fall back on a government safety net. The dollars planned for federal food programs change from one year to the next. Congress writes that budget. The members of Congress are the national lawmakers. Their meeting place is Washington, D.C. SNAP loses billions of dollars in some years because Congress cuts spending. School lunch programs gain money in other years because Congress raises spending. People argue about the size of these food budgets. They do not share one view of what the federal government should spend. They also split over who should be fed. They split over the test the government should use when it chooses who gets help. Household bills keep climbing for other reasons. Childcare is one rising cost.

5Housing is another. Education is another. Paying those bills is a struggle for many families now. Some observers want food assistance to reach more Americans. Wider help, they say, would ease that struggle. A five-year-old stands with a sign. The child is protesting a farm bill from the House. The year of the bill is 2013. Under that bill, SNAP would have lost $20.5 billion. Real cuts followed in November 2013. The people hit by those cuts numbered more than forty-seven million. Local food programs step in when government funding cannot cover the need. Food banks operate in every state. Growers give food to the banks. Restaurants give food too. Grocery stores give food too. Charities receive the food from the banks. A food pantry is one kind of charity. A soup kitchen is another kind. The public can eat through those charities. Fresh fruit spoils if a bank tries to store it. Fresh vegetables spoil too. Canned food keeps longer, so banks often hand that out. Boxed food keeps longer too. People with lower incomes still need food that is fresh. A community garden can sometimes supply it. Neighbors plant the garden. They care for what grows. They harvest the crop. Some of the food goes home with them. Some of it is shared around the community. Vegetables from many of these gardens are grown for a local food bank. Areas with high poverty are often food deserts. A food desert has no large grocery store to serve it. Inner cities hold some of these places.

6The stores there have closed. Rural places hold others. The population in those rural spots is small. A trip of ten miles for groceries costs more than many of these shoppers can pay. A trip of fifteen miles is too far as well. Basic food then comes from a small store nearby. Prices in that store are generally higher. Foods that keep people healthy are often missing. Quality meat may not be on the shelf. Fresh produce may not be there either. Life in a food desert is especially hard for some neighborhoods. The neighborhoods are communities of underrepresented people. A chain supermarket is out of reach for those communities. Tomatoes go into the soil in a school garden. A mother works beside her children. The school is a middle school. Colorado is the state. Their plan is to give the produce away. A food bank nearby will receive it. Feeding America is the largest group helping families in the country with this work. Food banks in its network number more than two hundred. Food pantries in the network number sixty thousand. About twelve million children eat because of that network each year. Charity is only a thin slice of food help. The slice is under 6 percent of food assistance across the United States. Prices keep rising. Paychecks stay flat. Cuts hit federal food programs. The charities then discover a limit. They cannot cover every hungry person. Food runs out. Families are turned away. Gardens that have extra produce can meet food charities through a nonprofit.

7The nonprofit is called AmpleHarvest. More than 7,500 charities are part of that link. Children all over the country take part in the fight against hunger. Students in New Hampshire planned a hunger banquet. Parents were the guests. A seat cost canned food. The cans went to the food pantry. A class of sixth graders in Massachusetts filmed a piece about hunger. The film won an award. Neighbors in their town were the audience they hoped to teach. Students in other places have earned money for food banks in several ways. Some ran a bake sale. Some sold art. Some washed cars. Some held a walk-a-thon. Some held a read-a-thon. Sam Adamo brought his cello to a farmer's market near his home. Shoppers heard him play. He raised $700. His local food bank received the money. Rhode Island is the state where that bank operates. Carson Pazdan made a cookbook. The readers were kids. The writers were kids too. Sales passed $20,000. One food bank in northern Illinois received every dollar. A girl named Macy Stewart handed bags to students at her school. She asked each classmate to fill a bag with food. The filled bags weighed 844 pounds, or 383 kg. She gathered them for a food bank named St. Mary's. Surprise, Arizona, is the city of that bank. William Winslow was seven. He lived in one area of North Carolina. He noticed a problem during spring break. Kids around him would have no meals while class was out.

8He asked a nearby grocery store for a favor. He wanted to stand outside the doors. He wanted to speak with shoppers. He told them about a program called BackPack Buddies. The program sends meals home for the weekend. Students in need are the ones who get those meals. BackPack Buddies received a large gift from William in 2016. He gathered 8,100 pounds of food, which is 3,674 kg. A single weekend accounted for 1,400 pounds, or 635 kg. Cash gifts that same weekend totaled $305. William is in fourth grade now. A fundraiser is still his project every year. Volunteers joined him in 2016. He organized 171 of them. They stood at ten grocery stores and took donations. Children in his area got eleven thousand meals from what people gave. He looked ahead to 2017 with a bigger map in mind. He hopes the work will spread through the whole state of North Carolina. William calls himself a regular kid. He also calls himself involved. In his view, a child who decides to help really can help. Wanting to act is the part that matters. One way to fight hunger is a higher minimum wage. Several states have lifted that wage. Workers there now earn $10 to $15 per hour. Hunger rates in those states fell after the raise. A map titled Map the Meal Gap shows how common food insecurity is. The lowest band on the map runs from 4 to 14 percent. The next band is 15 to 19 percent.

9The next is 20 to 24 percent. The next is 25 to 29 percent. The top band is 30 percent or more. A family garden can yield vegetables beyond what the family eats. Those extra vegetables can go to the food pantry in town. The family can also plant one more patch for the sole purpose of giving food away. A child can give part of an allowance. That child can invite other people to give as well. Students in Virginia invented a small weekly habit. Each time a kid bought lunch, the kid could add a dime. The food bank collected those dimes. A school can write its own plan. The plan can back a food bank. Volunteers cook meals at the Central Kitchen. The kitchen is in Washington, D.C. Ask what the local food bank needs. Ask what the charities near you need. Learn the season when that need is greatest. Pass the answer to friends. Pass it to your family. Pass it to neighbors. Send letters to Congress. Send letters to the leaders of your state. Send letters to the leaders of your town. Urge them to back programs that keep children fed. The aim is simple. No child should go hungry. The tools to protect a child like Aaron are already here. Hunger does not have to return for him. The missing piece is the will to use those tools. A phone call can start help for a hungry person. The same call can help someone you know. One line serves English speakers.

10Another line serves Spanish speakers. The National Hunger Hotline answers. Callers are linked to places that give emergency food. They are linked to government assistance programs. They are linked to various social services. These words appear in the book. Budgeted means people planned an amount or set it aside. The amount is usually money. It is kept for one purpose. Food assistance means help for people who need food. Federal programs supply that help. A census means officials count the people who live in a place. Food banks are centers that gather food. They pass that food to people who need it. A charity accepts gifts of money, goods, or services. It uses the gifts to help people in need. A food stamp is a coupon, or another paper, from the government. The paper helps a shopper buy food. Congress is the top lawmaking body in the government of the United States. The Senate sits inside it. The House of Representatives sits inside it too. Funding is money handed over for one stated purpose. A government can supply it. Some other organization can supply it. Nutrition means eating the foods that life, growth, and health require. The word also names the study of food and of healthy eating. An epidemic is a sudden wave of something harmful or unwelcome. It spreads quickly inside a community. Federal describes a central government. Power is divided. Separate states hold part of it. Regions may hold part of it too. Poverty means being poor.

29. The Path to the Presidency

1A 2020 photo shows Donald Trump, then president, putting his name on a paper. He is in the Oval Office. So you have thought it over. You want to lead the United States. Are you allowed to run? Which political party will you stand for? What kind of campaign will move people to vote for you? How, in the end, do you win on Election Day? Do you think you can do it? Let us follow the path, one step at a time, until a person is elected president. This book starts with that wish. It asks whether you are qualified. It explains the time to campaign. It explains political parties. It explains that there are two campaigns. It explains how a campaign is paid for. It explains Election Day and inauguration. A glossary lists key words. Only a few rules stand between you and a run for president. The Constitution writes those rules down. You must be thirty-five years old. If you are younger, your campaign may have to wait. Your birthplace has to be the United States. You need fourteen years of living here, inside the United States. There is one more question if you are already president. Count the terms already served. Win once, and you serve a term of four years. You may then run for a second term. The total you may serve is two terms. That is eight years. If you are already in a second term, it may be time to think about a new career. Four presidents are shown with their years. Barack H. Obama served from 2009 to 2017. William J. Clinton served from 1993 to 2001. George W. Bush served from 2001 to 2009. George H.W. Bush served from 1989 to 1993. Among those four, only George H.W. Bush served a single term. Being a politician is not required.

2Even so, you will most likely want that background before you run. Voters usually prefer a presidential candidate who has already shown skill in government. They also look for skill at leading people. Donald Trump was not a politician before he won the presidency. Dwight D. Eisenhower was not one either. Trump had worked in business and in real estate. Eisenhower was a famous hero of war. During World War II, command of Europe's Allied forces was his. Other people who were not politicians tried for the presidency and did not win. Ross Perot was a billionaire from Texas. Ralph Nader was a consumer advocate. Dr. Benjamin Spock was a famous doctor for children. In a photo, Eisenhower urges on the Allied soldiers of World War II. In 1987, American President Ronald Reagan stands on the right. He signs an agreement with Mikhail Gorbachev, the president of Russia. Do you know the job you will have as president? A job description helps when you seek new work. In short, you will do four things. You will make sure the government is working well. You will make sure the laws are being followed. You will command the army, the navy, the marines, and the air force. You will meet leaders of other countries. You say you are ready for that challenge. You say you will lead the country well. Good. Your next step is to persuade voters. Voters have to see you as the strongest choice for this work. Presidents of the United States have mostly been white men, and Protestant men. John F. Kennedy was an exception. He was Catholic. Barack Obama was an exception too. He was elected in 2008. He was the first African American president. The vice presidents show a similar pattern. Most were white men who were Protestant.

3Charles Curtis served with Herbert Hoover. Curtis was Native American. Joe Biden served with Obama. No Catholic had been vice president before Biden. No woman has ever been president of the United States. The closest approach came in 2016. Hillary Clinton became the first woman a major party nominated for president. Photos show John F. Kennedy, Charles Curtis, Barack Obama, and Hillary Clinton. In 2004, George W. Bush greets children at one stop on his campaign. Now you need people to gather behind you and vote for you. Your campaign is everything you do so voters will choose you instead of the person running against you. You will travel the country. You will advertise, speak, and debate. You will have your photo taken. You will go to events and meet supporters. You will hold news conferences too. The schedule is grueling. Are you ready for it? Wait before the travel starts. First you must choose a political party to represent. Think of a political party as people gathered together. The people in it share similar ideas about how government should be run. Democrats form one of the two leading parties in this country. Republicans form the other. Democrats usually want a strong federal government. Federal programs are how they want that government to take part in daily life. Republicans usually want government to step back from people's daily lives. They especially want less involvement where money is concerned. There is no single party that is the right one. There is no single party that is the wrong one. Most Americans do not accept every idea one party holds. They pick the party that fits their values most closely. An elephant stands for the Republican Party. A donkey stands for the Democratic Party. The year 2000 was one time Ralph Nader sought the presidency. He ran again in 2004.

4He was a third-party candidate. The major parties are the ones called Republican and Democrat. Other parties exist too. A third-party candidate runs for a party that is not Republican and not Democrat. The Republican Party did not start as a major party. It began as a third party. Long ago, the top two were a different pair. They were the Whig Party and the Democratic Party. The last year the Whigs offered a presidential candidate was 1856. The Republican Party took the Whigs' place. It took a strong stand against slavery. The Republican Party became a center of political power. The Democratic Party did too. William Henry Harrison was the first president from the Whig Party. In 2020, presidential candidate Joe Biden is out campaigning. You have chosen a party. Next, hire a campaign manager. The manager will write a plan. The plan will map every move of your campaign. Advisors in large numbers will assist that manager. The manager matters a great deal. A strong campaign can be the thing that separates a loss from a win. A presidential campaign has two parts. Part one is about the nomination. You are trying to win it from your own party. You want to be that party's candidate for president. In the second part, you already have the nomination. You focus on beating the candidates of the other parties. At the start, you campaign so you can win the primaries. A primary is a local election. The vote chooses a single person. That person will represent one political party when the general election comes. Some states do not hold primaries. In those states, members of the party pick a candidate by voting in a meeting. That meeting is a caucus. After the primaries and the caucuses end, each party holds a convention.

5At the convention, delegates vote. Delegates are party members sent from each state. Their vote chooses the party's candidate. A delegate promises to support the person who won the primary in that delegate's state. A caucus win counts the same way. Each group of delegates speaks in turn. Oregon might be called a great state as it gives its votes to you, the candidate. After the votes are counted, you learn whether the nomination is yours. The crowd cheers at the Democratic National Convention of 2016. Once you are your party's nominee for president, the second part of the campaign begins. Your aim is to beat the presidential candidates of every other party. Then you become president of the United States. The country is your route again, just as it was ahead of the primaries. You speak. You shake hands. You debate the other parties' candidates. Mitt Romney, a presidential candidate, appears on the right in a 2012 scene. Volunteers help him. They call voters. When does the campaign truly start? A great deal of work comes before any primary is held. You may have started years before those primaries are held. Many candidates campaign for years. They still never get past the primaries. Someone else is chosen as the party's nominee. Candidates run ads on TV. The ads promote their own policies. The ads also attack the plans of their opponents. Is any allowance set aside yet? A campaign costs a lot of money. Lately, the price of a successful run for president has reached hundreds of millions of dollars. Pay for the job is four hundred thousand dollars each year. Ads take a huge share of the money. One commercial lasts thirty seconds. A popular show on TV can charge hundreds of thousands of dollars for those thirty seconds. You do not have to be a millionaire to stay in the race.

6Candidates usually do not pay the campaign bills themselves. They depend on gifts of money from supporters. That is a relief. You should start making many friends now. Presidential Election Day comes every four years. It falls on the first Tuesday that follows the first Monday in November. If your campaign has worked, millions of people will show up to vote. They will mark your name on the ballot. A 2016 photo shows a queue of voters outside the place where they will vote. Is this a year for a presidential election? One easy test helps you remember. Election years end in a number you can divide by four. Examples are 2016, 2020, and 2024. The pattern continues. In 1922, women voters turned out in large numbers. The Nineteenth Amendment had passed two years earlier. The amendment allowed women to vote. Who is allowed to vote? A citizen of the United States who is over eighteen may vote in a U.S. election. Across history, many limits have kept people from voting. Women could not vote until 1920. The year 1870 brought voting rights to African American men. Even then, poll taxes were often used to block racial minorities. Literacy tests were used that way too. Ethnic minorities were blocked as well. Those practices were banned in 1965 by the Voting Rights Act. Many states still have rules that make it hard for every citizen to take part. Voters mark ballots inside private booths. People vote by mail. Or they vote at a polling place. The place may be a church, a school, or another public building. A private booth is where each voter goes. The way the vote is cast is not the same everywhere. Some states use a voting machine. The voter moves a switch to show a choice.

7The switch shows the chosen candidate, which would be you. In other polling places, voters use a pen or a pencil. They mark a paper ballot. During the campaign, the media take frequent polls. TV networks do this. Newspapers do this. They want to see who is ahead. On Election Day, reporters ask people how they voted. They ask as people leave polling places all over the country. That method is called exit polling. The answers go into a computer. The computer offers an educated guess about the winner. The process sounds simple. There is more to it than a quick look shows. This system for choosing a president has a name. The name is the electoral college. The votes people cast in the booths are the popular vote. The election can still be lost even after the popular vote is yours. In 2016, Hillary Clinton, the Democratic candidate, won the popular vote. Donald Trump, the Republican candidate, won the electoral vote. Trump became president. Al Gore was ahead in the popular vote in the year 2000. George W. Bush became president because he won the electoral vote. A chart lists how many electors each state had in 2020. In 2020, Alabama had 9 electors. In 2020, Alaska had 3 electors. In 2020, Arizona had 11 electors. In 2020, Arkansas had 6 electors. In 2020, California had 55 electors. In 2020, Colorado had 9 electors. In 2020, Connecticut had 7 electors. In 2020, Delaware had 3 electors. In 2020, Washington, D.C. had 3 electors. In 2020, Florida had 29 electors. In 2020, Georgia had 16 electors. In 2020, Hawaii had 4 electors. In 2020, Idaho had 4 electors. In 2020, Illinois had 20 electors. In 2020, Indiana had 11 electors. In 2020, Iowa had 6 electors. In 2020, Kansas had 6 electors.

8In 2020, Kentucky had 8 electors. In 2020, Louisiana had 8 electors. In 2020, Maine had 4 electors. In 2020, Maryland had 10 electors. In 2020, Massachusetts had 11 electors. In 2020, Michigan had 16 electors. In 2020, Minnesota had 10 electors. In 2020, Mississippi had 6 electors. In 2020, Missouri had 10 electors. In 2020, Montana had 3 electors. In 2020, Nebraska had 5 electors. In 2020, Nevada had 6 electors. In 2020, New Hampshire had 4 electors. In 2020, New Jersey had 14 electors. In 2020, New Mexico had 5 electors. In 2020, New York had 29 electors. In 2020, North Carolina had 15 electors. In 2020, North Dakota had 3 electors. In 2020, Ohio had 18 electors. In 2020, Oklahoma had 7 electors. In 2020, Oregon had 7 electors. In 2020, Pennsylvania had 20 electors. In 2020, Rhode Island had 4 electors. In 2020, South Carolina had 9 electors. In 2020, South Dakota had 3 electors. In 2020, Tennessee had 11 electors. In 2020, Texas had 38 electors. In 2020, Utah had 6 electors. In 2020, Vermont had 3 electors. In 2020, Virginia had 13 electors. In 2020, Washington had 12 electors. In 2020, West Virginia had 5 electors. In 2020, Wisconsin had 10 electors. In 2020, Wyoming had 3 electors. After the popular vote comes a second election. The people in that election are called electors. Each state has its own number of electoral votes. That number equals the state's senators added to its representatives. A state where more people live has more electoral votes. The popular vote is totaled inside each state. Take a state's popular vote and every electoral vote from that state comes with it. Imagine the state's electoral votes number 24. If you win the popular vote there, all 24 of those electoral votes are yours.

9In 2008, Republican John McCain gives a concession speech. He has lost to Barack Obama. The system can feel confusing. Remember this. On Election Day, a voter is really choosing a candidate's electors. To win the presidency, you need the most electoral votes. The losing candidate speaks once it is clear the other side has the victory. That talk is a concession speech. You hope that speaker is not you. Campaign headquarters is the usual setting for this talk. The audience is the people who backed the campaign. Usually the speaker thanks everyone who helped. The speaker also wishes the winner well as president. A single vote can be enough to choose a winner. Virginia held an election in 1882 for a seat in its House of Representatives. Robert Mayo received 10,505 votes. George Garrison received 10,504 votes. About ten weeks pass before the new president starts the job. That time is needed to choose a staff. Presidents choose with care. They pick people they trust. They pick people they have worked with, or people they know by reputation. If you become president, you will need hundreds of staff members. Fifteen executive departments have leaders. Those leaders include the head of the Department of Energy. They include the head of the Department of Defense. They will serve as your expert advisors. Moving a family into the White House takes time as well. Andrew Jackson was president from 1829 to 1837. He often met his administrative staff in the kitchen. His opponents were angry about those secret meetings. They struck back with a mocking name. Jackson and the advisors became the kitchen cabinet. People have used that name ever since. In 2012, U.S. Secretary of State Hillary Clinton, on the left, meets China's President Hu Jintao. The meeting is in Beijing. The year 2009 shows Barack Obama taking the oath for president.

10His wife, Michelle, watches. Inauguration Day falls on January 20, which comes after Election Day. On that day a president officially begins a term of four years. The day is a huge celebration. It marks the end of a hard campaign. If you win, you will take part in parades. You will take part in receptions and in balls. Thousands of people attend. The swearing-in itself is a solemn event. You pledge to carry out the office of president in a faithful way. You promise to preserve the Constitution. You promise to protect it and to defend it. If you are lucky enough to be elected, you become the country's most powerful leader. You will soon see something. Next to the duties of the job, winning the election feels easy. These words have special meanings in this book. A campaign is a planned set of actions aimed at a goal. Delegates are people who were chosen or elected. They represent others and act for them. Campaign headquarters means the chief place a campaign uses for its work. Electors are the state representatives chosen to sit in the electoral college. A caucus is a meeting where leaders of a political party vote for a candidate. A nominee is the person a political party picks to run for office. A concession speech tells people the speaker accepts that the rival candidate has won. A political party is an organized group of people who share ideas about government. The Constitution is the written set of rules and principles that U.S. laws rest on. A politician takes part in government. Often that person holds an office voters filled. A convention is a meeting where party delegates announce their choice for president. Primaries are elections that choose a political party's candidate.

30. The Player in Jersey 42

1Contents. Introduction. The Early Years. Taking a Stand. You're Hired! Number 42 Takes the Field. Beyond Baseball. Glossary. Introduction. On April 15, 1947, the stands at Ebbets Field were full. Jackie Robinson of the Brooklyn Dodgers picked up his bat. Fans watched him walk to the plate. He was the only African American then playing in Major League Baseball, or MLB. Insults rang from the dugout of the other team. Branch Rickey, the Dodgers' general manager, had warned him. Rickey had said this would happen. The words hurt. Robinson had promised he would not answer them. He would play the game. His bat would speak for him. Many people noticed the great courage he showed that day. That courage would make him a legend of baseball. It would make him a champion for equal rights. A picture shows Robinson waiting for the pitch. The game is an exhibition at Ebbets Field. It is the day before his debut in MLB.

2The field that day was Ebbets Field in Brooklyn. A full stand watched a barrier start to crack. Courage, not a shouted reply, was the choice he kept. The Early Years. January 31, 1919, was the birth date of Jack Roosevelt Robinson. He was the youngest of five children. The family was poor, and they lived in Georgia. California became the family home while Jackie was still a boy. There, people came to know him as a gifted athlete. His gift was not in one sport only. It showed in several sports. A picture from about 1925 shows Jackie Robinson with his family. He is second from the left. His college was in Los Angeles. The school was part of the University of California. A short name for it is UCLA. Football was one team where he starred. He was a star in track, in basketball, and in baseball. No earlier student at the school had won special awards in four sports inside one year.

3Even with that success, he did not graduate from UCLA. The Robinsons had little money. In 1941 he left school so he could help support his family. Besides baseball, Robinson ran track at UCLA. He played basketball there. He played football there. He excelled at all of them. Taking a Stand. After college, Robinson played semiprofessional football. December 1941 was the month the United States went into World War II. The call to service reached Robinson, as it reached many young men then. He entered the U.S. Army. He served at Fort Hood, in Texas. In 1944, he boarded an army bus with a group of soldiers. The bus was segregated. The front seats were for white soldiers. Black soldiers sat in the back. Robinson knew he was every bit as much a soldier as the white men. An order told him to sit at the back of the bus.

4He would not do it. For that, he was arrested. A picture shows Robinson in his U.S. Army uniform. The army wanted to punish him. Many people defended him. He was a good soldier. All he had done was defend what was right. The final ruling said he was not guilty. You're Hired! Once his army service ended, Robinson coached at a college in Texas. He coached basketball there. In 1945, a letter came from the Kansas City Monarchs. They were a professional baseball team. They offered him a contract. Monthly pay would be four hundred dollars. The Negro Leagues included the Monarchs. Baseball kept the races apart in those years. One set of leagues was for white athletes. Another set was for black athletes. Robinson signed on with the Monarchs. He thought the job might be a path into the major leagues, which were all white. A picture from the mid-1940s shows his position.

5He plays shortstop. The team is the Kansas City Monarchs. A picture from about 1945 shows Wesley Branch Rickey. He wanted to see the color barrier broken. Those were the rules that kept white athletes and black athletes apart. Robinson showed everyone that he was a skilled player. Branch Rickey noticed him. The Brooklyn Dodgers were the club Rickey managed at the top. Rickey felt that baseball should not have separate leagues based on race. He put it this way. In his view, pro baseball ought to ignore color. He wanted the Dodgers to take a player out of the Negro Leagues. It would be a first try at bringing major league baseball together across race. Rickey knew the player had to be a great athlete. The player also had to show strong character when people abused him. Rickey and Robinson met in 1945. The job offer carried a single condition. Robinson could not answer any prejudice aimed at him because of his race.

6A Team of Two. A picture captures a handshake. Robinson has just signed. The contract year is 1948. Rickey is the other man. At first, Robinson was very surprised. Robinson wondered why he was being asked to leave prejudice unanswered. Rickey explained his view. Rickey said strong play would be the better answer. His play would prove his skill. Number 42 Takes the Field. October 23, 1945, was signing day. The team was the Dodgers' club in the minor leagues. The team held spring training in Florida. Florida gave him an early sample of the treatment ahead. He would be the first African American player in MLB. Hotels that housed the rest of the team would not take him. Some other minor league teams would not take the field if he was playing. Pitchers on the other side threw the ball at him. Even some of his own teammates did not want to play with him.

7Punishing Pitches. A pitch strikes his arm during the 1947 major league season. An umpire motions him to first base. None of this abuse stopped him. Strong play earned him a place with the Dodgers the following year. April 15, 1947, was his first day on that field. Number 42 was on his shirt. The crowd was larger than twenty-six thousand. By the end of the day, the Dodgers had a win. They beat the Boston Braves. They also beat prejudice against African Americans. Robinson and Pee Wee Reese smile after a game. Reese was the Dodgers' team captain. Both men hit home runs. Those hits helped take Game 1. The series was the World Series of 1952. Closeness grew between them as teammates. The friendship lasted as long as they lived. Both were stars in 1952. Another Great Steal. During the 1955 World Series, Yankees catcher Yogi Berra tries to tag Robinson.

8The years after that opening grew into a great career. Rookie of the Year was the honor he received for 1947. The league voted him Most Valuable Player in 1949. He was a fast runner. Stolen bases were a category he led in the league. Six league championships for the Dodgers came with his help. He helped them win a World Series. The Baseball Hall of Fame admitted him in 1962. He was the first African American to go in. His Number, His Day. April 15, 2014, shows two clubs in number 42. One club is the Los Angeles Angels. The other is the Oakland Athletics. The game goes eleven innings. That picture is at the top. At the right, President Barack Obama looks at Robinson's jersey. The visit is a tour through the Baseball Hall of Fame. Speed, awards, and October games all belonged to the same long career. Number 42 later returned to other clubs on the date of that first game.

9A president stood before the jersey in the Hall of Fame. Beyond Baseball. Robinson retired from baseball in 1957. He then joined a company that made coffee and also ran a fast-food chain. No black person before him had been a vice president of a national company in the United States. In 1964, he helped found the Freedom National Bank. Among banks owned by black people in the United States, it became one of the biggest. Six years later, he started a construction company. He also became a leader in civil rights. He kept working so that all people would have equality. He died in 1972. Heart problems were the cause, along with complications from diabetes. People of color in the United States now find more openings in sports than in any earlier time. We can all thank Number 42 for that. A picture from January 1962 shows Robinson at his desk.

10He understood that money's power could push society toward good change. In his autobiography, he wrote about green power. He said that power could strengthen black power. Glossary. Abuse means harmful treatment. The one harmed is a living thing. athlete (n.): someone trained for sports or games that take strength, speed, and skill. character (n.): the moral strength a person has. Civil rights are promises to citizens. One promise is freedom. Another promise is equality. integrate (v.): to bring different ideas together, or to bring different groups of people together. A legend is a person of wide fame. A special talent can be the cause. A special action can be the cause. Major League Baseball (n.): thirty professional baseball teams based in the United States and Canada. prejudice (n.): a bad opinion formed without a real understanding of a person, a group, or a subject. segregated (adj.): kept apart because of group differences, such as race.

31. Two Brothers and the First Balloons

1A map of early balloon routes is set near Paris, in France. Gonesse is marked on it. Paris is marked. Saint Denis is marked. Belleville is marked. The Tuileries Garden is marked. Versailles is marked. It also marks England, Germany, Italy, Spain, and France. Waters on the map are the Atlantic Ocean and the Mediterranean Sea. Dates on the map are August 27, 1783, September 19, 1783, November 21, 1783, and December 1, 1783. The scale runs from 0 to 1 in miles and in kilometers. Contents. Seeing a New Age Start. Two Brothers with Big Ideas. Constructing Smoke-Powered Balloons. A Balloon Rivalry. Soaring into the Blue. What Lighter-Than-Air Flight Left Behind. Glossary. Seeing a New Age Start. September 19, 1783, brought an afternoon gathering. Excitement filled a crowd of 130,000 people. They stood outside the Palace of Versailles. The palace sits just outside Paris, France. The people had come to watch a large hot-air balloon fly. Two brothers had built it. Joseph-Michel Montgolfier faced the test that counted. Jacques-Étienne Montgolfier faced it with him. France's king looked on from the palace courtyard. The queen looked on beside him. The balloon was made of silk and paper. It was beautiful. Gold designs sat on a blue background. The air felt tense. Three animals waited in a wicker basket. The basket hung under the balloon. A sheep rode along, and so did a rooster and a duck. A science test was the reason for choosing those animals.

2What a ride in a balloon would do to a person was still unknown. Ducks spend much of their time in the air. The brothers expected that bird to stay unharmed. The rooster was a bird too, but it did not fly very high. That is why it was included. The sheep lived on the ground, and it was a mammal. The brothers thought it was like a person in that way. They were anxious to learn one thing. Could these animals go up and come down without being hurt? The Montgolfiers filled the balloon with hot, smoky air. A fire in a pit produced the hot air. The balloon was released a little after 1:00 pm. The crowd watched in wonder. It rose about 460 meters, or 1,500 feet. Eight minutes later, it came back to Earth. It landed 3.2 kilometers, or 2 miles, away. The animals were just fine. People had dreamed of flight for a long time. That day, the dream had a real start. A route into the clouds was now open to people. The Montgolfiers deserved a large share of the credit. Two Brothers with Big Ideas. The Montgolfier brothers belonged to a large family. Home was a small town. The town lay in southern France. Their father, Pierre Montgolfier, made paper with great success. Those factories held an official job. Writing paper for King Louis XVI's court came from those factories.

3The 1770s were when Joseph took charge of the family business. Étienne took charge with him. The business did well. Hours were left free. Other interests filled those hours. In his early forties, Joseph grew deeply curious about flight. Scientists had studied craft lighter than air from the 1600s onward. Such a craft would fly because of buoyancy. No one had yet worked out how to build that kind of vehicle. The ancient Greek mathematician Archimedes, was the first to understand buoyancy. Buoyancy has to do with density. Density is the weight of an object for a given volume. That weight is compared with the fluid around the object. In this sense, a fluid can be a liquid or a gas. If an object is less dense than the fluid around it, an upward force acts on the object. A cork floats because it is less dense than water. A balloon full of hot air rises for a reason of the same kind. Heat gives the air inside the balloon a lower density. Air outside the balloon is denser. If an object is denser than the fluid it sits in, the object will sink. Joseph had a suspicion. A light gas inside a light shell might make flight possible. He read about a gas that earlier researchers had reported from their tests. This strange gas was already familiar to scientists. That knowledge was many years old. Henry Cavendish, an English chemist, showed in 1766 that the gas was an element.

4He named it inflammable air. He chose that name because the gas exploded very easily. The French chemist Antoine Lavoisier, gave it a new name. He called it hydrogen. Small spheres of paper, filled with hydrogen, were set loose by Joseph. The tests let him down. The spheres rose only a few meters. Then they came back down. The atoms of hydrogen were tiny enough to pass through the paper. They passed into the air outside. Elements and Atoms. The universe is built from chemical pieces called elements. Every element is made of atoms. Atoms are the smallest bits of a substance that still have the traits of that substance. Oxygen is an element. Hydrogen is an element too. One atom of oxygen and two atoms of hydrogen can join. Together they make one molecule of water. Joseph searched for a different plan for a machine that could leave the ground. Shirts were drying one day over a fire full of smoke, and he noticed them. The shirts puffed upward. Smoke climbing out of chimneys was a sight he knew, as most people did. He decided that smoke was the answer. A portrait shows Joseph-Michel Montgolfier. Early in 1783, the Montgolfiers started burning different materials. They wanted materials that made a lot of smoke. A mix of damp straw and chopped wool worked best. It also smelled terrible. The smoke did give buoyancy to any light container.

5The brothers decided that the smoke must hold something unknown. That something, they thought, had a lifting property. They named this wonderful stuff Montgolfier gas. Of course, no such stuff existed. The lift came from the hot air itself. When air is heated, the molecules in it move faster. The air then spreads out. It becomes less dense. When that lighter air is held inside a balloon, the balloon rises. A portrait shows Jacques-Étienne Montgolfier. Constructing Smoke-Powered Balloons. Joseph and Étienne did not yet understand the real science of hot-air balloons. They kept building new balloons and testing them. They tried different materials for the envelope. Those materials included silk, paper, and linen. As the work moved ahead, the balloons got larger. They rose higher and higher. The experiments began to draw attention. A village square in southern France was packed with people on June 4, 1783. Cloth lined with paper formed the large balloon used in the test. No one rode in it. The crowd watched in amazement. The balloon soared about 2,000 meters, or 6,560 feet, into the sky. After a flight of ten minutes, it landed about 2 kilometers away. That distance is 1.2 miles. Test flights like this one started a balloon craze in France. From Ascending Machines to Balloons. The French did not say balloon yet. The word came only after the Montgolfiers had made history. Earlier, people called them ascending machines.

6Later, people called them aerostats. The word balloon came from the French word ballon. Players use the word for a big ball in a game. A portrait shows Jacques Charles. A Balloon Rivalry. An official nod from the French Academy of Sciences was what the Montgolfier brothers requested. The academy held back its approval. It chose to wait. Someone else might invent a better balloon. Their main rival worked as a physicist. Jacques Charles was his name. He proposed a balloon that used hydrogen. Hydrogen was a path the Montgolfiers had already left behind. They favored their hot-air balloons. Charles saw great promise in the hydrogen plan. July 1783 was when Charles started building his balloon. The academy had approved the work. Two brothers helped him. Nicolas-Louis Robert was one brother. Anne-Jean Robert was the other. Both were professional engineers. The three built a small test balloon. It was made of silk. A rubber coat kept the hydrogen from leaking out. King Louis, meanwhile, asked the Montgolfier brothers to come to Paris. He wanted a dazzling balloon. He wanted every part of France to see his glory in it. The court treated the brothers as favorites. Government money then paid for their work. Each team wanted to be first. People riding in a balloon was the launch each team hoped to achieve first. The race was on. Helium, a Safer Gas. Hydrogen is the lightest element. It weighs only one-fourteenth as much as air.

7At first look, it seems perfect for balloons. Hydrogen is dangerous to handle, though. It catches fire. It explodes easily. For balloons, helium is a far safer gas. Helium is the second-lightest element. It does not catch fire. It does not explode. It is therefore fully safe for lighter-than-air flight. Helium stayed unknown until the decade of the 1880s. Balloon makers in the 1700s did not have it. Soaring into the Blue. A grassy spot in Paris drew a large crowd on August 27, 1783. The Eiffel Tower stands there now. The people had come to see the Charles balloon launch. Benjamin Franklin was among the watchers. He was a famous American. He was serving as the U.S. ambassador to France. The balloon was fairly small. It was about 4 meters, or 13 feet, across. Red stripes and white stripes took turns around it. The Scientific Method. A question pulled at the Montgolfier brothers. Jacques Charles faced that same question. Is a device lighter than air possible, if the aim is human flight? To answer it, they followed a procedure called the scientific method. That method has several steps. Ask a question. Do research on what is already known. Build a hypothesis. Test that hypothesis by watching or by running experiments. Study the data, then decide what it shows. Tell other people what you found. Late in the afternoon, the balloon was released with no pilot.

8It rose quickly. Northeast was the direction. The flight lasted under an hour. It landed about 15 kilometers, or 9 miles, away. The landing place was a small village. Terrified villagers thought this strange monster had come from the sky. They attacked it with pitchforks. Now it was the turn of the Montgolfiers to amaze Paris. Their test balloon left the ground at Versailles on September 19. Animal passengers rode in it. The animals landed safely. A trial with human riders could finally happen. Severe danger would ride with that flight. Two prisoners, the king believed, should be the ones to make the flight. Those prisoners had been sentenced to death. A teacher of science volunteered. A soldier volunteered as well. Their historic flight was on November 21. This time they left from the edge of Paris. The balloon stood 23 meters high. That height is 75 feet. Various golden designs covered it. The designs honored the king and the royal family. The teacher of science rode in the basket. The soldier rode there too. The balloon left the ground and climbed. About 8 kilometers from the start, it came down after twenty-five minutes. That distance is 5 miles. Outside the city, it came to rest between two windmills. People greeted the two men as heroes. Benjamin Franklin watched that historic flight. Another spectator asked what the balloon was good for. Franklin is reported to have answered with a question of his own.

9He asked what good a baby is on the day it is born. On December 1, only ten days after the Montgolfier success, two men made the first human climb in a hydrogen balloon. Jacques Charles was one rider. Nicolas-Louis Robert was the other. They took off from the Tuileries Garden in Paris. The name is pronounced TWEE-luh-reez. More than two hours passed in the air. Their landing was in a town some 36 kilometers from the start. That distance is 22 miles. Later years saw several more launches by the Montgolfiers. The French Academy of Sciences honored the brothers. King Louis made their father, Pierre, a nobleman. What Lighter-Than-Air Flight Left Behind. Love of ballooning soon spread across Europe. It was not long before the risks became clear. Two men died in June 1785. A balloon crossing of the English Channel was what they were attempting. Pioneers kept developing balloon designs anyway. Work on new designs went on well past the late-1700s rush for balloons. Rudders were added to lighter-than-air vehicles during the 1800s. They were given propellers run by motors. Huge airships of the 1930s took passengers both ways over the Atlantic Ocean. Airships enjoyed a golden age. It closed without warning. A German airship caught fire in 1937. Its name was the Hindenburg. The crash came during the landing. New Jersey was where it happened. Today, airships are used mostly for advertising. People often see them over open-air stadiums during big sports events.

10Gas balloons are used mostly to gather weather data. Hot-air balloons are used mostly for fun. Many places hold festivals each year. The sky fills with hundreds of hot-air balloons in bright colors. At those events, it can seem that the old balloon mania never stopped. Glossary. experiment (n.): a scientific test, or a scientific trial. airships (n.): aircraft with no wings that can be steered and flown under power. Gas inside the body makes them lighter than the air. immersed (v.): dipped into a fluid, or placed in some other substance that surrounds the object fully. atoms (n.): the smallest parts inside an element. inflammable (adj.): able to catch fire easily and burn fast. buoyancy (n.): the ability to float, or the tendency to float. intrigued (adj.): very curious, or strongly interested. A chemist is a scientist of chemical elements. A chemist also studies how chemicals act on one another. molecules (n.): the tiniest pieces of a substance that are able to exist alone. One or more atoms make them. An element is a substance. It cannot be split into a simpler substance. Only one kind of atom makes it up. physicist (n.): a scientist who studies what energy and matter are, and what properties they have. An envelope is the outer bag of an airship. A hot-air balloon is one craft that uses this bag. It holds gas or heated air.

32. Where Your Traits Come From

1Genes make each person one of a kind. Hair color raises a question. One person may have brown hair. Another person may have blond hair. Why is that? Eye color raises a question too. One person may have brown eyes. A brother may have blue eyes. A further question asks why certain people resemble a parent. What gives each of us our own characteristics? Heredity and genetics hold the answers. Heredity is the path traits take as they move into the next generation. Hair color is one of those traits. Eye color is another. Genetics is the science that studies that path. Ahead, the book examines genetics more carefully. Some of those questions get answers. The answers explain the person you are. This book begins with an introduction. It gives a short history of genetics. It compares traits you pick up with traits you inherit. It shows how to use a Punnett square. It tracks simple genetic traits. It looks at genetics in the years ahead. It offers a conclusion and a try-this activity. A glossary lists important words. When did people first learn about heredity? The idea is ancient. Even long ago, people saw that some traits could move from parents to their young. With that basic knowledge, they domesticated animals. They bred animals that had traits people wanted. They kept cows that produced plenty of milk. They chose chickens that laid many eggs. They used the same knowledge to make new food crops. They grew kinds of wheat that were larger and more nutritious. They did the same with rice. Ancient people understood basic heredity. The science of genetics still did not begin until about the 1850s and 1860s. Gregor Mendel was a man from Austria. He began studying the way pea plants hand down traits.

2A drawing shows a short pea plant crossed with a tall pea plant. The plant that results is tall. Mendel wanted to know the result of one cross. One parent would be a very short pea plant. The other parent would be a very tall pea plant. He expected a plant of medium height. That height would sit right between the very short parent and the very tall parent. What he found surprised him. A short plant crossed with a tall plant always produced a tall plant. Mendel then studied other pea traits. He looked at seed color. He looked at whether seeds were round or wrinkled. He crossed pea plants that had opposite traits. Short met tall. Smooth seeds met wrinkled seeds. The young plant always looked like one parent or the other. It did not look like a blend of both. Mendel needed a way to explain those results. He proposed units of heredity. We now call those units genes. Genes carry traits forward into the next generation. Genes usually come in pairs. The offspring gets one gene from one parent. It gets the other gene from the second parent. Genes carry every hereditary trait. Every gene belongs to one of two groups. It is dominant, or it is recessive. A dominant gene always overrides a recessive gene. In Mendel's tests, the tall gene in pea plants was dominant. The short gene was recessive. Two dominant genes for tallness sit in a purebred tall pea plant. Two recessive genes for shortness sit in a purebred short pea plant. The letters Ts stand for one dominant gene together with one recessive gene. The letters ss stand for two recessive genes. The letters TT stand for two dominant genes. An uppercase letter stands for a dominant gene.

3A lowercase letter stands for a recessive gene. In one test, Mendel matched a purebred tall pea plant and a purebred short pea plant. From the tall parent, the young plant got a single dominant gene for height. From the short parent, it got a single recessive gene for shortness. Put one dominant gene for tallness with one recessive gene for shortness. The young plant is always tall. The gene for tallness fully beats the gene for shortness. That result is the top row of the chart. These simple tests on pea plants gave birth to the field of genetics. The second row shows another cross. Each of those parent plants holds a tall gene plus a short gene. The young plants may show one of three mixes. The mixes are TT, Ts, or ss. Mendel's work on pea plants helped scientists later on. They began to see why some traits move to the next generation. They also saw why some traits do not. Pea plant traits are straightforward. Human traits can be very confusing. All sorts of things can change how a person looks. Genetics is one of those things. Heredity is one of them too. Behavior is another. The environment is another. Inherited traits are features present at birth. You cannot change them. Your height is one. The color of your hair is one. The color of your eyes is one. A common wrong idea is called Lamarckism. It was named for the French biologist LaMarck. His writing in 1801 claimed that a trait you gain could be inherited. He suggested a giraffe might stretch its neck while reaching for food. He thought its young would then be born with that long neck. That is not how it works.

4Only genetic traits can be passed along. A trait an organism gains during its life cannot. People can sometimes change how an inherited trait looks. They can also pick up new traits. Platform shoes can make someone look taller. Colored contact lenses can show a different eye color. The person will seem taller, or will seem to have eyes of a new color. The change is not permanent. It does not truly change the person. Take off the shoes and remove the lenses. The old height is still there. The old eye color is still there. Some traits that come from the environment can be permanent. Poor nutrition may leave someone short. That can happen even if the person has tall genes. Shoes with thick soles can create the look of height. The person may not be tall at all. Driving a car is an ability a person acquires. Nobody is born already knowing how to drive. Acquired traits come in many kinds. Swimming is one. Dancing is one. Telling a good joke is one. Cooking is one. Juggling is one. There are many more. Some traits come from heredity and the environment mixed together. Most humans are born able to learn speech and language. The language they learn depends on where they grow up. They might learn Dutch. They might learn Spanish, Swahili, or Korean. For the building of a body, DNA acts like a plan. Mendel saw, with pea plants, that traits move from parents through genes. Genes sit on rod-shaped structures called chromosomes. Chromosomes are inside your cells. Male, female, or intersex: chromosomes are what settle that. They also carry every other characteristic inherited from parents. Genes are built from even smaller molecules. Those molecules are called DNA.

5DNA molecules are in all of your cells. You can picture DNA as a code. You can also picture it as a plan for how a living thing is built. Everything required to build a living thing is stored in DNA molecules. Any living thing you name was put together by the plan its DNA holds. Let us return to genes. For now, we do not need to go any smaller than genes. For most simple characteristics, each parent carries two genes. Eye color is one of those characteristics. Dimples are another. Tongue rolling is another. Pea plants taught Mendel a rule. A gene falls into the dominant group or the recessive group. A dominant gene is stronger. Put it with a recessive gene, and the dominant gene always wins. Picture a mother whose eyes are brown. Picture a father whose eyes are blue. The child might receive the mother's gene for brown eyes, and only that one gene from her. The child might also receive the father's gene for blue eyes, and only that one gene from him. Genes for brown eyes are dominant. Genes for blue eyes are recessive. Here, the gene for brown eyes wins. The gene for blue eyes loses. The child's eyes would be brown. A straight thumb is another genetic trait. So is a bent thumb. The straight thumb is on the left in the picture. The bent thumb is on the right. Here the story gets a little confusing. Brown eyes in one person do not mean every child will have brown eyes. The mother with brown eyes might still carry one recessive gene for blue eyes. If that recessive gene pairs with the father's recessive gene for blue eyes, the child's eyes will be blue.

6Some people with brown eyes have two dominant genes for brown eyes. Those people will not have a child with blue eyes. If they do have a recessive gene for blue eyes to pass along, a blue-eyed child is possible. When parents hold both dominant genes and recessive genes, it is hard to say what the child will receive. A Punnett square makes this easier to see. A family chart shows how the genes can mix. One grandparent has brown eyes and holds no gene for blue. A second grandparent is the same. One grandparent has blue eyes and holds no gene for brown. A second grandparent is the same. One parent has brown eyes but also holds a gene for blue. The other parent is the same. One child has blue eyes and holds no gene for brown. Two children have brown eyes and also hold a gene for blue. One child has brown eyes and holds no gene for blue. The mix of genes can let people with brown eyes have children with blue eyes. The odds of one coin toss do not control what happens across many tosses. Heredity works the same way. A Punnett square can help you find the odds. It shows the chance that two parents will pass certain genes to their children. It does not promise that those odds will show up in real life. Think of a coin toss. There is a 1-in-2 chance of heads. There is a 1-in-2 chance of tails. Heads can still show up on five throws, one after another. An example may help. What are the odds of a child with brown eyes in this family? The mother has brown eyes. She has one dominant gene for brown eyes and one recessive gene for blue eyes.

7The father has blue eyes. He has two recessive genes for blue eyes. A Punnett square can answer this. Let the capital letters BR stand for brown eyes in the dominant form. Let the small letters bl stand for blue eyes in the recessive form. Start by placing the mother's gene pair across the square's top. Set one of her genes over the left-hand boxes. Set her other gene over the right-hand boxes. Write the father's pair down the left side of the square. The mother, who has brown eyes, is marked BR and bl. The father, who has blue eyes, is marked bl and bl. Next, bring the mother's genes down from the top. Write each gene in the boxes under it. One column gets BR. The other column gets bl. The father's side still shows bl and bl. Then bring the father's genes across from the left. Write each of them in the boxes to the right. The finished square has four pairs. Two pairs are BR with bl. Two pairs are bl with bl. Now count the pairs that include at least one dominant gene. Compare that count with the total number of pairs. Do the same for pairs made only of recessive genes. Brown eyes have a 2-in-4 chance. That chance is 50 percent. Blue eyes have a 2-in-4 chance. That chance is also 50 percent. Other simple genetic traits can go into a Punnett square as well. It can also help you track which parent passed on a certain trait. Imagine a child with curly hair. The dominant form of this hair trait is curls. A child with curly hair got that trait from a biological parent. At least one parent must show it.

8If the child does not have curly hair, both parents passed on recessive genes. That is true even when both parents have curly hair and each holds one dominant gene. Hair that hangs straight is the recessive form. Earlobes that attach to the head show a recessive trait. When both biological parents have earlobes that attach, every biological child will share that feature. Dimples are another dominant trait. Do you know anyone who has dimples? Do you know whether that person's parents have dimples? Do the grandparents have them? A picture shows an attached earlobe on the left. It shows an unattached earlobe on the right. Albinism is a genetic trait. The skin of a person, or of an animal, has no pigment. That is when albinism shows. The skin then looks very white. It happens when a person receives a recessive group of skin genes from each parent. The iris is the colored part of the eye. In albinism, the irises have no color either. The eyes look red because vessels carrying blood show through. Genetics is useful for more than fun puzzles about who gave which trait to whom. It also helps us guess which traits might reach children still to come. It is used in practical and important ways. A better grasp of the genetic makeup of humans helps scientists. They can diagnose diseases that are passed through genes. They can treat those diseases. New medicines can be made to fight them. In recent years, genetics has helped officers of the law fight crime. Crimes have been solved by naming the criminal. The proof can be DNA from a small sample of hair, of skin, or of fluid. A countrywide file of genetic information was opened by the FBI. Law officers use it to keep track of criminals and to find them.

9The same database has helped free people who were convicted but were not guilty. A scientist in a photo compares several samples of DNA. Some farmers love to grow enormous vegetables that win prizes. Genetics is also helping farmers grow more food. The goal is to meet the needs of a world population that is growing. Scientists design grains that look better. They design vegetables that way too. They design fruits that way too. The foods last longer. They carry extra nutrients. They grow in greater amounts. Many of these finds are very useful. Some people believe that changing genes may harm our world. How will organisms that were changed in their genes affect the environment? How will they affect us? Scientists must think about that. The rest of us have to consider it as well. So which things make you the person you are? You now know a large share of your looks began with your biological parents. They pass down genes. Genes are units of heredity. Those units brought traits along the line from grandparents into your parents. Then they carried traits from your parents to you. There is much more to learn about genetics. Many people spend their whole lives studying it. Knowing more about genetics will matter in the future. The study is also certain to feel fun. It will feel interesting too. Some genetic traits are tied to a person's biological gender. Hemophilia is a genetic disease. Say it like this: hee-mo-FEEL-ee-ya. It is linked to male chromosomes. People with hemophilia cannot make a blood-clotting agent called fibrin. They bleed very easily. They often need blood transfusions. A photo shows a blood cell in the background. It also shows fibrin, the clotting agent that looks like a web.

10Try this, and see a possible future. You can make an educated guess about how later generations will look. Use facts about people alive today as the base for that guess. Read about a mother and a father who do not share the same traits. Then fill in the Punnett squares. Decide which traits this family's next generation is most likely to carry forward. This book tells you the dominant traits. It also tells you the recessive ones. Use those facts. The mother has brown eyes. She has unattached earlobes. She has dimples. The father has blue eyes. He has attached earlobes. He has no dimples. The squares lead you to the results. These words have special meanings in this book. Acquire means to get something, or to come to possess it. Genetics is the area of science that studies the way traits are handed on. Chromosomes are rod-shaped parts inside your cells. They hold all of the genes. Heredity is the way traits move from older family members to younger ones. Hair color is an example. Eye color is an example too. DNA is a code of molecules found in every cell. It records how a living thing is built. Offspring are the young. They include children. Domesticate means raising an animal until living beside people, or close to them, is easier. Override means one thing outranks another. It means to outweigh that other thing. Dominant means ahead of the others in influence or in importance. Recessive means lesser in influence or in importance. Genes are the units of heredity. They move traits from parents to their young. Traits are the separate features, or the separate details, of a living thing.

33. Wild Places Nations Protect

1Search until a place feels peaceful to you. Let that place be special. In that place you feel linked to nature. Picture a beautiful spot in wild country. No buildings stand nearby. No light posts stand nearby. You step along a slick log. Below it, cool water trickles in a stream. Sunlight bounces off red walls of a canyon. An eagle glides overhead without a sound. Dry grasses rustle around a perfect hiding spot. Far off, gazelles leap. Places this wild are growing rare. People take more and more land for farms. They take land for houses. They take land to harvest resources. Many nations have still set wild areas aside as national parks. Inside a national park, special places in nature can still be found. This book starts with a special place. It explains why parks matter. It tells of troubled beginnings. It visits the Serengeti and the Masai Mara. It visits Santa Rosa. It visits Rock Creek. It visits a whale park on the Saguenay and the St. Lawrence. It looks at unique spots around the world. It ends with challenges now and later. A glossary lists key words. Land that is protected matters for many reasons. A national park can guard a rich diversity of plants. It can guard animals. It can guard natural landforms. Without that guard, human building and overuse might change them. Inside a protected area, native plants can live with very little human interference.

2Native animals can too. An endangered animal can survive there. Habitat destruction is not the threat it would be outside. Hunting is not that threat either. Yellowstone has many geysers. Parks are planned mostly to help plants, animals, and landforms. They can help people too. A national park is a beautiful space. People relax there. They enjoy nature by hiking, backpacking, and camping. They enjoy it in other ways as well. The Grand Canyon is among the most famous national parks. Parks serve important purposes. They are beautiful places to visit. Many of them still have troubled histories. A national park often stands on land that is a homeland for Indigenous people. Or it stands on land that once was. To make a park, governments often removed people. Sometimes the removal was by force. Families were split from land they had lived on for generations. Those forced moves often took away the work that kept people alive. The land had given them hunting, crops, and fishing for thousands of years. That land was denied to them. The Ancestral Puebloan people have a long history beside the Grand Canyon. That history is longer than 13,000 years. Eleven tribes of Indigenous people live in that area today. In the 1970s, governments began to work with Indigenous communities. The aim was to repair a history of inhuman treatment. Since then, many nations have tried to bring Indigenous knowledge back into care of the land.

3Indigenous people once lived on land that is now protected. They cared for it then. Governments around the world protect many kinds of sites. You might see these labels. One is national park. Another is national monument. Others are national forest, game preserve, and World Heritage Site. National reserve and preservation area are labels too. Signs mark real parks and forests. Glacier Bay National Park is in Alaska, in the United States. Gunung Mulu National Park is in Sarawak, Malaysia. Amboseli National Park is in Entonet, Kenya. Los Roques Archipelago is in Dos Mosquises, Venezuela. Inyo National Forest is in California, in the United States. Films and photos from the Serengeti are among the most famous pictures of Africa. Say the name like this: SARE-en-GET-ee. The park and the Masai Mara Reserve sit in Tanzania and Kenya. Dry grasses rustle over wide land. The African sun colors that land brown, red, and orange. Lions rest on the huge plains. Gazelles, zebras, and antelopes rest there too. They look peaceful. For thousands of years, this land has been home to the Masai. The British set up Serengeti National Park in 1951. They set it up in Tanzania. Making the park forced many Masai to leave their lands. They now hold only a small share of land that used to be theirs. The Masai have always named the Serengeti Siringitu.

4The name means a place where land runs on forever. A photo shows a Masai child. Wildlife is plentiful on the Serengeti plains. The Serengeti is a savanna, which means a dry grassland. It is one of the oldest savannas on Earth. It is also one of the largest. Many famous animals of Africa have no other home left but this one. Each year, wildebeests move across the plains in huge numbers. Zebras move with them. Hundreds of thousands make the trip. They follow the seasonal rains. Strong predators follow those herds.
